Internal modes and radiation damping for quadratic Klein-Gordon in 3 3 d
Tristan Léger, Fabio Pusateri

Abstract
. We consider Klein-Gordon equations with an external potential V V and a quadratic nonlinearity in 3 + 1 3+1 space dimensions. We assume that V V is regular and decaying and that the (massive) Schrödinger operator H = − Δ + V + m 2 H=-\Delta+V+m^{2} has a positive eigenvalue λ 2 < m 2 \lambda^{2}<m^{2} with associated eigenfunction ϕ \phi . This is a so-called internal mode and gives rise to time-periodic and spatially localized solutions of the linear flow. We address the classical question of whether such solutions persist under the full nonlinear flow, and describe the behavior of all solutions in a suitable neighborhood of zero. Provided a natural Fermi-Golden rule holds, our main result shows that a solution to the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation can be decomposed into a discrete component a ⁡ ( t ) ​ ϕ a(t)\phi where a ⁡ ( t ) a(t) decays over time, and a continuous component v v which has some weak dispersive properties. We obtain precise asymptotic information on these components such as the sharp rates of decay | a ( t ) | ≈ t − 1 / 2 |a(t)|\approx t^{-1/2} and ‖ v ⁡ ( t ) ‖ L x ∞ ≈ t − 1 {\|v(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\approx t^{-1} , (where the implicit constants are independent of the small size of the data) as well as the growth of a natural weighted norm of the profile of v v . In particular, our result extends the seminal work of Soffer-Weinstein [ 48 ] for the cubic Klein-Gordon, and shows that radiation damping also occurs in the quadratic case.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Description of the problem and difficulties

We consider the following initial value problem:
1
1

1

MSC classes:
35Q40, 35L70, 35P25, 37K55

Subjects:
Partial differential equations, Mathematical Physics

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+(-\Delta+V(x)+m^{2})u=u^{2},\\
u(0,x)=u_{0},\quad\partial_{t}u(0,x)=u_{1},\end{cases}

(1.1)

for an unknown
u:{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
,
with a sufficiently regular and decaying external potential
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
,
and sufficiently smooth and localized initial data
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
.
Precise assumptions on
V
and the data will be given further below.
In the nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation (
1.1
) one can rescale the mass of the scalar field and therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume that
m\equiv 1

from now on.

Under our assumption on the decay of
V
,
the spectrum of the Schrödinger operator
H:=-\Delta+V
consists of purely absolutely continuous
spectrum in
[0,\infty]
and a finite number of negative eigenvalues,
with corresponding smooth and fast decaying eigenfunctions
[
46
]
.
We then further assume that
the operator
L^{2}:=-\Delta+V+1
has a unique strictly positive eigenvalue
2
2

2

Generalizations
of our results to multiple eigenvalues may be possible under some suitable conditions, but we do not pursue this here.
See the work of Bambusi and Cuccagna
[
5
]
for the cubic problem.

with a corresponding normalized eigenfunction
\phi
:

\displaystyle(-\Delta+V+1)\phi=\lambda^{2}\phi,\qquad 1/2<\lambda<1,\qquad{\|\phi\|}_{L^{2}}=1.

(1.2)

In particular we have
\lambda\notin\sigma_{ac}(L)
and
2\lambda\in\sigma_{ac}(L).

The eigenvalue
\lambda
is usually called the “internal frequency of oscillation”,
and
\phi
is referred to as an “internal mode” of the dynamics.

(
1.2
) gives rise to a two parameter family of solutions to the linear equation

\partial_{t}^{2}u+(-\Delta+V+1)u=0
of the form:

\displaystyle\phi_{A,\theta}(t,x)=A\cos(\lambda t+\theta)\phi(x),\qquad A,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.3)

These solutions - referred to as “bound states”, or “internal modes” with a slight abuse -
are time-periodic, oscillating and spatially localized.
One is then interested in understanding what happens to these states under
the full nonlinear flow of (
1.1
), and how general small solutions of (
1.1
) behave.
In this paper we answer these questions. Our main result can be informally stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), for regular and decaying
V,

under generic assumptions on
H,

including the nonlinear Fermi-Golden rule (
1.26
) below.

Then, for all data that are sufficiently small in a weighted Sobolev space,
solutions of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) decay over time.

More precisely, if we decompose
u(t,x)=a(t)\phi(x)+v(t,x)
with
v\perp\phi
, we have

\displaystyle|a(t)|\approx t^{-1/2},\qquad{\|v(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}}\approx t^{-1},\quad t\gg 1.

(1.4)

Theorem
1.1
implies that, in a neighborhood of zero, the bound states (
1.3
) do not persist under the nonlinear flow of (
1.1
),
nor continue to quasi-periodic solutions or other non-decaying solutions.
However, the decay of solutions of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
),
is much slower compared to linear solutions of
(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+1+V)u=0

(for generic
V
with no bound states), or to nonlinear solutions of

(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta+1)u=u^{2}
,
which all decay at the rate of
t^{-3/2}
(in
L^{\infty}_{x}
).

To the authors’ knowledge, this result is the first construction and asymptotic description of dispersive solutions in
d=2,3
for low powers (below the Strauss exponent) and non trivial spectrum, that is
\sigma(-\Delta+V)\neq\sigma(-\Delta).
We remark that the linearization of the
\phi^{4}
model near its kink solution in
1d
fits that setting. This has prompted several groups to investigate the small data regime of such equations in
1d
, although the results currently available are either only valid on shorter than global time scales, or for local-in-space norms. We refer to Section
1.5
for more information on the related literature.

The question of the persistence for (
1.3
) is classical,
and was first addressed by Sigal
[
45
]
, who showed instability
of the bound states for very general classes of equations. Soffer and Weinstein
[
48
]
then obtained asymptotics
in the case of cubic Klein-Gordon equations, that is, (
1.1
) with a
u^{3}
nonlinearity.
We will discuss the literature around this problem and related ones later in Subsection
1.5
.
For the moment, we just mention that the case of a quadratic nonlinearity is substantially more difficult
than the cubic case, as it is to be expected in the context of the long-time analysis of dispersive and wave equations.
It is also worth noting that quadratic models appear naturally in the linearization around
(topological) soliton solutions
of many physical systems,
and, therefore, are often more relevant in applications.

In short, the main difficulties in the analysis of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) are:

1.

A complicated ODE analysis for the amplitude of the discrete component,
where separating the leading order damping dynamics
from remainders terms requires a more careful study of resonances and oscillations.

2.

Analyzing localized solutions
on the background of a large potential, and propagating weighted estimates
for low power nonlinearities. In fact even when
H:=-\Delta+V
does not have bound states, asymptotic stability was not previously known for quadratic Klein-Gordon with
V\neq 0.

3.

The slow decay of the discrete component creating a singularity in frequency space
in the continuous component of the solution, that is,
v.

This originates precisely from the feedback of the discrete mode of the solution into the PDE satisfied by the radiation.

Let us briefly elaborate on these points before explaining our approach to overcome them in the next subsection.

(1)

The presence of a bound state for
H
is an obvious difficulty and can potentially
generate a non-decaying state within the full nonlinear flow,
thus making the dynamics too complicated to control for long times.
Fortunately, it is well known, following the seminal work of Sigal
[
45
]
,
that the solutions (
1.2
) are unstable for the flow of (
1.1
)
under a suitable generic assumption, the so-called nonlinear ‘Fermi Golden rule’ (see (
1.26
)).

It turns out that the states (
1.3
) are in fact meta-stable,
in the sense that the component of
u
along
\phi
will decay over time,
but at an anomalously slow rate compared to the
t^{-3/2}
decay
of linear solutions of the Klein-Gordon equation (KGE);
in the case of (
1.1
), the rate that we prove is
t^{-1/2}
.
This meta-stability phenomenon was rigorously proven in
[
48
]
for the cubic KGE
(see also Remark
1.7
).

To capture this in the quadratic model (
1.1
), one can start (as in the cubic case) by decomposing

\displaystyle u=a(t)\phi+v,\qquad a(t):=(u(t),\phi),\qquad v\perp\phi\quad(v={\bf P}_{c}v).

(1.5)

See (
1.25
) for definitions.
This leads to analyze an ODE for the amplitude of the internal oscillations
(or discrete component)
a(t)
,
and a PDE for the ‘continuous component’
v
. Both equations are obviously quadratic;
see (
1.6
) below.
After standard normal form transformations, and upon plugging in the leading order (singular component)
of
v
in the ODE, this equation assumes the form
\dot{x}=cx^{2}+R
, with
\Re(c)<0
,
where
x:=|a|^{2}
and
R
includes several types of nonlinear expression in
a

and (nonlinear expression in)
v
(projected along
\phi
).
Since the equation is quadratic, many of these terms
are slowly decaying and cannot be treated perturbatively simply using linear estimates
(e.g., linear decay, Strichartz, or local decay) as in the cubic case.

(2)

In the basic case where
H=-\Delta+V
has no bound states (for example when
V\geqslant 0
)
a strong notion of asymptotic stability for small solutions of quadratic KGE
in
3
d is not known, to the best of our knowledge.
The existence of global solutions follows from local-wellposedness and conservation of energy;
however, proving time-decay and asymptotics is a non-trivial task.

One should note that the case
V=0
is classical (see Klainerman
[
39
]
and Shatah
[
44
]
),
but relies on methods (vectorfields or normal forms) which are not available as soon as
V\neq 0
.
Indeed, the presence of a (large) potential is a well-known difficulty in the analysis of the long-time
behavior of nonlinear PDEs as it simultaneously destroys the invariance of the equation
(e.g., the Lorentz invariance in the case of KGE) ruling out the direct use of vectorfields methods,
and perturbs the frequencies of interacting waves,
thereby preventing standard (nonlinear) Fourier methods from being effective.

The combination of a potential and a quadratic nonlinearity
naturally requires using a weighted norm which
(a) is strong enough to imply dispersive properties and (b) is sufficiently well-adapted to the problem
so that it can be controlled along the flow.
Our choice is to measure
\partial_{\xi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}{f})(t,\xi)
in
L^{2}
,
where
f
is the profile of
v
defined in (
1.16
), and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the distorted Fourier transform associated to the Schrödinger operator
H
(see Section
2
).
This choice has been used in the
3
d works
[
16
,
41
]
, as well as in the
1
d works
[
17
,
8
]
;
it essentially corresponds to standard weighted norms measuring
xf
in
L^{2}
(in physical space). Such a norm fairly easily takes care of (a) above.
Controlling it over time turns out to be a difficult task, as the internal mode causes various growth phenomena.

(3)

Indeed in our problem (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) there are additional difficulties
coming from the feedback of the internal mode
a(t)\phi

into the equation governing the behavior of the continuous (spectral projection) component
v(t,x).
The slow decay of
a
in turn causes
v
to have anomalously slow decay.
More precisely, its
L^{\infty}
norm decays at the rate
1/t
(without smallness)
instead of
\varepsilon/t^{3/2},
where
\varepsilon
denotes the size of the initial data,
as would be the case for linear Klein-Gordon equations
[
24
,
29
,
42
]

as well as for quadratic KG with
3
3

3

We
also expect the same rate of decay for (
1.1
) when
V
has no bound states; see Remark
1.6

V=0

[
44
]
.

Furthermore, it turns out that the slow decay of
a(t)
also
creates a singularity in (distorted) frequency space
(essentially corresponding to a lack of good localization in physical space).
In fact, we prove that the natural
L^{2}(\langle x\rangle^{2}dx)
weighted norm of the profile
f

is lower bounded by

\sqrt{t}
.
This means that parts of the solution are very badly behaved when measured in the
most natural norms that one would like to control.
By contrast, in the case where
H
does not have bound states,
this weighted norm is expected to stay small for all times.
See Remark
1.4
for more on this.

1.2.
Strategy

To address the issues described above we propose an approach based on some new ideas which we now describe.

1.2.1.
Discrete and Continuous decomposition, ODE dynamics, damping and remainders

As it is standard, we begin by decomposing
the solution as in (
1.5
). This leads to an ODE for
a
and a PDE for
v
:

\begin{cases}\ddot{a}+\lambda^{2}a=\big((a\phi+v)^{2},\phi\big)\\
\partial_{t}^{2}v+L^{2}v={\bf{P_{c}}}\big((a\phi+v)^{2}\big),\qquad L=\sqrt{-\Delta+V+1}.\end{cases}

(1.6)

The analysis of the ODE in (
1.6
) is conceptually similar to that of Soffer-Weinstein
[
48
]
.
However we follow a slightly different approach based solely on the method of averaging for ODEs.
This allows us to derive both upper and lower bounds for
a,
proving sharpness of the decay rate.
More importantly, as pointed out above, it is quite harder here to separate the leading order (damping) dynamics,
that are responsible for the decay of
|a(t)|
, from the remainder terms.
This is due to the quadratic nature of the nonlinearity.

To explain this, let us filter out the linear oscillations passing to the profile
A(t)
via

\displaystyle\begin{split}&A(t):=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}(\dot{a}+i\lambda a),\\
&(a(t)=A(t)e^{i\lambda t}+\overline{A}(t)e^{-i\lambda t},\quad\dot{A}(t)e^{i\lambda t}+\dot{\overline{A}}(t)e^{-i\lambda t}=0).\end{split}

(1.7)

The ODE in (
1.6
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{A}(t)&=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}\Big(\big(a(t)\phi+v(t)\big)^{2},\phi\Big),\\
&=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}a^{2}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{3}\,dx+\frac{1}{i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}a(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}v(t)\phi^{2}\,dx+\cdots,\end{split}

(1.8)

having retained only some of the main terms for the purpose of our explanation.
The terms that are quadratic in
a
on the right-hand side of (
1.8
)
can be made cubic using standard ODE normal forms.
Those can in turn be eliminated in favor of higher order remainder terms,
with the exception of the ‘integrable’ ones,
i.e
of the form
ic|A|^{2}A
for a constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
;

The leading order dynamics are coming from the second integral in (
1.8
).
In a perturbative regime one may expect the solution of the PDE in (
1.6
)
to be given at leading order by the solution
z
of
\partial_{t}^{2}z+L^{2}z=a^{2}{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}

(with the same initial data of
v
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}z(t)&=\frac{1}{L}\Im\big(e^{itL}(v_{t}(0)+iLv(0)\big)+\frac{1}{L}\Im\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}a^{2}(s)\,ds\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}\\
&=\frac{1}{L}\Im e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}A^{2}(s)\,ds\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}+\cdots,\end{split}

(1.9)

where in the last identity we used (
1.7
) and only wrote out explicitly the leading order
4
4

4

This
term corresponds to the first integral in the right-hand side of (
1.17
)
term.
Upon integration by parts in
s
, and disregarding less important contributions, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}A^{2}(s)\,ds\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}&=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0^{+}}i\frac{e^{2i\lambda t}e^{-\eta t}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}\,A^{2}(t)+\cdots\\
&=\pi e^{2i\lambda t}A^{2}(t)\delta(L-2\lambda){\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}+\cdots.\end{split}

(1.10)

Plugging (
1.10
) into (
1.9
), and then
z
in place of
v

in the last integral of (
1.8
) leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{A}(t)=-\frac{\pi}{(2\lambda)^{2}}|A(t)|^{2}A(t)\int\delta(L-2\lambda){\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}\,\phi^{2}\,dx+R,\end{split}

(1.11)

for some
R
.
One then imposes that the Fermi Golden rule

\displaystyle\Big({\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2},\delta(L-2\lambda){\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}\Big)>0

holds, so as to obtain damping for
|A|
from the ODE (
1.11
),
provided
R
is shown to be a remainder.

This turns out to be a non-trivial task. Indeed
R
includes a term of the form

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{2}(x)\,\frac{1}{L}\Im\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}{\bf P_{c}}\big(v(s,x)\big)^{2}ds\,dx,

(1.12)

which leads to an issue that is specific to the quadratic case: in order for this term to give a perturbative contribution to the dynamics of
A
in (
1.11
)
one needs to show that it decays faster than
1/t
(that is,
|A|^{2}
).
This amounts to showing

\displaystyle{\Big\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}{\bf P_{c}}\big(v(\tau)\big)^{2}d\tau\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{1}{s^{1+b}},

(1.13)

for some
b>0
, when
s\gg 1
.
(
1.13
) is akin to a decay estimate for a quadratic Klein-Gordon equation,
which is classical for
V=0
but not known for any
V\neq 0
.
A simple bootstrap argument is not sufficient, therefore we rely on
multilinear analysis techniques in distorted Fourier space.

1.2.2.
Harmonic Analysis tools

Our approach to the PDE part of the problem is based on the use of (nonlinear)
Fourier analysis techniques adapted to the context of the Schrödinger operator;
it follows the general philosophy
of many recent works that used Fourier analysis in the flat case and were inspired
by the space-time resonance method.
However this requires developing new tools adapted to the non flat background,
namely a generalization of the Fourier transform,
as well as a precise understanding of the interaction of three generalized eigenfunctions
of the perturbed Laplacian
H=-\Delta+V.
This is detailed in the remainder of this subsection.

Distorted Fourier Transform (dFT) and ‘continuous’ interactions

Given a regular and decaying
V
, under our assumption (
1.2
),
we have the canonical decomposition of
L^{2}
into absolutely continuous and pure point subspaces:

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=L^{2}_{ac}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\oplus L^{2}_{pp}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
where
L^{2}_{pp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathrm{span}(\phi)
.
Letting
\psi(x,\xi)
denote the generalized eigenfunctions solving

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(-\Delta+V)\psi(x,\xi)=|\xi|^{2}\psi(x,\xi),\qquad\xi,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\\
&\mbox{with}\quad\big|\psi(x,\xi)-e^{ix\cdot\xi}\big|\longrightarrow 0,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,\end{split}

(1.14)

the familiar formulas relating the Fourier transform and its inverse hold if one replaces (up to constants)
e^{ix\cdot\xi}
by
\psi(x,\xi)
:
for any function
g\in L^{2}_{ac}
, there exists a unitary operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
(the dFT) defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}f(\xi):=\widetilde{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\lim_{R\to+\infty}\int_{|x|\leqslant R}f(x)\overline{\psi(x,\xi)}dx,

with inverse

\displaystyle\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}f\big](x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\lim_{R\to+\infty}\int_{|\xi|\leqslant R}f(\xi)\psi(x,\xi)d\xi.

In particular, for any
g\in L^{2}
we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&g(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\big(\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}g}\big)+(g,\phi)\phi,\end{split}

(1.15)

where
{\bf P_{c}}
denotes the projection onto the continuous subspace
L^{2}_{ac}
.
Also,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
diagonalizes the Schrödinger operator (on the continuous spectrum),
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}{\bf{P_{c}}}H=|\xi|^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
,
and associated functional calculus ensues.

We then look at the ‘profile’ or ‘interaction variable’ given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f:=e^{-itL}w=e^{-itL}(\partial_{t}+iL)v,\qquad v=\Im\big(\frac{1}{L}e^{itL}f\big).\end{split}

(1.16)

Duhamel’s formula for
f
reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(t)-f(0)&=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}{\bf{P_{c}}}\Big(a(s)\phi+\frac{1}{2iL}(w(s)-\bar{w}(s))\Big)^{2}\,ds\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}A(s)^{2}\,ds\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}\big(L^{-1}e^{isL}f(s)\big)^{2}\,ds+\cdots,\end{split}

(1.17)

having kept only some of the main terms above for the sake of explanation.
In distorted frequency space
\widetilde{f}(t,\xi)=e^{-it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{w}(t,\xi),\langle\xi\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}
,
so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{f}(t,\xi)-\widetilde{f}(0,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}A(t)^{2}{\bf{P_{c}}}\widetilde{\phi^{2}}\,ds+F(t,\xi)+\cdots\end{split}

(1.18)

where

\displaystyle F(t,\xi)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{is(-\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle)}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\widetilde{f}(s,\sigma)\,\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma ds,

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{{(2\pi)}^{9/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\overline{\psi(x,\xi)}\psi(x,\eta)\psi(x,\sigma)\,dx.

(1.20)

(
1.18
)-(
1.20
) is the starting point for our analysis of the continuous component of the solution.
For the moment let us just point out that there are two main terms:
the first time integral which gives the (main) feedback of the internal mode into the continuous part of the solution,
and the term
F
in (
1.19
)-(
1.20
) which contains the main
nonlinear interaction in the continuous component.

The Nonlinear Spectral Distribution (NSD)

The distribution
\mu
in (
1.19
)-(
1.20
)
characterizes the interaction between the generalized eigenfunctions,
and we call it the “
Nonlinear Spectral Distribution
” (NSD),
following
[
17
,
41
]
.
Note that in the unperturbed case
V=0
the NSD is just a delta function
\delta(\xi-\eta-\sigma)
.
In contrast with this, in equations (
1.19
)-(
1.20
) all frequencies interact with each other without any a priori constraint.

To handle (
1.19
) and prove a priori estimates in weighted spaces one needs
to exploit the oscillations of the phase in distorted frequency space,
through integrations by parts in
\eta,\sigma
.
This requires information on the regularity of the integrand, and in particular of the distribution
\mu.

As it turns out,
\mu
is not regular in any direction but can be decomposed in many pieces
that are regular in at least one direction.
Oversimplifying, one can think that
\mu
looks as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\delta(\xi-\eta-\sigma)+\mathrm{p.v.}\frac{1}{|\xi-\sigma|-|\eta|}+\mathrm{p.v.}\frac{1}{|\xi|-|\eta+\sigma|}+\cdots.\end{split}

(1.21)

A precise version of this was shown by the second author and Soffer in
[
41
]
.
In the Klein-Gordon case we need to refine that analysis
and adapt the decomposition of
\mu
to the geometry of the equation
and of the nonlinear phases
\Phi(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=-\langle\xi\rangle\pm\langle\eta\rangle\pm\langle\sigma\rangle
.

One useful realization in dealing with the term
F
above is that when
\mu
is close to being singular
(for example, looking at the second term on the right-hand side of (
1.21
), when
|\eta|\approx|\xi-\sigma|
)
then
\Phi
is essentially lower bounded, and one can exploit oscillations in
s
.
When instead
\mu
is regular, decay can be obtained from the integral in
\eta,\sigma
.

Once we have dealt with regularity issues for the measure
\mu,
we turn to the other terms that make
up the integrand in (
1.19
), namely the profiles
\widetilde{f}.

Unlike in the case where no internal mode is present,
\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{f}
is not that regular at all:
it cannot be controlled uniformly along the flow, and in fact we show that it grows in time like
\sqrt{t}
.
This leads us to our last point in the strategy.

1.2.3.
Good vs. bad decomposition and the ‘Fermi frequency’

An important idea in our analysis is to further decompose the profile
f
(see (
1.16
))
into a ‘good’ part (that is regular in distorted frequency space), which we call
h
,
and a ‘bad’ or ‘Fermi’ component (singular in Fourier space), which we call
g
.
Looking at (
1.17
) it is natural to let

\displaystyle\begin{split}f&=g+h,\qquad g(t,x)=\chi(|L-2\lambda|)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}A(s)^{2}\,ds\,{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2},\end{split}

(1.22)

where
\chi
is a cutoff with small support around the origin. Notice that
g
has a fairly simple and explicit expression.
Passing to Fourier space and differentiating we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(t,\xi)\approx\chi(|\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda|)\int_{0}^{t}s\,e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}A(s)^{2}\,ds\,\widetilde{{\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}}(\xi).\end{split}

(1.23)

We can then see that
g
behaves poorly when the size of its (distorted) frequencies
approaches the solution of
\langle\xi\rangle=2\lambda
, i.e., the bad ‘Fermi frequency’
\xi_{0}:=\sqrt{(2\lambda)^{2}-1}
.
From rigorous asymptotics for
A
we show that

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(t,\xi)\|}_{L^{2}}\gtrsim\sqrt{t}.

(1.24)

See Remark
1.4
for more on this.
In particular
\widetilde{g}
is quite irregular, which complicates the estimates of expressions like (
1.19
).

Once the bad component has been removed, the hope is that
the remaining component
h
of the solution is more regular and has better localization properties.
We are indeed eventually able to show that, for
t\gg 1
,

{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t,\xi)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim t^{\beta}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
, for some small
\beta
.

1.3.
The main result

Let us introduce some notation and state our precise assumptions before giving the details of our main result.
We let
{\bf P_{c}}
denote the projection onto the continuous spectral subspace,
namely, for every
\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3};{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle{\bf P_{c}}\psi:=\psi-(\phi,\psi)\phi,\qquad(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\,dx.

(1.25)

We make the assumptions that:

•

Regularity and decay of
V
: The potential
V
is sufficiently smooth and decaying.
For simplicity we assume
V\in\mathcal{S}
, but a finite amount of regularity
and decay would suffice.
5
5

5

The main property we need to guarantee is that wave operators are bounded on sufficiently regular weighted Sobolev spaces, as in the statement of the Theorem
2.2
.

•

Coupling to continuous spectrum
:
\lambda\in(1/2,1)
so that
\lambda\notin\sigma_{ac}(-\Delta+V+1)

and
2\lambda\in\sigma_{ac}(-\Delta+V+1).

•

Fermi Golden Rule
: The “Fermi Golden Rule” resonance condition holds:

\displaystyle\Gamma:=\frac{\pi}{2\lambda}\Big({\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2},\delta(L-2\lambda){\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}\Big)>0.

(1.26)

•

Genericity of
V
: the
0
energy level is regular for
H:=-\Delta+V
,
that is,
0
is not an eigenvalue,
nor a resonance, i.e., there is no
\psi\in\langle x\rangle^{1/2+}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
such that
H\psi=0
.
Such a potential
V
is said to be ‘generic’.

The first two conditions above are the same as in
[
48
]
, naturally transposed to the quadratic case.
The last condition ensures that one has the same linear decay for the propagator
e^{itL}{\bf P}_{c}

as the one for
e^{it\langle\nabla\rangle}
.

Recall that we decompose a solution
u
into a discrete and a continuous component:

\displaystyle u(t)=a(t)\phi+v(t),

(1.27)

with
(v(t),\phi)=0
for all
t.

This yields the coupled system (
1.6
). We then pass to a first order system by introducing the new unknowns
A
(see (
3.21
)) and

w:=\partial_{t}v+iLv
which satisfy (
1.6
).

We call
v
or, with a slight abuse of notation,
w
, the
radiation
or
field

component of the solution
u
, and will call
a
the (amplitude of the)

discrete component
or
internal mode
of the solution.

Theorem 1.2
.

Consider (
1.1
), with initial data
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x)
,
u_{t}(0,x)=u_{1}(x)
such that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|(u_{0},\phi)|+|(u_{1},\phi)|\leqslant{\varepsilon}_{0},\\
&{\|(\langle\nabla\rangle u_{0},u_{1})\|}_{W^{3,1}}+{\|\langle x\rangle(\langle\nabla\rangle u_{0},u_{1})\|}_{H^{3}}+{\|(\langle\nabla\rangle u_{0},u_{1})\|}_{H^{N}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}_{0}\end{split}

(1.28)

for
6
6

6

See (
1.45
) for a more quantitative, albeit far from optimal, choice.

N\gg 1
.
Then, there exists
\overline{{\varepsilon}}\in(0,1)
such that, for all
{\varepsilon}_{0}\leqslant\bar{{\varepsilon}}
,

•

The equation (
1.1
) under the assumptions stated above, has a unique global solution

u\in C({\mathbb{R}};H^{N+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))
such that the following hold:

u=a(t)\phi+v(t)
with

\displaystyle|a(t)|\approx{\varepsilon}_{0}(1+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}t)^{-1/2},

(1.29)

and

\displaystyle\langle t\rangle{\big\|\partial_{t}v+iLv\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\approx 1,\qquad t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2},

(1.30)

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{t}v+iLv\big\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta},

(1.31)

for arbitrary small
\delta>0
.

•

Furthermore, we have the following asymptotic behavior:
Let
f:=e^{-itL}(\partial_{t}+iL)v.

Then, there exists
f_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta}H^{N}_{x}
, with
\delta>0
arbitrarily small as above,
such that

\displaystyle{\|f(t)-f_{\infty}\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta^{\prime}}

(1.32)

for some small
\delta^{\prime}>0
.
Finally, we have the asymptotic growth

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{f}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\gtrsim\langle t\rangle^{1/2},\qquad t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}.

(1.33)

The proof of our main theorem is based on a bootstrap argument for the
amplitude of the internal mode
a(t)
and for the ‘good’ component of the profile
f
,
which we call
h
. Proposition
1.10
is our main bootstrap.

The bad part of the profile
g=h-f
(see (
4.5
) and (
3.34
) for the definition,
as well as the simplified version in (
1.22
))
is the one responsible for the weak decay rate (
1.30
) and the growth (
1.33
).
We discuss this in more details in Remark
1.4
below.

The decay (
1.29
) is part of the main bootstrap; see (
1.48
).

The proof of (
1.30
) is given in §
1.6.2
as a consequence of the main bootstrap proposition.

(
1.31
) is a direct consequence of (
1.32
).
The proof of the latter is discussed in §
1.6.1
;
there we show an analogous scattering statement with
g
instead of
f
,
while Proposition
9.1
gives the (stronger) scattering statement for
h
.

The growth estimate (
1.33
) is a consequence of the inequality

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\gtrsim\langle t\rangle^{1/2},

(1.34)

which is proven in §
3.5.2

and the bound
{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\langle t\rangle^{\beta}
, for small
\beta>0
,
in the main bootstrap Proposition
1.10
; see (
1.49
).

1.4.
Some remarks

Here are a few remarks about our result and some related problems.

Remark 1.3

(About energy and decay)
.

The energy of the solution, initially split between
discrete and continuous modes, slowly migrates to the continuous spectrum,
since the
H^{N}_{x}
norm of
a(t)\phi
decays to zero.

The full solution
u
decays over time as slowly as
a(t)
, that is, at the rate of
t^{-1/2}
,
t\gg 1
.
The internal mode is responsible for the slow decay,
while the radiation part decays faster, but still at a rate that is not integrable in time.
Moreover, the
L^{\infty}_{x}
decay rate comes without smallness.
On the other hand we can prove an (almost optimal)
L^{6}_{x}
decay estimate with some smallness:

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{t}v+iLv\big\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\beta}\langle t\rangle^{-1+\delta},

(1.35)

for small
\delta\geqslant\beta+3/N
, where
N
is the Sobolev regularity
and
\beta
is a small parameter (see (
1.45
)). (
1.35
) is proven in §
1.6.3
.

Note that we prove sharpness of the decay both for the discrete
component and for the radiation in
L^{\infty}_{x}.

The Fermi Golden Rule is ultimately the reason for this sharpness,
stressing the fact that the interaction between the discrete and continuous parts
of the spectrum dictates the exact behavior of the solution.

Remark 1.4

(The bad component: A
1
d “singularity” and (
1.33
))
.

As already mentioned, a key part of our analysis is to isolate the bad contributions
to the radiation by splitting the profile
f
into
h
and
g
, see (
4.5
).
The bad part
g
is quite explicit and it is apparent that it behaves badly
for small
\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda
, that is, as
|\xi|\longrightarrow\xi_{0}:=\sqrt{4\lambda^{2}-1}
,
as the integral lacks oscillations.
Notice that this is a
1
d singularity in
|\xi|-\xi_{0}
.
In this respect our
3
d problem shares many features with its
1
d analogue
which arises in the study of the stability of the
1
d kink for the
\phi^{4}
model.
See §
1.5
for some literature and recent advances on this.

The growth of the bad component
g
of the radiation profile
f
, as given in (
1.33
),
can be shown through accurate asymptotics for the amplitude of the internal mode
A

(or, equivalently, of the renormalized amplitude
B
).
As expected, this growth happens around the Fermi frequency
\xi_{0}
.
In (
4.26
) we prove a similar upper bound up to a logarithm using just basic estimates.

Similar growth phenomena (but with a small factor of
{\varepsilon}
)
were previously observed (though not rigorously proven) for equations without external potentials
in
2
d; see
[
14
,
15
]
and references therein.
A related growth phenomenon is also expected in
1
d problems (with potentials)
in the presence of internal modes
(see
[
13
]
) or zero-energy resonances (see the discussions in the works
[
35
]
and
[
18
]
).

Remark 1.5

(Pointwise asymptotics and scattering)
.

It is also possible to show a more refined scattering statement in
L^{\infty}_{\xi}.

We only state a result below and prove it rigorously in a separate note
[
34
]
.

First, by using the estimates (and variants of those) in Sections
5
-
8

one can show that there there exist
\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{f})\in L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}_{\xi}
and
h_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}^{\delta}L^{\infty}_{\xi}
such that

\displaystyle{\big\|\widetilde{h}(t)-\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{f})(t)-h_{\infty}\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}

(1.36)

for some
\delta>0.

Then, combining this with the asymptotics (
3.64
),
letting
F_{\infty}(t):=\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{f})(t)+h_{\infty}-g_{\infty},
we can show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{f}(t,\xi)=\int_{0}^{t}\exp\Big(is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)+i\tfrac{2c_{2}\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}s\big)\Big)y(s)^{2}ds\,e^{i2\Psi_{\infty}}\,\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}}(\xi)\\
+F_{\infty}(t,\xi)+\,O_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}\big(\varepsilon^{\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}\big),\end{split}

(1.37)

where
y(s):=Y_{0}\,(1+(\Gamma/\lambda)sY^{2}_{0})^{-1/2}
,
with
Y_{0}
that can be determined in terms of the initial data
(through (
3.51
) with (
3.43
) and (
3.34
)),
the phase
\Psi_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}^{\delta}L^{\infty}

(see (
3.60
) with (
3.55
) and (
3.57
)),
and
c_{2}
is the constant in (
3.44
). Relying on new bilinear lemmas for operators with singular kernels, we show in
[
34
]

that the correction
\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{f})
is of size
{\varepsilon}^{\delta}.

Next, note that the time integral in (
1.37
) is large when
\langle\xi\rangle\approx 2\lambda
:
indeed, when
|\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda|\ll t^{-1}
,
t\gg 1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\exp\Big(is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)+i2c_{2}\tfrac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}s\big)\Big)y(s)^{2}ds\,e^{i2\Psi_{\infty}}\,\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}}(\xi)\\
&\approx\int_{0}^{t}\exp\Big(i2c_{2}\tfrac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}s\big)\Big)\frac{Y_{0}^{2}}{(1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}sY^{2}_{0})}\,ds\,e^{i2\Psi_{\infty}}\,\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}}(\xi)+o(1),\\
&=\frac{1}{2ic_{2}}\Big[\exp\Big(i2c_{2}\tfrac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}t\big)\Big)-1\Big]e^{i2\Psi_{\infty}}\,\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}}(\xi)+o(1).\end{split}

(1.38)

The quantity above has modulus
O(1)
for (arbitrarily large) times
t\gtrsim Y_{0}^{-2}\approx{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}
.
Together with (
1.36
) and the smallness of the correction, this entails that
\widetilde{f}(t,\xi)

is also of size
O(1)
around the frequency
|\xi|=\sqrt{4\lambda^{2}-1}.

Note, however, that its
H^{N}_{x}
norm is small,
as in (
1.31
) and (
1.32
), since the singularity/growth happens on a lower dimensional set.

Remark 1.6

(The case without bound states)
.

If one considers (
1.1
) when
H=-\Delta+V
has no bound states
(for example when
V\geqslant 0
or has a small negative part) the problem is substantially easier.
Nevertheless, as mentioned in the introduction, global decay of solutions in this case
was not known before this work.
It is certainly possible that, in this special case one could actually prove stronger decay estimates
than just the almost sharp
L^{6}_{x}
bound we have in (
1.30
).
The case of the Schrödinger equation with a
u^{2}
nonlinearity was treated by
the second author and Soffer in
[
41
]
where integrable-in-time decay
was obtained.

Remark 1.7

(Comparing to Soffer-Weinstein 1: weighted norms)
.

In the seminal paper
[
48
]
, the authors treated the cubic problem.
Our result settles the quadratic problem, and therefore
the case of a generic (semilinear) Hamiltonian perturbation of the linear Klein-Gordon equation.

Our analysis is very much complicated by the need to estimate weighted norms to control quadratic interactions.
And, as observed, weighted norms naturally grow due to the interaction between discrete and continuous spectrum.
For the cubic problem, if one were to measure weighted norms, one would also see a large growth over time.
However, the cubic problem can be handled just using suitable
L^{p}_{x}
type norms
(for example bootstrapping sharp
L^{8}_{x}
decay),
so that the main problems we face can be bypassed.

Remark 1.8

(Comparing to Soffer-Weinstein 2: an apparent paradox)
.

In comparing the result of
[
48
]
on the cubic problem
with our result, there is one apparent conceptual paradox that it is worth addressing.
One could think that the objective here is to destroy
coherent states (the bound states (
1.2
)) and, therefore, a stronger perturbation (quadratic)
should be more favorable than a weaker one (cubic).
However, as it is clear from the approach that one naturally needs to follow, the heart of the matter is to control the dynamics of (
1.6
) for long-times
which this is substantially harder for a lower power nonlinearity.

Remark 1.9
.

The analysis can be extended to the case where the nonlinearity is multiplied by a smooth bounded coefficient, provided the Fermi Golden rule is suitably adapted.

1.5.
Some literature

As already mentioned at the beginning of this introduction,
our work is greatly inspired by the seminal paper
[
48
]
where the cubic KG problem was treated,
and the foundational paper by Sigal
[
45
]
.
We refer to the beautiful introductions of
[
45
,
48
]

for a more thorough discussion about the history of the problem and of the physical background.
The problem of meta-stability in the presence of multiple bound states in
3
d was treated by Bambusi-Cuccagna
[
5
]
.
For the nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) the problem of meta-stability (for excited states) was
considered by Tsai-Yau
[
49
]
; see also the recent advances by Cuccagna-Maeda
[
9
]
,
their survey
[
10
]
and references therein.
In one dimension, nonlinear Klein-Gordon equation with bound states were studied by
Komech-Kopylova
[
30
,
31
]
in the context of the kink stability problem
for relativistic Ginzburg-Landau theories with sufficiently flat wells.
In a recent monograph
[
13
]
, Delort-Masmoudi studied this problem in the context of the
one dimensional
\phi^{4}
equation and the stability of its kink solution under odd perturbations;
these authors were able to prove a meta-stability theorem for times
of
O({\varepsilon}_{0}^{-4})
, where
{\varepsilon}_{0}
is the size of the perturbation.
See also the recent work by Cuccagna-Maeda on NLS
[
11
]
and references therein.

Concerning the general theory of nonlinear PDE with potentials, let us focus on recent
works about the study of nonlinear resonances
7
7

7

Since
it would be impossible for us to give a complete account of the literature,
we refer to the surveys
[
51
,
47
,
43
]
and reference therein
for more general discussions on PDE with potentials and various applications.
.
Germain-Hani-Walsh
[
16
]
treated the quadratic NLS
i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta+V)u=\bar{u}^{2}
in
3
d and
established a first set of useful Coifman-Meyer type multilinear bounds for the NSD
\mu
(see (
1.20
)),
together with a commutation identity for the weight
|x|
.
The first author proved global decay in the case of a
u^{2}
nonlinearity with small
V

[
32
]
.
Note that while a
\bar{u}^{2}
nonlinearity is non-resonant (for any type of evolution), a
u^{2}
nonlinearity has
classical space-time resonances even in the case
V=0
.
See also
[
33
]
for the case of electromagnetic perturbations.

More recently, Soffer and the second author
[
41
]
, following the initial ideas of
[
16
]
,
laid down the foundations for some of the harmonic analysis tools that we also use in
the present paper; these include a precise study of the singularities of
\mu

and product estimates for the operators associated to the various terms in the expansion of
\mu

(see (
1.21
)).

While the approach based on the use of the distorted Fourier transform
to study nonlinear oscillations in
d=3

[
16
]
had not been fully developed prior to
[
41
]
and the present work,
it was successfully used in several
1
d problems in recent years.
We remark that in one spatial dimension the analysis of
\mu
is simpler
since the generalized eigenfunctions (
1.14
) solve an ODE and differ from plane waves
e^{ix\xi}
by a fast decaying term. Of course, in one dimension
the dispersive properties of waves are weaker compared to the
3
d case.
Concerning
1
d works that use the dFT in a spirit similar to ours,
we mention the papers on NLS
[
17
,
8
,
7
]
and on KG
[
18
,
19
]
.
For other approaches to similar or related problems,
we refer the reader to the works by Delort
[
12
]
and Naumkin
[
38
]
on NLS,
and by Lindblad-Lührmann-Soffer
[
35
]
and Lindblad-Lührmann-Schlag-Soffer
[
36
]
on KG.
We also mention the major recent advances on the related problem of
the stability of kinks by Kowalczyk-Martel-Muñoz
[
27
]
,
Kowalczyk-Martel-Muñoz-Van de Bosch
[
28
]
, Delort-Masmoudi
[
13
]
and Lührmann-Schlag
[
37
]
.

1.6.
The main bootstrap and proof of Theorem
1.2

Our analysis will be carried out for the system for the profiles (of the first order equations)
given by (
1.7
)-(
1.8
) and (
1.16
)-(
1.17
).
In particular, our main bootstrap argument will be based on estimates for
the amplitude
|A|\approx|\dot{a}|+|a|
,
and norms of the distorted transform of
f=e^{-itL}w=e^{-itL}(\partial_{t}+iL)v
;
see the main bootstrap Propositions
3.1
and
4.2
.
The estimates obtained through this argument will imply the
decay properties in (
1.29
)-(
1.30
)
and the statements (
1.32
) and (
1.33
) as explained just after the main theorem.
We explain in detail our bootstrap below, together with the global structure of the proof.

Since
u=a(t)\phi+v(t)
with
(v(t),\phi)=0
,
the first inequality in (
1.28
) implies

\displaystyle|a(0)|+|\dot{a}(0)|\leqslant{\varepsilon}_{0},

(1.39)

and the second gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\partial_{t}v(0)+i\langle\nabla\rangle v(0)\|_{W^{3,1}}+{\big\|\langle x\rangle\big(\partial_{t}v(0)+i\langle\nabla\rangle v(0)\big)\big\|}_{H^{3}}\\
&\leqslant(1+2{\|\langle x\rangle\phi\|}_{H^{4}}+2\|\phi\|_{W^{4,1}})\,{\varepsilon}_{0},\\
&{\big\|\partial_{t}v(0)+i\langle\nabla\rangle v(0)\big\|}_{H^{N}}\leqslant(1+2{\|\phi\|}_{H^{N+1}}){\varepsilon}_{0}.\end{split}

(1.40)

To transfer the assumption to
w
(in Fourier space) we use
the boundedness of wave operators on
H^{s}
(see Theorem
2.2
) which in particular implies,
for all
f\in L^{2}_{ac}
,

\displaystyle{\|\langle\xi\rangle\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{f}\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|\langle\xi\rangle\widetilde{f}\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\|\langle x\rangle Lf\|}_{L^{2}}.

(1.41)

Then, from (
1.39
)-(
1.40
) and (
1.7
) and (
1.16
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|A(0)|\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0},\qquad{\|\langle\xi\rangle^{3}\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{w}(0)\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|w(0)\|}_{H^{N}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0},\end{split}

(1.42)

with
w(0)=w(0,x)=f(0,x)
.

We define

\displaystyle\rho(t):=|A(t)|^{2},\qquad\rho_{0}:=\rho(0)\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}.

(1.43)

Recall that
f=e^{-itL}w
and let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{h}:=\widetilde{f}+\widetilde{g},\\
&\widetilde{g}(t,\xi):=-\chi_{C}(\xi)\int_{0}^{t}B^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}ds\,\widetilde{{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}}(\xi),\qquad\chi_{C}(\xi):=\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\end{split}

(1.44)

for
C
sufficiently large so that
2^{-C+10}\leqslant 2\lambda-1
,
where
B
is defined from
A
through (
3.34
); see (
4.5
).
Notice that
\widetilde{g}(t,\xi)
can be estimated using only information about
A(t)
.
Therefore, we can use
A
and
\widetilde{h}
as our main unknowns.

We pick a large enough absolute constant
C_{0}
and set

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}:=C_{0}{\varepsilon}_{0},\qquad\beta=\frac{1}{300},\qquad N=10^{6},\qquad\delta_{N}:=5/N.

(1.45)

C_{0}
is chosen to be at least larger than the implied constants in (
1.42
).

We assume that local-in-time solutions for the system (
1.6
)
with
(a,v)\in C^{1}([0,1];\mathbb{R})\times C([0,1];H^{N+1}_{x})
and
v_{t}\in C([0,1];H^{N}_{x})
,
have been constructed by standard methods.
The following proposition is our main bootstrap.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let the following a priori assumptions hold for some
T>0:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\big(1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\big)\frac{\rho(t)}{\rho_{0}}-1\right|<\frac{1}{2},

(1.46)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle^{-1}\rho^{\beta-1/2}(t){\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon}^{\beta},\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\|h(t)\|}_{H^{N}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

(1.47)

Then, there exists
\bar{{\varepsilon}}>0
such that if
C_{0}^{-1}{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}_{0}\leqslant\bar{{\varepsilon}}
, we have the improved estimates

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big|\big(1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\big)\frac{\rho(t)}{\rho_{0}}-1\Big|<\frac{1}{4},

(1.48)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle^{-1}\rho^{\beta-1/2}(t){\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}^{\beta},\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\|h(t)\|}_{H^{N}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

(1.49)

Note that (
1.46
) implies

\displaystyle\rho(t)\approx\frac{{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}}{1+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}t},\qquad|a(t)|+|\dot{a}(t)|\approx\frac{{\varepsilon}_{0}}{\sqrt{1+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}t}}.

Proposition
1.10
is proved through Proposition
3.1
(which proves (
1.48
))
and Proposition
4.2
(which proves (
1.49
)).
Note that the assumptions (
3.18
) of Proposition
3.1

are stated in terms of norms of
w
rather than
h
for convenience;
in Remark
3.3
we show how (
1.47
) implies (
3.18
).

Remark 1.11
.

The expression for the time-weights in (
1.47
) may seem slightly complicated.
This is due to the fact that
\rho(t)\approx\rho_{0}(1+\rho_{0}t)^{-1}

does not decrease until times of the order
\rho_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}
,
and that we want to use a single bound to bootstrap for all times.
To fix ideas, notice that, in view of (
1.46
), we have the following bounds:

•

For times up to the standard local existence time
T_{loc}=O({\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1})
we have

t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\approx\sqrt{\rho_{0}}t\lesssim 1
and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\rho^{1/2-\beta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-2\beta}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\approx{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\beta}.\end{split}

(1.50)

•

For intermediate times
t\in[c{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1},C{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}]
we have

t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\approx t\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\gtrsim 1
, and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim t\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\cdot\rho^{1/2-\beta}(t)\cdot{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\lesssim t{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2-\beta}.\end{split}

Note how the
-\beta
exponent here gives us room to fit a
\log t
or
small polynomial-in-time growth for the norms above, as
t
approaches
{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}
.

•

For large times
t\geqslant C{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}
we have
\rho(t)\approx t^{-1}

and the bounds (
1.47
) read instead

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{t}\cdot\rho^{1/2-\beta}(t)\cdot{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\lesssim t^{\beta}{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\beta}.\end{split}

(1.51)

In the course of the proof we will prove bounds taking care of all these time scales,
and will only a few times simplify our notation by letting
t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}

when it is obvious how to take care of smaller times.
Nevertheless, we invite the reader to think that time
t
is always large enough so that
one can effectively work with the simpler looking bounds (
1.51
).

Once the main bootstrap is proven, a standard continuity argument
provides us with global solutions satisfying, in particular,

\displaystyle\rho(t)\leqslant\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t}

(1.52)

and

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\leqslant\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle\rho^{1/2-\beta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta},\qquad{\|h(t)\|}_{H^{N}}\leqslant{\varepsilon},

(1.53)

for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.
Then, (
1.52
) and the definitions (
1.43
) and (
1.7
)
immediately imply (
1.29
).

We complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
below by showing (
1.30
),
(
1.31
) and (
1.32
) (or indicating where they are proven later in the paper).
The lower bound (
1.33
) is proven in §
3.5.2
through the proof of (
1.34
).

Without loss of generality we can restrict to times
t\geqslant 1
.
Recall that
(\partial_{t}+iL)v=w=e^{itL}f=e^{itL}h-e^{itL}g
, see (
4.5
).

1.6.1.
Proof of (
1.31
) and (
1.32
)

Recall from (
1.44
) that
f=g-h
.
For
h
we can use the statement of Proposition
9.1
to see that there exists

h_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}H^{N}_{x}
such that, for some small enough
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle{\|h(t)-h_{\infty}\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{1+\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}.

(1.54)

For
g
we proceed as follows: from the definition (
1.44
)
using the boundedness of wave operators (Theorem
2.2
),
using
|B(s)|^{2}\lesssim|A(s)|^{2}=\rho(s)
(see Lemma
3.5
and (
3.34
)),
and the Strichartz estimate of Lemma
2.6
with
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r},\gamma)=(2,\infty,0)

(see the notation in Subsection
1.8
), we have, for all
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leqslant T
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|g(t_{1})-g(t_{2})\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}&\lesssim{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}(s)e^{-isL}\big(L^{N}{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}\big)\,|B(s)|^{2}\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}\big\|}_{W^{N+2,1}_{x}}\,{\|\rho\|}_{L^{2}_{t}([t_{1},t_{2}])}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta}t_{1}^{-\delta/2};\end{split}

(1.55)

for the last inequality we have used
\rho(t)\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1/2-\delta/2}{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta}
.
In particular
g
is Cauchy in
H^{N}_{x}
and there exists
g_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}^{1-\delta}H^{N}_{x}
such that

\displaystyle{\|g(t)-g_{\infty}\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta/2}.

(1.56)

(
1.54
) and (
1.56
) give us (
1.32
) with
f_{\infty}:=g_{\infty}-h_{\infty}
.

\hfill\Box

1.6.2.
Proof of (
1.30
)

Note that this
L^{\infty}_{x}
decay estimate is not part of the bootstrap and it is just obtained a posteriori.
We start from Duhamel’s formula for
w=(\partial_{t}+iL)v
(see (
1.6
))
which, up to irrelevant constants, is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&w(t)=e^{itL}w(0)+D_{1}(t)+D_{2}(t)+D_{3}(t),\\
&D_{1}(t)=e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}\,a^{2}(s)ds\,\theta,\qquad\theta:={\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2},\\
&D_{2}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}\,a(s){\bf P}_{c}\Big(\phi\,\Im\frac{w(s)}{L}\Big)ds,\\
&D_{3}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}\,{\bf P}_{c}\Big(\frac{\Im w(s)}{L}\Big)^{2}ds.\end{split}

(1.57)

The bound for the linear solution follows from (
2.15
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|e^{itL}w(0)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}&\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-3/2}\|w_{0}\|_{W^{3,1}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-3/2}.\end{split}

(1.58)

From the linear estimate (
2.5
) and the bound (
1.52
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|D_{1}(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{3/2}}|a(s)|^{2}ds{\big\|{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}\big\|}_{H^{2}\cap W^{3,1}}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{3/2}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}}{1+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}s}ds\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.\end{split}

(1.59)

Similarly, we can estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|D_{2}(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{3/2}}|a(s)|{\big\|{\bf P}_{c}(\phi\,L^{-1}\Im w(s)\big\|}_{W^{3,1}\cap H^{2}}ds\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{3/2}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{0}}{\sqrt{1+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}s}}{\|w(s)\|}_{W^{2,6}}ds.\end{split}

The bound

\displaystyle{\|D_{2}(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-5/4},

(1.60)

would then follow from the estimate

\displaystyle{\|w(s)\|}_{W^{2,6}}\lesssim\langle s\rangle^{-1+\delta}.

(1.61)

A stronger version of (
1.61
) is (
1.35
) proven in §
1.6.3
below.

An estimate for the last term in (
1.57
), in fact the stronger inequality

\displaystyle{\|D_{3}(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho^{0+}(t)}{\langle t\rangle},

(1.62)

is given in (
3.27
) and proven in Section
5
(see Proposition
5.2
).
The bounds (
1.58
)-(
1.60
) and (
1.62
) give us one side of the approximate equality (
1.30
).

In view of (
1.57
), in order to prove the sharpness of the
L^{\infty}_{x}
decay
it will suffice to show that
{\|D_{1}(t)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\gtrsim\langle t\rangle^{-1}
,
t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}^{-2}
,
which in turn follows from the stronger inequality

\displaystyle{\big\|\langle x\rangle^{-5}D_{1}(t,\cdot)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\gtrsim\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

(1.63)

We start by first expanding
a^{2}
as in (
1.7
),
and subsequently integrate by parts in time, to find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{1}&=iD_{1,0}-iD_{1,1}-iD_{1,2},\\
&D_{1,0}(t):=A_{0}^{2}\frac{e^{itL}\theta}{L-2\lambda}+e^{itL}\Big(2|A_{0}|^{2}\frac{\theta}{L}+\overline{A_{0}}^{2}\frac{\theta}{L+2\lambda}\Big),\\
&D_{1,1}(t):=e^{2i\lambda t}A^{2}(t)\frac{\theta}{L-2\lambda}+2|A(t)|^{2}\frac{\theta}{L}+e^{-2i\lambda t}\overline{A(t)}^{2}\frac{\theta}{L+2\lambda},\\
&D_{1,2}(t):=e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}\bigg(e^{2i\lambda s}\frac{(\dot{A}A)(s)}{L-2\lambda}+\frac{(\dot{A}\bar{A})(s)+(\dot{\bar{A}}A)(s)}{L}+e^{-2i\lambda s}\frac{(\dot{\bar{A}}\bar{A})(s)}{L+2\lambda}\bigg)ds\,\theta.\end{split}

(1.64)

It is not hard to see that the first and third terms are lower order.
Indeed, from the equation for
\dot{A}
in (
1.8
) with (
1.61
)
and (
1.46
) we see that
|\dot{A}|\lesssim s^{-1}
;
therefore, using the local decay estimates (
2.7
) we get

\displaystyle{\big\|\langle x\rangle^{-5}D_{1,0}(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}+{\big\|\langle x\rangle^{-5}D_{1,2}(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-3/2}.

To deal with the leading order term
D_{1,1}
,
assume by contradiction that there exists
\zeta\in\mathbb{C},|\zeta|=1
, such that

\displaystyle\zeta^{2}\frac{\theta}{L-2\lambda}+2\frac{\theta}{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{2}\frac{\theta}{L+2\lambda}=0.

Then

\displaystyle\theta=-\zeta^{-2}(L-2\lambda)\bigg[2\frac{\theta}{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{2}\frac{\theta}{L+2\lambda}\bigg].

On the Fourier side (see Proposition
2.1
),
after evaluating at the Fermi frequency
|\xi_{0}|:=\sqrt{4\lambda^{2}-1},
the above implies that
\widetilde{\theta}(\xi_{0})=0,

which violates the Fermi Golden rule (
1.26
).
From this we infer

\displaystyle\inf_{\zeta\in\mathbb{C},|\zeta|=1}\Bigg\|\zeta^{2}\frac{\theta}{L-2\lambda}+2\frac{\theta}{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{2}\frac{\theta}{L+2\lambda}\Bigg\|_{\langle x\rangle^{5}L^{\infty}}\gtrsim 1.

Therefore, applying this to
\zeta=e^{i\lambda t}A(t)\rho^{-1/2}

(recall
\rho(t):=|A(t)|^{2}
), we have the lower bound

\displaystyle\rho^{-1}(t){\big\|\langle x\rangle^{-5}D_{1,1}(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\gtrsim 1

and hence obtain (
1.63
). We have proved (
1.30
).
\hfill\Box

1.6.3.
Proof of (
1.35
) and (
1.61
)

It suffices to show the slightly stronger estimate

\displaystyle{\big\|w(t)\big\|}_{W^{2,6}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\beta}\langle t\rangle^{-1+\delta},

(1.65)

for, recall,
w=\partial_{t}v+iLv=e^{itL}h-e^{itL}g
and some
\delta>\beta>0
.

Using Bernstein’s inequality and the bounds (
1.53
)
(here
P_{k}
is the distorted Littlewood-Paley projection, see (
1.71
))
we can estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|e^{itL}h\|}_{W^{2,6}_{x}}&\lesssim\sum_{0\leqslant k\,:\,2^{k}\lesssim t^{\gamma}}2^{2k}{\|e^{itL}P_{k}h(t)\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}+{\big\|e^{itL}P_{\geqslant t^{\gamma}}h(t)\big\|}_{H^{3}}\\
&\lesssim t^{11\gamma/3}t^{-1}{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|}_{L^{2}_{\xi}}+t^{-\gamma(N-3)}{\|h(t)\|}_{H^{N}}\\
&\lesssim t^{-1+\beta+11\gamma/3}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}+t^{-\gamma(N-3)}{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

(1.66)

This is consistent with the estimate in (
1.65
) provided

\gamma
is chosen small enough so that

11\gamma/3+\beta\leqslant\delta
and
N
is chosen large enough so that
\gamma(N-3)\geqslant 1
.

For the contribution from
g
we can use the linear estimate (
2.5
) together with Sobolev’s embedding,
and the bound
|B(s)|^{2}\lesssim|A(s)|^{2}=\rho(s)
, see Lemma
3.5
and (
3.34
), to see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|e^{itL}g\|}_{W^{2,6}_{x}}&={\Big\|e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}\,B^{2}(s)ds\,{\bf P}_{c}\phi^{2}\Big\|}_{W^{2,6}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle}\,\rho(s)ds\,{\big\|\phi^{2}\big\|}_{W^{11/3,6/5}_{x}\cap H^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\log(1+t)}{\langle t\rangle}.\end{split}

(1.67)

This proves (
1.65
) if
t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1}
.
When instead
t\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1},
(
1.65
) holds in view of Sobolev’s embedding,
and the a priori bounds, see (
1.48
) and (
1.49
),
provided we let
\delta\geqslant 2\beta
.
\hfill\Box

1.7.
Structure of the paper

Section
2
contains some preliminary material concerning the distorted Fourier transform
and linear estimates (pointwise decay and Strichartz).

Section
3
is dedicated to the discrete mode ODE analysis and the proof of radiation damping.
The main estimate is stated in the bootstrap Proposition
3.1
.
To complete its proof we use the estimate (
3.27
) which is proven later on
in Sections
5
-
7
along with weighted estimates.

Section
4
sets up the analysis for the continuous component of the solution
v
.
We split the profile into a ‘good’ and a ‘bad’ component and write out Duhamel’s formula for
the good component
h
; see Lemma
4.1
.
The main bootstrap estimate on weighted and Sobolev norms of
h
is stated in Proposition
4.2
.
The proof of this proposition occupies the rest of the paper.

Sections
5
,
6
and
7
constitute the bulk of the proof and deal with the quadratic terms in the continuous component.
Here we prove the main weighted estimate as well as the decay estimates used
in the ODE analysis in Section
3
,
under the assumptions that all frequencies are upperbounded by a small power of the time variable.
The argument uses a splitting of the NSD
\mu
(see (
1.20
))
into a singular and a regular part which is described in §
5.2
-
5.4
.
We also give bilinear estimates for the corresponding pieces,
as well as pointwise estimates for the regular part.
In Section
6
we treat the terms corresponding to the singular part of the measure.
In Section
7
we handle the terms that come from the regular part.
In both of these sections we distinguish the different types of interactions
between the good and bad parts of the solution, since they require different strategies.

In Section
8
we estimate the mixed-type nonlinearity which is quadratic
in the continuous-discrete components. This is not as hard as the quadratic terms that only
involve the continuous components, but it is still non-trivial; one of the main difficulties
is the need to apply normal forms which delocalize the bilinear operators
and make the combined use of local-decay and Strichartz-type estimates less efficient.

In Section
9
we deal with the high frequency estimates.
First we control the Sobolev norm of
h
.
Since the decay is not integrable we need to resort to Strichartz estimates here,
and exploit the fact that the nonlinearity is strongly semilinear.
Then we prove a result which controls the interactions when the frequencies involved are
larger than a small power of the time variable.

Appendix
A
and Appendix
B

contain results on the nonlinear spectral distribution, bilinear product estimates
for the relevant operators appearing in our analysis, and some other supporting material.
The results there are mostly borrowed from
[
41
]
and
[
16
]
.

1.8.
Notation

We adopt the following notation, most of which are standard.

\langle x\rangle=:\sqrt{1+|x|^{2}}
.

a\vee b
denotes the largest number between
a
and
b
,
\max(a,b)
;

a\wedge b
denotes the smallest number between
a
and
b
,
\min(a,b)
.

We let
a^{+}=a\vee 0
and
a^{-}=a\wedge 0
.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
we denote its integer part (the largest integer smaller than
x
) by
\lfloor x\rfloor
.

\Re
and
\Im
denote the real and imaginary parts of a complex number.

We use
a\lesssim b
when
a\leqslant Cb
for some absolute constant
C>0
independent on
a
and
b
.

a\approx b
means that
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
.
When
a
and
b
are expressions depending on variables or parameters, the inequalities
are assumed to hold uniformly over these.

We denote by
a+
, resp.
a-
, a number
b>a
, resp.
b<a
, that can be chosen arbitrarily close to
a
.

We use standard notation for Lebesgue, Sobolev and Besov norms, such as
L^{p}
,
W^{s,p}
and
B^{s,p}_{q}

with
H^{s}=W^{s,2}
.

We denote by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(f):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-ix\xi}f(x)\,dx

(1.68)

the standard Fourier transform of
f
.

For a kernel
K:(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
,
we denote its Schur norm by

\displaystyle{\|K\|}_{Sch}:=\Big(\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|K(x,y)|\,dy\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\Big(\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|K(x,y)|\,dx\Big)^{1/2}.

(1.69)

This will always be used in conjunction with Schur’s test:
if
(T_{K}f)(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}K(x,y)f(y)\,dy
, then
{\|T_{K}\|}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\leqslant{\|K\|}_{Sch}
.

We will often use a dot symbol ‘
\cdot
’ to distinguish the bounds on various quantities
involved in multilinear estimates; see for example the estimates after (
6.36
) or (
6.50
).
This is meant to help the reader navigate some of the longer bounds.

Cutoffs
.
We fix a smooth even cutoff function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]

supported in
[-8/5,8/5]
and equal to
1
on
[-5/4,5/4]
.
For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we define
\varphi_{k}(x):=\varphi(2^{-k}x)-\varphi(2^{-k+1}x)
,
so that the family
(\varphi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
forms a partition of unity,

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{k}(\xi)=1,\quad\xi\neq 0.

We let

\displaystyle\varphi_{I}(x):=\sum_{k\in I\cap{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{k},\quad\text{for any}\quad I\subset{\mathbb{R}},\quad\varphi_{\leqslant a}(x):=\varphi_{(-\infty,a]}(x),\quad\varphi_{>a}(x)=\varphi_{(a,\infty)}(x),

(1.70)

with similar definitions for
\varphi_{<a},\varphi_{\geqslant a}
.
We will also denote
\varphi_{\sim k}
a generic smooth cutoff function
that is supported around
|\xi|\approx 2^{k}
, e.g.
\varphi_{[k-2,k+2]}
or
\varphi^{\prime}_{k}
.

We denote by
P_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the Littlewood-Paley projections adapted to the distorted Fourier transform:

\widetilde{P_{k}f}(\xi)=\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi),\quad\widetilde{P_{\leqslant k}f}(\xi)=\varphi_{\leqslant k}(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi),\quad\textrm{ etc.}

(1.71)

We will also sometimes use the notation
f_{k}
for
P_{k}f
.
We will avoid using, as a recurrent notation, the flat analogue of these projections;
they may still appear but only when there is no risk of confusion,
or when they are equivalent to their distorted analogues in view of the boundedness of wave operators
(Theorem
2.2
).

We also define the cutoff functions

\varphi_{k}^{(k_{0})}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{k}(\xi)&\mbox{if}\quad k>\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor,\\
\varphi_{\leqslant\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor}(\xi)&\mbox{if}\quad k=\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor,\end{array}\right.

(1.72)

and

\varphi_{k}^{[k_{0},k_{1}]}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{k}(\xi)&\mbox{if}\quad k\in(\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor,\lfloor k_{1}\rfloor)\cap{\mathbb{Z}},\\
\varphi_{\leqslant\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor}(\xi)&\mbox{if}\quad k=\lfloor k_{0}\rfloor,\\
\varphi_{\geqslant\lfloor k_{1}\rfloor}(\xi)&\mbox{if}\quad k=\lfloor k_{1}\rfloor.\end{array}\right.

(1.73)

Note that the indexes
k_{0}
and
k_{1}
above do not need to be integers.

We will denote by
T
a positive time, and always work on an interval
[0,T]

for our bootstrap estimates; see for example Proposition
4.2
.
To decompose the time integrals, such as (
1.19
), for any
t\in[0,T]
,
we will use a suitable decomposition of the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}

by fixing functions
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{L+1}:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
,
for an integer
L
with
|L-\log_{2}(t+2)|<2
,
with the properties that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{n=0}^{L+1}\tau_{n}(s)={\bf{1}}_{[0,t]}(s),\qquad\mathrm{supp}(\tau_{0})\subset[0,2],\quad\mathrm{supp}(\tau_{L+1})\subset[t-2,t],\\
&\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{supp}(\tau_{n})\subseteq[2^{n-1},2^{n+1}],\quad|\tau_{n}^{\prime}(t)|\lesssim 2^{-n},\quad\mbox{for}\quad n=1,\dots,L.\end{split}

(1.74)

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we gather several tools from the linear theory.
In Subsection
2.1
we state basic properties of the distorted Fourier transform,
and introduce the wave operators. We state some of their boundedness properties on Sobolev and weighted spaces.
In Subsection
2.2
we state some
L^{p}_{x}
decay estimates for the linear propagator
e^{itL}
,
classical Strichartz estimates, and a useful local decay estimate.

2.1.
Linear theory

Let
\psi(x,\xi)
denote the generalized eigenfunction (or modified complex exponential),
that is, the solution to

\displaystyle(-\Delta+V)\psi(x,\xi)=|\xi|^{2}\psi(x,\xi),\qquad\xi,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

(2.1)

with
|\psi(x,\xi)-e^{ix\cdot\xi}|\longrightarrow 0
as
|x|\rightarrow+\infty.

We have the canonical decomposition
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=L^{2}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus L^{2}_{pp}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Note that given our assumption on the potential and (
1.2
),
we have
L^{2}_{pp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathrm{span}(\phi).

In this context we can define a modified Fourier transform:

Proposition 2.1
.

Assume that
V=O(|x|^{-1-}).
Let
f\in L^{2}_{ac}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\cap\mathcal{S}.

Define the distorted Fourier Transform (dFT) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}f(\xi):=\widetilde{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\lim_{R\to+\infty}\int_{|x|\leqslant R}f(x)\overline{\psi(x,\xi)}dx.

(2.2)

Then, the Plancherel identity holds: for any
f,g\in L^{2}_{ac}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\cap\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}f,\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle,

(2.3)

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
has an isometric extension to
L^{2}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with inverse

\displaystyle\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}f\big](x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\lim_{R\to+\infty}\int_{|\xi|\leqslant R}f(\xi)\psi(x,\xi)d\xi.

(2.4)

Moreover, the restriction of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
to
L^{2}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
diagonalizes
-\Delta+V,

in the sense that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(-\Delta+V)\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=|\xi|^{2}.

The above result is due several authors, including
Ikebe
[
23
]
, Alsholm-Schmidt
[
4
]
and Agmon
[
3
]
.
See also
[
16
, Theorem 2.1]
and additional references therein. We will use the inversion formula (
2.4
) to write Duhamel’s formula for
f
in distorted Fourier
space as in Subsection
4.1
.

A useful tool in the study of the operator
-\Delta+V
is the wave operator,
defined on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}L^{2}_{ac}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\qquad\mathcal{W}^{\ast}=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},

(2.5)

where
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the flat (
V=0
) Fourier transform.
The following result follows from the work of Yajima
[
50
]
and Germain-Hani-Walsh
[
16
]
:

Theorem 2.2

(Boundedness of Wave Operators)
.

Assume that
V
is generic, that for some
\delta>0,
and for all
|\alpha|\leqslant l,

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{1+\delta}D^{\alpha}V\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\langle x\rangle^{5+\delta}D^{\alpha}V\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}).

(2.6)

Then, the wave operators (
2.5
) are bounded on
W^{k,p}
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant l

and
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
:

\displaystyle{\big\|\mathcal{W}f\big\|}_{W^{k,p}}+{\big\|\mathcal{W}^{\ast}f\big\|}_{W^{k,p}}\approx{\|f\|}_{W^{k,p}}.

(2.7)

Moreover, under the assumptions (
2.6
) the wave operators are bounded on weighted
L^{2}
-spaces:
for all
f\in L^{2}_{ac}

\displaystyle{\big\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{W}f\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\big\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{W}^{\ast}f\big\|}_{L^{2}}\approx{\|\langle x\rangle f\|}_{L^{2}}.

(2.8)

Proof.

The results (
2.7
) is classical and, under the assumptions (
2.6
),
it is due to Yajima
[
50
, Theorem 1.1]
.

The proof of (
2.2
) is essentially a direct consequence of Theorem 3.4 in
[
16
]
which states the following:
for all radial functions
a(x)
that satisfy
|\nabla a(x)|\leqslant 1
one has

\displaystyle[a(x),\mathcal{W}],\,[a(x),\mathcal{W}^{\ast}]:L^{p}\rightarrow L^{q},\qquad 1/p-1/q\leqslant\min(1/6,1/p),\quad 1\leqslant p<q\leqslant\infty,

(2.9)

where
[a,b]:=ab-ba
. Using (
2.9
) with
q=2
and
p=2-
we see that

\displaystyle{\|[\langle x\rangle,\mathcal{W}]f\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|[\langle x\rangle,\mathcal{W}^{\ast}]f\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\|f\|}_{L^{2-}}\lesssim{\|\langle x\rangle f\|}_{L^{2}}.

In particular, the left-hand side of (
2.8
) is bounded by the right-hand side
thanks to the boundedness of wave operators on
L^{2}
.
The equivalence follows by letting
f
be
\mathcal{W}f
(or
\mathcal{W}^{\ast}f
).
∎

We have the following consequence of Theorem
2.2
:

Lemma 2.3

(Weighted norms and complex conjugate)
.

Assume that (
2.8
) holds.
Then, for all
f\in L^{2}_{ac}

\displaystyle{\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{\overline{f}}\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}}\approx{\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}},

(2.10)

or, in other words,
{\|\widetilde{f}\|}_{H^{1}_{\xi}}\approx{\|\widetilde{\overline{f}}\|}_{H^{1}_{\xi}}
.

Proof.

We first write

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{\overline{f}}\big\|}_{L^{2}}&={\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{W}^{\ast}\overline{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|x\mathcal{W}^{\ast}\overline{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\big\|\langle x\rangle\overline{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}={\big\|\langle x\rangle f\big\|}_{L^{2}},\end{split}

(2.11)

having used the boundedness of wave operators on weighted spaces for the second inequality.
Then, using again (
2.8
), we see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|\langle x\rangle f\big\|}_{L^{2}}&\lesssim{\big\|\langle x\rangle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{f}\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|f\|}_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(2.12)

Putting together (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) shows one of the inequalities in (
2.10
).
Exchanging the role of
f
and
\overline{f}
proves the equivalence.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Lemma
2.3
is useful since our bootstrap assumptions (
1.47
)
involve weighted norms of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}h
in Fourier space, but the nonlinearity will also contain
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{h})
; see for example (
4.4
).
Thanks to Lemma
2.3
our bootstrap assumptions also control
{\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{h})\|}_{L^{2}}

and, therefore, we may essentially disregard the conjugation.
Note that this simplifies the handling of expressions like those in the last line of (
4.4
);
since the conjugation appears before the distorted transform,
we do not need to conjugate the generalized eigenfunctions
\psi(x,\eta)
and
\psi(x,\sigma)
.

2.2.
Decay, Strichartz estimates, and local decay

An important identity related to the Wave operators is the intertwining property

\displaystyle a(-\Delta+V)=\mathcal{W}a(-\Delta)\mathcal{W}^{\ast},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}a(-\Delta+V)\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}a(-\Delta)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}.

(2.13)

Combined with the boundedness of wave operators on
W^{k,p}
,
this formula allows us to bring linear estimates known in the flat case to the perturbed setting.

We recall first some linear dispersive estimates.

Lemma 2.5

(Linear dispersive estimates)
.

We have

\displaystyle\|e^{itL}f_{k}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle 2^{5k/2(1/p^{\prime}-1/p)}\rangle t^{-3(1/2-1/p)}\|f_{k}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{x}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|e^{itL}f_{k}\|_{L^{6}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle 2^{5k/3}\rangle t^{-1}\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}},

(2.15)

where
f_{k}:=P_{k}f.

Proof.

The first inequality is a consequence of the standard
L^{p}
decay estimate
for the Klein-Gordon propagator, and boundedness of wave operators on
W^{k,p}.

For the second, we have to show the analog in the flat case, which is slightly less classical than the first.
Following
[
25
]
, we write

\displaystyle\|e^{itL}f_{k}\|_{L^{6}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{itL}f_{k}\|_{L^{6,2}_{x}}\lesssim\langle 2^{5k/3}\rangle t^{-1}\|f_{k}\|_{L^{6/5,2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle 2^{5k/3}\rangle t^{-1}\||x|^{-1}\|_{L^{3,\infty}_{x}}\||x|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle 2^{5k/3}\rangle t^{-1}\|\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}f\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.

We used successively the following facts about Lorentz spaces:

•

L^{p,p}_{x}=L^{p}_{x}
for
1\leqslant p<\infty,a<b.

•

L^{p,a}_{x}\hookrightarrow L^{p,b}_{x}
for
1\leqslant p<\infty,a<b.

•

\|fg\|_{L^{p,a}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{q,b}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{r,c}_{x}}
where
1\leqslant p,q,r<\infty
and
1\leqslant a,b,c\leqslant\infty
satisfy
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r}
and
\frac{1}{a}=\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}.

∎

We also record Strichartz estimates.

Lemma 2.6

(Strichartz estimates)
.

Let
u
be a solution to the inhomogenenous equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+L^{2}u=F,\ \ u(0)=u_{0},\ \ \partial_{t}u(0)=u_{1}

Let
0\leqslant\gamma\leqslant 1,

2\leqslant q,\widetilde{q}\leqslant\infty,
and
2\leqslant r,\widetilde{r}<\infty
be exponents satisfying the scaling and admissibility conditions

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}+\frac{d}{r}=\frac{d}{2}-\gamma=\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}^{\prime}}+\frac{d}{\widetilde{r}^{\prime}}-2,\ \ and\ \ \frac{1}{q}+\frac{d-1}{2r},\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{d-1}{2\widetilde{r}}\leqslant\frac{d-1}{4}.

Then

\displaystyle\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\gamma}u\|_{C_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\gamma-1}\partial_{t}u\|_{C_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|u\|_{L^{q}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\gamma}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\gamma-1}u_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|F\|_{L^{\widetilde{q}^{\prime}}_{t}L^{\widetilde{r}^{\prime}}_{x}}.

Proof.

Follows from the analog in the flat case, see for example
[
26
]
, Lemma 2.1.
∎

Finally, we will also need the following local decay estimate.

Lemma 2.7
.

We have the bound, for
\phi,\zeta\in{\bf P_{c}}\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\phi\frac{e^{itL}}{L-2\lambda+i0}\zeta\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim t^{-3/2}\|\langle x\rangle^{4}L^{5}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\langle x\rangle^{4}L^{5}\phi\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

Note that the norms on the right-hand side of the above estimate are, of course,
far from optimal, but suffice for our purposes.

Proof.

Introducing a cut-off
\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)
where
2^{-C+10}\leqslant 2\lambda-1,

we split the above into a singular and a non-singular part.
Standard dispersive estimates allow us to write that, for the non-singular part,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\phi(1-\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(L-2\lambda))\frac{e^{itL}}{L-2\lambda+i0}\zeta\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\phi\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\bigg\|(1-\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(L-2\lambda))\frac{e^{itL}}{L-2\lambda+i0}\zeta\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-3/2}\bigg\|L^{3}\frac{\varphi_{>-C}(L-2\lambda)}{L-2\lambda}\zeta\bigg\|_{L^{1}_{x}}.

For the more challenging singular part, we introduce a regularization of the above term. By the limiting absorption principle, it is sufficient to obtain estimates that are uniform in
\eta
to conclude. We write, integrating by parts in
\xi
, that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(L-2\lambda)\frac{e^{itL}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}\zeta=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi(x,\xi)\frac{e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}}{\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda+i\eta}\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\widetilde{\zeta}(\xi)d\xi

\displaystyle=-ie^{2it\lambda}\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi(x,\xi)e^{i\tau(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)-\tau\eta}\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\widetilde{\zeta}(\xi)d\xi d\tau

\displaystyle=ie^{2it\lambda}\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{e^{i\tau(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)-\tau\eta}}{(i\tau)^{3}}\mathcal{L}^{3}\bigg(\psi(x,\xi)\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\widetilde{\zeta}(\xi)\bigg)d\xi d\tau,

where the operator
\mathcal{L}
is defined by its action on
\mathcal{S}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f:={\rm{div}}\bigg(\xi\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{|\xi|^{2}}f(\xi)\bigg).

From Lemma 3.2 in
[
41
]
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\xi}^{A}\psi(x,\xi)|\lesssim(|\xi|/\langle\xi\rangle)^{1-|A|}\langle x\rangle^{|A|},\qquad A\in\mathbb{N}.

Moreover

\displaystyle\Bigg|\nabla_{\xi}^{A}\bigg(\big(\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{|\xi|}\big)^{A}\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\bigg)\Bigg|\lesssim 1,

hence we can conclude that for any
\eta>0,

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\phi\frac{\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(L-2\lambda)}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}e^{itL}\zeta\Bigg\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim\|\langle x\rangle^{4}\phi\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\Bigg\|\langle x\rangle^{-4}\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(L-2\lambda)\frac{e^{itL}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}\zeta\Bigg\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-2}\|\langle x\rangle^{4}\zeta\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

The desired result follows.
∎

3.
Analysis for the discrete component

In this section we analyze the ODE for the amplitude of the discrete component, and extract its driving behavior.

3.1.
Set-up

The computations in this section are analogous to those in
[
48
]
.
Anticipating that the discrete component will oscillate at frequency
\lambda,
we start by writing that

\displaystyle a(t)=A(t)e^{i\lambda t}+\overline{A}(t)e^{-i\lambda t},\qquad A(t)=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-it\lambda}(\dot{a}+i\lambda a(t))

(3.1)

imposing that
A
satisfies

\displaystyle\dot{A}(t)e^{i\lambda t}+\dot{\overline{A}}(t)e^{-i\lambda t}=0.

(3.2)

This yields the following equation for the profile of the amplitude of the discrete component:

\displaystyle\dot{A}(t)=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}\big((a\phi+\frac{1}{L}\Im w)^{2},\phi\big).

(3.3)

To extract the leading order dynamics from (
3.3
)
we first need to look at the equation for
w
, and identify the leading order terms there.

Duhamel’s formula for the profile
f
of the radiation
w
, see (
1.17
), yields

\displaystyle w(t)

\displaystyle=e^{iLt}w_{0}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}\Big(a^{2}(s){\bf{P_{c}}}\phi^{2}\Big)ds

(3.4)

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{t}a(s)e^{i(t-s)L}{\bf{P_{c}}}\big(\frac{\phi}{L}\frac{w-\overline{w}}{2i}\big)ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}{\bf{P_{c}}}\bigg(\frac{\Im w}{L}\bigg)^{2}ds.

(3.5)

We further inspect (
3.4
). Denoting
\theta:={\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2},

\displaystyle\eqref{linear-res}=e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}A^{2}(s)\theta ds+e^{itL}R_{w},
