(3.6)

where

\displaystyle R_{w}:=2\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}\,|A|^{2}(s)\,ds\,\theta+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(L+2\lambda)}\,\overline{A}^{2}(s)\,ds\,\theta.

(3.7)

After introducing a regularization of the above term (
\eta>0
) and integrating by parts in time we obtain

\displaystyle\eqref{main-linear-res}=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big[i\frac{e^{2i\lambda t}e^{-\eta t}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}\,\theta\,A^{2}(t)-ie^{itL}\frac{A_{0}^{2}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}\theta

\displaystyle-ie^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{-is(L-2\lambda)}e^{-s\eta}}{L-2\lambda+i\eta}2A(s)\dot{A}(s)\theta ds\Big]+e^{itL}R_{w}.

We then use

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x\pm i0^{+}}=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{1}{x\pm i\eta}=\mathrm{p.v.}\frac{1}{x}\mp i\pi\delta,

and go back to (
3.3
), extract the dominant term in the right-hand side, and write

\displaystyle\dot{A}(t):=P+\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)|A|^{2}A+R,

(3.8)

\displaystyle P:=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}(Ae^{i\lambda t}+\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda t})^{2}\int\phi^{3},

where we define the real constants

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\theta;\frac{1}{L}\mathrm{p.v.}\frac{1}{L-2\lambda}\theta\Bigg),\qquad\Gamma:=\frac{\pi}{2\lambda}\Bigg(\theta;\delta(L-2\lambda)\theta\Bigg),

and the remainder term

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle:=R_{D}+R_{F},

(3.9)

with

\displaystyle R_{D}(t):=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\bigg(e^{2i\lambda t}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)A^{3}-(\Lambda+i\Gamma)\big(e^{-4i\lambda t}\overline{A}^{3}+e^{-2i\lambda t}|A|^{2}\overline{A}\big)\bigg),

(3.10)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{F}(t):=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}e^{-i\lambda t}&\Bigg[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\big(\frac{1}{L}\Im w\big)^{2}\phi\\
&+2a(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{2}\frac{1}{L}\,\Im\bigg(-ie^{itL}\frac{A_{0}^{2}}{L-2\lambda+i0^{+}}\theta\\
&-ie^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{-is(L-2\lambda+i0^{+})}}{L-2\lambda+i0^{+}}2A(s)\dot{A}(s)\,\theta\,ds\bigg)\\
&+2a(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{2}\,\frac{1}{L}\,\Im\big(e^{itL}R_{w}+e^{itL}w_{0}+\eqref{quadratic}\big)dx\Bigg].\end{split}

(3.11)

3.2.
Behavior of the modulus of the discrete component

In this subsection we show the expected behavior
|a(t)|\approx{\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon}^{2}t)^{-1/2}
.
The analysis differs from
[
48
]
or
[
2
]
in that more refined estimates are needed, including
some that we postpone to Section
5
where we will analyze more in details the
interactions that are quadratic in the radiation component. Indeed, unlike in
[
48
]
, a bootstrap argument does not provide enough decay, and we have to rely on new ideas.

We start by multiplying (
3.8
) by
2\overline{A}
and taking the real part of the resulting equation
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big(|A|^{2}\big)=2\Re(P\overline{A})-\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}|A|^{4}+2\Re(R\overline{A}).

(3.12)

Let us denote

\displaystyle\rho(t):=|A(t)|^{2},\qquad\rho_{0}:=\rho(0)\approx{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}.

(3.13)

Then we have

\displaystyle-\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{\rho^{2}}=-2\rho^{-2}\Re(P\overline{A})+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}-2\rho^{-2}\Re(R\overline{A}),

(3.14)

and after integrating, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho(t)}-\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}=\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t-2\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)\Re(P\overline{A}(s))+\rho^{-2}(s)\Re(R\overline{A}(s))ds.

(3.15)

We will prove that the last two terms on the right-hand side are perturbations of the first term.
In particular the behavior of
\rho
will be proven to be a perturbation of

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}\rho_{0}t},

(3.16)

which is the solution of the above equation if the perturbative terms are set to 0.

Here is the key bootstrap proposition that gives us control on the amplitude of the discrete component:

Proposition 3.1
.

With
\rho=|A|^{2}
, assume that for
T>0

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\bigg|\big(1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\big)\frac{\rho(t)}{\rho_{0}}-1\bigg|<\frac{1}{2},

(3.17)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\rho^{\frac{2\beta-1}{2}}(t)\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle^{-1}\langle t\rangle^{1-10\delta_{N}}\big\|w(t)\|_{L^{6}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\beta},\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|w(t)\|_{H^{N}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{1-\delta},\end{split}

(3.18)

for
N\gg 1
,
\delta_{N}
as in (
1.45
),
0<\beta\ll 1
, and
\delta
sufficiently small.
Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if
\varepsilon,\rho_{0}<\varepsilon_{0},
then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\bigg|\big(1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\big)\frac{\rho(t)}{\rho_{0}}-1\bigg|<\frac{1}{4}.

(3.19)

Remark 3.2
.

Note that the quantity in (
3.16
) is approximately
\rho_{0}=|A(0)|^{2}
when
t\lesssim 1/\rho_{0}
,
and it is
1/t
if
t\gtrsim 1/\rho_{0}
.

For simplicity, the reader is invited to think that time is large enough (past the ‘local’ time-scale
1/\rho_{0}
)
so that
\rho(t)\approx\langle t\rangle^{-1}
, and (
3.18
) implies that
for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle{\|w(t)\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1+\beta+10\delta_{N}}\varepsilon^{\beta}.

In particular, one should always think that the continuous/radiation component
is smaller (in the
L^{6}
norm) than the amplitude of the discrete component:

{\|w(t)\|}_{L^{6}}=o(\rho^{1/2}(t))
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the assumptions (
3.18
) are stated in terms of
w
rather than
in terms of
h
as is done in the main bootstrap in Proposition
1.10
.
However it is easy to see how (
3.18
) follow from (
1.47
).
For the
L^{6}
estimate the proof is identical to that of (
1.35
) in §
1.6.3
.
For the
H^{N}
estimate, recall that
w=e^{itL}f=e^{itL}g+e^{itL}h
;
then we can conclude in view of (
1.55
).

We start by proving some estimates on remainder terms
R_{F}
in (
3.11
); this will allow us to restrict our attention to terms involving only the discrete component in the equation
(
3.8
) for the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
We naturally have that a term is a remainder for the leading order cubic dynamics in (
3.8
)
essentially if it is
o(\rho^{3/2})
.
Correspondingly, when looking at (
3.15
) we aim to say that the lower order terms on the right-hand side
are
o(\rho^{-1})
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Under the assumptions (
3.17
) and (
3.18
),
there exists
1/2<a<1
such that we have for all
s\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\big|R_{F}(s)\big|\lesssim\rho^{3/2-a}(s)\langle s\rangle^{-1}.

(3.20)

Proof.

We may assume
s\geqslant 1
, since for
s<1
all estimates are simpler to establish.
Using the a priori estimate (
3.18
) we can bound the first term in (
3.11
) by

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\Big(\frac{\Im w(s)}{L}\Big)^{2}\phi\ dx

\displaystyle\lesssim{\|w(s)\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}^{2}\|\phi\|_{L^{3/2}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(s)^{2-2\beta}s^{20\delta_{N}}\varepsilon^{2\beta}.

For the second term in (
3.11
),
we use the local decay estimate from Lemma
2.7
:

\displaystyle|a(s)|\Big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{2}\,\frac{1}{L}\Re\Big(e^{isL}\frac{A_{0}^{2}}{L-2\lambda+i0^{+}}\theta\Big)\,dx\Big|\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\cdot\rho_{0}\,\langle s\rangle^{-3/2},

which is enough.

To estimate the third term in (
3.11
), we first observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\dot{A}(t)|&\lesssim|a^{2}(t)|+|a(t)|{\big\|\phi^{2}L^{-1}\Im w(t)\big\|}_{L^{1}}+{\big\|\phi\big(L^{-1}\Im w(t)\big)^{2}\big\|}_{L^{1}}\lesssim\rho(t),\end{split}

(3.21)

having used (
3.17
)-(
3.18
); see also Remark
3.2
.
Then, using the local decay estimate from Lemma
2.7
we write

\displaystyle|a(s)|\Big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{\phi^{2}}{L}\Big(e^{isL}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{e^{-i\tau(L-2\lambda)}}{L-2\lambda+i0^{+}}\dot{A}(\tau)A(\tau)d\tau\,\theta\Big)\,dx\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{0}^{s}\Bigg\|\phi\frac{e^{-i(s-\tau)(L-2\lambda)}e^{-\eta\tau}}{L-2\lambda+i0^{+}}\theta\Bigg\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\rho^{3/2}(\tau)d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{0}^{s}\langle s-\tau\rangle^{-3/2}\rho^{3/2}(\tau)\,d\tau\lesssim\rho^{2}(s).

The terms involving
R_{w}
are easier to estimate than those just treated since
we can integrate by parts in
s
in the formula (
3.7
) without introducing any singular factor of
L-2\lambda
;
this gives quadratic boundary terms which contribute
O(\rho^{3/2})
to
R_{F}
,
and cubic terms integrated in time which, upon using the linear estimate of Lemma
2.5
,
produce a contribution to
R_{F}
that is
O(\rho^{2})
.

For the term involving the initial data, we write

\displaystyle|a(t)|\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\phi^{2}\frac{1}{L}\Im\big(e^{itL}w_{0}\big)dx\bigg|\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(t)\|e^{itL}w_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(t)\langle t\rangle^{-3/2}\varepsilon_{0},

which is sufficient to conclude.

We move on to more difficult terms in
R_{F}
involving (
3.5
) and begin by estimating

\displaystyle|a(s)|\Big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{\phi^{2}}{L}\eqref{quadratic}dx\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\Bigg\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}a(\tau){\bf P_{c}}\big(\phi\frac{w-\overline{w}}{2iL}\big)d\tau\Bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\rho^{1/2}(s)\Bigg\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}{\bf P_{c}}\bigg(\frac{\Im w}{L}\bigg)^{2}d\tau\Bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

(3.22)

We may assume
s\geqslant 2
, as the case of
s<2
is easy to handle.
For the first term above, using the dispersive
L^{1}
-
L^{\infty}
estimates from Lemma
2.5

and Sobolev’s embedding, we find that

\displaystyle\rho^{1/2}(s)\Bigg\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}a(\tau){\bf P_{c}}\big(\phi\frac{w-\overline{w}}{2iL}\big)d\tau\Bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{0}^{1}\rho(\tau)^{1/2}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}{\big\|w(\tau)\big\|}_{H^{2}_{x}}d\tau

(3.23)

\displaystyle+\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{1}^{s-1}\rho(\tau)^{1/2}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}{\big\|L^{5/2}w(\tau)\big\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}d\tau

(3.24)

\displaystyle+\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{s-1}^{s}\rho(\tau)^{1/2}{\big\|L^{5/2}w(\tau)\big\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}d\tau,

(3.25)

where the implicit constant depends on
\|(1-\Delta)\phi\|_{L^{6/5}\cap L^{2}}
.
The term in (
3.23
) is bounded by

\rho^{1/2}(s)\cdot\rho_{0}^{1/2}\cdot\langle s\rangle^{-3/2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
,
which is sufficient.
For the remaining terms, we notice that by the Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev interpolation inequality
and the a priori bounds (
3.18
) we have for
\tau\geqslant 1

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|L^{5/2}w(\tau)\big\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}&\lesssim{\|w(\tau)\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}^{1-\alpha}{\|w(\tau)\|}_{H^{N}_{x}}^{\alpha}\\
&\lesssim\tau^{-1+\alpha}\tau^{10(1-\alpha)\delta_{N}}\rho(\tau)^{(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}\langle\tau\sqrt{\rho(\tau)}\rangle^{1-\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{\beta(1-\alpha)}\cdot{\varepsilon}^{\alpha(1-\delta)},\end{split}

(3.26)

with
\alpha=5/(2N-4)
.
Then, taking
N
large enough to make
\alpha
sufficiently small,
and plugging this into (
3.25
) we find that it is bounded by

\displaystyle\rho^{1/2}(s)\cdot\rho^{1/2}(s)\cdot s^{-1+\alpha+10(1-\alpha)\delta_{N}}\cdot\langle s\sqrt{\rho(s)}\rangle^{1-\alpha}\cdot\rho(s)^{(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)},

which yields the desired bound by the right-hand side of (
3.4
).

Next, we use inequality (
3.26
) in (
3.24
);
we estimate the integral in three parts, assuming that
s\geqslant\rho_{0}^{-1/2}
and leave the
easier case
s<\rho_{0}^{-1/2}
to the reader.
First, we have

\displaystyle\rho^{1/2}(s)

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\rho_{0}^{-1/2}}\rho(\tau)^{1/2+(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}\langle\tau\rangle^{-1+\alpha+10\delta_{N}}d\tau\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\rho_{0}^{1-\beta}\int_{1}^{\rho_{0}^{-1/2}}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}\tau^{-1+\alpha+10\delta_{N}}d\tau\,\varepsilon^{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\rho_{0}^{1-\beta}\cdot s^{-3/2}\cdot\rho_{0}^{-5\delta_{N}-\alpha/2}\varepsilon^{\beta}.

Second, we can also estimate

\displaystyle\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{\rho_{0}^{-1/2}}^{s/2}\rho(\tau)^{1/2}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}\tau^{-1+\alpha}\cdot\tau^{10\delta_{N}}\rho(\tau)^{(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}\tau^{1-\alpha}\rho(\tau)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}d\tau\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-3/2}\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{\rho_{0}^{-1/2}}^{s/2}\rho(\tau)^{1/2+(1-\beta)(1-\alpha)}\tau^{10\delta_{N}}d\tau\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-3/2}\rho(s)^{1/2}\int_{\rho_{0}^{1/2}}^{s\rho_{0}/2}\bigg(\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\tau}\bigg)^{1/2+(1-\beta)(1-\alpha)}\rho_{0}^{-10\delta_{N}}\tau^{10\delta_{N}}\frac{d\tau}{\rho_{0}}\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-3/2}\rho(s)^{1/2}\rho_{0}^{-1/2+(1-\beta)(1-\alpha)-10\delta_{N}}\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)},

which is sufficient to conclude, provided
\alpha,\beta
and
\delta_{N}
are small enough.
For the remaining piece the bound reads

\displaystyle\rho^{1/2}(s)\int_{s/2}^{s-1}\rho(\tau)^{1/2}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}\tau^{-1+\alpha}\cdot\tau^{10\delta_{N}}\tau^{1-\alpha}\rho(\tau)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}\cdot\rho(\tau)^{(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}d\tau\,{\varepsilon}^{\beta+\alpha(1-\delta-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(s)\rho(s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}+(1/2-\beta)(1-\alpha)}s^{10\delta_{N}}\int_{s/2}^{s-1}|s-\tau|^{-3/2}d\tau.

This is consistent with the desired (
3.20
).

We are then left with the quadratic term in
w
in (
3.22
). We observe that

\displaystyle|a(s)|\Bigg|\int\frac{\phi^{2}}{L}\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}{\bf P_{c}}\big(\frac{\Im w}{L}\big)^{2}d\tau dx\bigg|\lesssim\rho(s)^{1/2}\bigg\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}{\bf P_{c}}\big(\frac{\Im w}{L}\big)^{2}d\tau\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

Therefore, the desired bound will follow from proving that there exists
\frac{1}{2}<a<1
such that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{0}^{s}e^{i(s-\tau)L}{\bf P_{c}}\big(\frac{\Im w}{L}\big)^{2}d\tau\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho(s)^{1-a}}{\langle s\rangle}.

(3.27)

This estimate will be a consequence of the analysis carried out in Section
5
,
and is postponed until that section.
∎

We move on to the proof the main result of this section:

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We proceed in three steps.

Step 1: Acceptable growth rate
.
The result will follow from showing that

\displaystyle\Big|\Re\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)(P(s)\overline{A}(s))+\rho^{-2}(s)(R(s)\overline{A}(s))ds\Big|\lesssim\rho^{-a}(t),

(3.28)

where
\frac{1}{2}<a<1
and the implicit constant only depends on
\phi.

Indeed, assume this result is proved. Then, we have,
for some constant
C_{1}>0
independent of
\rho_{0},
using (
3.15
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}-C_{1}\rho_{0}^{-a}\Big(\displaystyle 1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t\Big)^{a}-C_{1}+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\leqslant\frac{1}{\rho(t)}\leqslant\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}+C_{1}\rho_{0}^{-a}\Big(\displaystyle 1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t\Big)^{a}+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t+C_{1},

and we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{2\lambda}t+C_{1}\rho_{0}^{1-a}\big(\displaystyle 1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t\big)^{a}+C_{1}\rho_{0}}\leqslant\rho(t)\leqslant\frac{\rho_{0}}{1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t-C_{1}\rho_{0}^{1-a}\big(\displaystyle 1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t\big)^{a}-C_{1}\rho_{0}}.

The desired result (
3.19
) follows provided
\rho_{0}
is small enough.

Step 2: Lower order terms
.
We treat the easier terms on the right-hand side of (
3.28
),
which are the ones involving
R
, see (
3.9
).
Lemma
3.4
directly gives acceptable bounds for the
R_{F}
part. For the discrete part,
\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)|\Re(\overline{A}(s)R_{D}(s))|ds

can be written as a sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{i\alpha\lambda s}\frac{A_{+}^{i}A_{-}^{j}}{\rho^{3}(s)}ds,\qquad i+j=6,\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},\quad A_{+}:=A,\quad A_{-}:=\overline{A}.

(3.29)

Therefore it is sufficient to show how to deal with such oscillatory integrals.
Integrating by parts, with
\widetilde{A}
denoting either
A_{+}
or
A_{-}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{i\alpha\lambda s}\frac{\widetilde{A}^{6}(s)}{\rho^{3}(s)}ds=\frac{e^{i\alpha\lambda t}}{i\alpha\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{A}^{6}(t)}{\rho(t)^{3}}-\frac{1}{i\alpha\lambda}\frac{\widetilde{A}^{6}(0)}{\rho^{3}(0)}-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{e^{i\alpha\lambda s}}{i\alpha\lambda}\partial_{s}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{A}^{6}}{\rho^{3}}\bigg)ds.

Bounding the remainder, using
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}=O(\rho)
(from (
3.21
)),
we see that the integrand is
O(\rho^{1/2}).

We can conclude since, using (
3.17
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{1/2}(s)ds

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\Gamma\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\sqrt{1+\frac{\Gamma\rho_{0}}{\lambda}t}\lesssim\rho^{-1/2}(t).

(3.30)

Step 3: Normal forms
.
From (
3.8
) we see that
the leading order terms on the right-hand side of (
3.28
) are of the form

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{is\alpha\lambda}A_{+}^{i}(s)A_{-}^{j}(s)\rho(s)^{-3}ds,\qquad i+j=5,\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},\quad A_{+}:=A,\quad A_{-}:=\overline{A},

(3.31)

and a crude bound gives a growth at the rate
t^{3/2},
which is not acceptable.
To conclude the proof we show that these terms
can be written in the form of integrals treated in Step 2.

We use the definition of
P
in (
3.8
), to see that

\displaystyle 2\Re(P\overline{A})

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{0}}{2i\lambda}\big(A^{3}e^{3i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{3}e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)-\frac{c_{0}}{2i\lambda}|A|^{2}\big(Ae^{i\lambda s}-\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}\big),\qquad c_{0}:=\int\phi^{3},

(3.32)

and write

\displaystyle-2\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}\Re(P\overline{A})ds=c_{0}\big(T_{1}+T_{2}\big),

\displaystyle T_{1}:=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)\big(A^{3}e^{3i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{3}e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)ds,\qquad T_{2}:=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-1}(s)\big(Ae^{i\lambda s}-\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}\big)ds.

Both terms can be dealt with in the same way.
Integrating by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle T_{2}=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\Big[\rho^{-1}\frac{Ae^{i\lambda s}}{i\lambda}+\rho^{-1}\frac{\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}}{i\lambda}\Big]_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}(\rho^{-1})\Big(\frac{Ae^{i\lambda s}}{i\lambda}+\frac{\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}}{i\lambda}\Big)ds

(3.33)

where we used (
3.2
).
The boundary terms satisfy the required bound.
For the other term in (
3.33
) we use (
3.14
) and (
3.32
) to calculate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}(\rho^{-1})\big(Ae^{i\lambda s}+\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}\big)ds

\displaystyle=\frac{-ic_{0}}{4\lambda^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)\big(Ae^{i\lambda s}+\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s}\big)\big(A^{3}e^{3i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{3}e^{-3i\lambda s}+\rho^{-1}(s)(Ae^{i\lambda s}-\overline{A}e^{-i\lambda s})\big)ds+L_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-ic_{0}}{4\lambda^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}2\rho^{-1}(s)\big(A^{2}e^{2i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{2}e^{-2i\lambda s}\big)+\rho^{-2}(s)\big(A^{4}e^{4i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{4}e^{-4i\lambda s}\big)ds+L_{1}(t),

where

\displaystyle L_{1}(t):=\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)a(s)\bigg(\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}\rho^{2}(s)-\Re(R\overline{A}(s))\bigg)ds.

All the integrals above are of the form (
3.29
) as desired
(note that there is a key cancellation, which makes the end result of the acceptable form with non-vanishing oscillations),
and
L_{1}
is a lower order term satisfying an acceptable bound, also in view of Step 2.

We can treat
T_{1}
similarly to
T_{2}
. Integrating by parts we get

\displaystyle 2i\lambda T_{1}

\displaystyle=B-T_{21}-T_{22},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{3i\lambda\rho^{2}(s)}\big(A^{3}(s)e^{3i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{3}e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)\Big|_{0}^{t},

\displaystyle T_{21}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{3i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\big(\rho^{-2}(s)\big)\big(A^{3}(s)e^{3i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{3}e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)ds,

\displaystyle T_{22}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)\big(A^{\prime}(s)A^{2}(s)e^{3i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{\prime}(s)\overline{A}^{2}(s)e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)ds.

Then, using (
3.14
), (
3.32
), and (
3.8
), we get
(notice again a key cancellation of the terms without oscillations)

\displaystyle T_{21}

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{0}}{3\lambda^{2}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-3}(s)\big(A^{6}(s)e^{6i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{6}e^{-6i\lambda s}\big)+\rho^{-2}(s)\big(A^{4}(s)e^{4i\lambda s}-\overline{A}^{4}e^{-4i\lambda s}\big)

\displaystyle+\rho^{-1}(s)\big(-A^{2}(s)e^{2i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{2}e^{-2i\lambda s}\big)ds\Bigg]+L_{2}(t),

\displaystyle T_{22}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{0}}{2\lambda^{2}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)\big(A^{4}(s)e^{4i\lambda s}+e^{-4i\lambda s}\overline{A}^{4}(s)\big)+2\rho^{-1}(s)\big(A^{2}(s)e^{2i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{2}(s)e^{-2i\lambda s}\big)ds\Bigg]

\displaystyle+L_{3}(t),

where

\displaystyle L_{2}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2}{3i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-3}(s)\big(A^{3}(s)e^{3i\lambda s}+\overline{A}^{3}(s)e^{-3i\lambda s}\big)\bigg(-\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}\rho^{2}(s)+2\Re(R\overline{A}(s))\bigg)ds,

\displaystyle L_{3}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\rho^{-2}(s)2\Re\Big[A^{2}(s)e^{3i\lambda s}\Big(\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)|A|^{2}A+R\Big)\Big]ds.

The integrals in
T_{21}
and
T_{22}
are of the acceptable form (
3.29
).
The integrands in the terms
L_{2}(t)
and
L_{3}(t)
that involve the part of
R

coming from
R_{D}=O(\rho^{3/2})
are
O(\rho^{1/2})
, so we can conclude given (
3.30
).
The parts involving
R_{F}
can be seen to satisfy an acceptable estimate by the right-hand side of
(
3.28
) using the estimate (
3.20
).
∎

3.3.
Renormalized equation

To simplify the dispersive analysis, we renormalize the equation for the amplitude

A
by introducing a new variable which varies more slowly than
A
.
We will denote by
B
this renormalized profile of the amplitude of the internal mode.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let

\displaystyle B=F(A):=A+c_{0}\Big(\frac{e^{i\lambda t}}{2\lambda^{2}}A^{2}-\frac{e^{-3i\lambda t}}{6\lambda^{2}}\overline{A}^{2}-\frac{e^{-i\lambda t}}{2\lambda^{2}}|A|^{2}\Big),\qquad c_{0}:=\int\phi^{3}.

(3.34)

Then

(i)

F(A)/A\rightarrow 1
as
|A|\to 0
;

(ii)

|(d/dt)F(A)|\lesssim\rho(t)^{3/2}+\rho(t)^{3/2-a}\langle t\rangle^{-1},

where
\frac{1}{2}<a<1
is as in Lemma
3.4
.

Proof.

The first property in the statement clearly holds true by definition.
Integrating (
3.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(t)-A_{0}&=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(e^{i\lambda s}A^{2}+e^{-3i\lambda s}\overline{A}^{2}+2e^{-i\lambda s}|A|^{2}\bigg)ds\int\phi^{3}\\
&+\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)\int_{0}^{t}|A|^{2}Ads+\int_{0}^{t}Rds\\
&=:\bigg[-\frac{e^{i\lambda s}}{2\lambda^{2}}A^{2}+\frac{e^{-3i\lambda s}}{6\lambda^{2}}\overline{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\lambda s}|A|^{2}(s)\bigg]_{0}^{t}\,\int\phi^{3}+\int_{0}^{t}R_{1}(s)ds,\end{split}

(3.35)

that is,
F(A)-F(A_{0})=\int_{0}^{t}R_{1}(s)ds
,
with

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{1}(s):=\Big(\frac{e^{i\lambda s}}{2\lambda^{2}}\frac{d}{ds}A^{2}(s)-\frac{e^{-3i\lambda s}}{6\lambda^{2}}\frac{d}{ds}\overline{A}^{2}(s)-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\lambda s}\frac{d}{ds}|A|^{2}(s)\Big)\int\phi^{3}\\
+\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)|A|^{2}A+R(s).\end{split}

(3.36)

We can then verify (ii) noticing that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}F(A)=R_{1}=R_{F}+O\big(|A||\dot{A}|\big)+O(|A|^{3}),

where
R_{F}
is defined in (
3.11
), and
O(|A|^{3})
also includes
R_{D}
in (
3.10
).
The a priori assumption
|A|\lesssim\rho^{1/2}
, the estimate (
3.21
) and Lemma
3.4
give the conclusion.
∎

3.4.
Asymptotics for the internal mode

We now obtain asymptotics for
A
, and then use this to get asymptotics for
parts of
w
as well. We proceed in a few steps.

Equation for
B
.
First, let us derive an equation for the renormalized amplitude
B
as defined in (
3.34
).
In view of (
3.35
)-(
3.36
), and (
3.9
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{B}(s)=c_{0}\Big(\frac{e^{i\lambda s}}{\lambda^{2}}A(s)\frac{d}{ds}A(s)-\frac{e^{-3i\lambda s}}{3\lambda^{2}}\overline{A}(s)\frac{d}{ds}\overline{A}(s)-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\lambda s}\Re\big(\overline{A}(s)\frac{d}{ds}A(s)\big)\Big)\\
+\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)|A|^{2}A+R_{D}(s)+R_{F}(s).\end{split}

(3.37)

Using the equation (
3.8
) we write the above identity more explicitly
by separating the terms that are cubic in
A
from those that are faster decaying:

\displaystyle\dot{B}

\displaystyle=C(A)+R_{2},

(3.38)

where (recall also (
3.10
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}C(A)&:=c_{0}\Big(\frac{e^{i\lambda s}}{\lambda^{2}}A(s)P(s)-\frac{e^{-3i\lambda s}}{3\lambda^{2}}\overline{A}(s)\overline{P}(s)-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\lambda s}\Re\big(\overline{A}(s)P(s)\big)\Big)\\
&+\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)|A|^{2}(s)A(s)+R_{D}(s)\end{split}

(3.39)

and
R_{2}
is defined via (
3.37
)-(
3.39
) and (
3.8
)-(
3.11
) and satisfies

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle=c_{0}\Big(\frac{e^{i\lambda s}}{\lambda^{2}}A(s)R_{F}-\frac{e^{-3i\lambda s}}{3\lambda^{2}}\overline{A}(s)\overline{R_{F}}(s)-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\lambda s}\Re\big(\overline{A}(s)R_{F}\big)\Big)+R_{F}(s)+O(|A|^{4}).

In particular the
O(|A|^{4})
appearing above has an explicit expression that can be calculated, but
its exact form is unimportant. We will use such a notation for various other remainder terms in the
rest of the argument below.
In view of (
3.20
), we have

\displaystyle R_{2}(s)=O(\rho^{3/2-a}(s)\langle s\rangle^{-1}),\qquad 1/2<a<1.

(3.40)

In what follows, we will consider the right-hand side of (
3.40
) as an acceptable remainder.

Cubic Normal Form and renormalization
.
Let us analyze more closely the cubic terms in (
3.39
).
Recall that, see (
3.8
)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{0}}{2i\lambda}\big(e^{i\lambda t}A^{2}+2e^{-i\lambda t}|A|^{2}+e^{-3i\lambda t}\overline{A}^{2}\big),

so that, gathering terms with the same oscillation we can write

\displaystyle C(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}}c_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}A_{{\epsilon}_{2}}A_{{\epsilon}_{3}}e^{i(-1+{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{3})t},\qquad A_{+}:=A,\quad A_{-}:=\overline{A}

(3.41)

where
c_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}\in{\mathbb{C}}
are certain constants.
We now write

\displaystyle\begin{split}C(A)&=c_{1}|A|^{2}A+\sum_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}\neq 1}c_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}}A_{\epsilon_{1}}A_{\epsilon_{2}}A_{\epsilon_{3}}e^{i(-1+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3})t},\\
c_{1}&:=\sum_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}=1}c_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}}=\frac{1}{2i\lambda}(\Lambda-i\Gamma)+\frac{8}{3}\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{i\lambda^{3}}.\end{split}

(3.42)

We then eliminate all the cubic non-resonant terms from the right-hand side of (
3.39
),
that is, all oscillating terms in (
3.41
).
We let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&W:=B-N(A),\\
&N(A)(t):=\sum_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}\neq 1}\frac{c_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}}{i(-1+{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{3})}A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}A_{{\epsilon}_{2}}A_{{\epsilon}_{3}}e^{i(-1+{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{3})t},\end{split}

(3.43)

so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{W}&=c_{1}|A|^{2}A+R_{2}-\sum_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}\neq 1}\frac{c_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}}}{i(-1+{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{3})}\frac{d}{dt}\big(A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}A_{{\epsilon}_{2}}A_{{\epsilon}_{3}}\big)e^{i(-1+{\epsilon}_{1}+{\epsilon}_{2}+{\epsilon}_{3})t}\\
&=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\lambda}|W|^{2}W+ic_{2}|W|^{2}W+R_{2}+O(|A|^{4}),\qquad c_{2}:=-\frac{\Lambda}{2\lambda}-\frac{8}{3}\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{\lambda^{3}},\end{split}

(3.44)

having used
B-A=O(A^{2})
,
W-B=O(A^{3})
,
\dot{A}=O(A^{2})
, and (
3.42
).

We then gauge away the ‘rotating’ terms
ic_{2}|W|^{2}W
by setting

\displaystyle Z(t):=W(t)\exp\Big(-ic_{2}\int_{0}^{t}|W|^{2}(s)\,ds\Big).

(3.45)

Notice

\displaystyle|Z|=|W|=O(\rho^{1/2}),\qquad|W-B|=O(\rho^{3/2}),\qquad|B-A|=O(\rho).

(3.46)

We then have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{Z}=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\lambda}|Z|^{2}Z+R_{2}^{\prime}+O(|Z|^{4}),\qquad R_{2}^{\prime}:=\exp\Big(-ic_{2}\int_{0}^{t}|W|^{2}(s)\,ds\Big)R_{2},\end{split}

which we rewrite as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{Z}=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\lambda}|Z|^{2}Z+O(\rho^{3/2-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}).\end{split}

(3.47)

Asymptotic equations
.
To find a limit equation for
Z
we let

\displaystyle Z(t)=Y(t)e^{i\vartheta(t)},\qquad Y,\vartheta\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.48)

Observe that (
3.46
) gives
Y=O(\rho^{1/2})

(recall that
\rho(t):=|A|^{2}
) and
\big|Y-\rho^{1/2}|=O(\rho)
;
in particular, we may assume that
Y_{0}:=Y(0)>0
.
Taking real and imaginary parts in (
3.47
), we find

\displaystyle\dot{Y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\lambda}Y^{3}+O(\rho^{3/2-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}),

(3.49)

\displaystyle\dot{\vartheta}

\displaystyle=O(\rho^{1-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}).

(3.50)

The data for these equations can be found from the initial data for the amplitude
(a(0),\dot{a}(0))

(see (
3.1
)) and the relations (
3.34
), (
3.43
) and (
3.45
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y_{0}:=Y(0)&=\big(F(A(0))-N(A(0))\big)e^{-i\vartheta(0)},\\
\vartheta_{0}:=\vartheta(0)&=\mathrm{arg}\big(F(A(0))-N(A(0))\big).\end{split}

(3.51)

From (
3.49
) we can write

\displaystyle-2\frac{\dot{Y}}{Y^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}+O(\rho^{-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}),

and integrating this we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{Y(t)}{y(t)}=\Big(1+y^{2}(t)\int_{0}^{t}O\big(\rho(s)^{-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}\big)ds\Big)^{-1/2},\qquad y(t):=\frac{Y_{0}}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}tY^{2}_{0}}}.

(3.52)

Notice that

\displaystyle y(t)^{2}\int_{0}^{t}O\big(\rho(s)^{-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}\big)ds=O(\rho^{1-a}(t))\,|\log{\varepsilon}|,

and, therefore,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{Y(t)}{y(t)}-1\Big|=O\big(\rho^{1-a}(t)|\log{\varepsilon}|\big).

(3.53)

Next, since

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\rho(s)^{1-a}\langle s\rangle^{-1}ds=O\big(\rho(t_{2})^{1-a}\,|\log{\varepsilon}|\big)

we see from (
3.50
) that we can define a limit

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\infty}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\dot{\vartheta}(s)ds,\qquad\vartheta_{\infty}=O({\varepsilon}^{2(1-a)}|\log{\varepsilon}|),

(3.54)

and that we have

\displaystyle|\vartheta(t)-\vartheta_{\infty}|=O\big(\rho(t)^{1-a}|\log{\varepsilon}|\big).

(3.55)

We now go back to the complex variables
Z
and
W
.
From (
3.48
) and the asymptotics (
3.53
) and (
3.55
) it follows that, recall (
3.52
),

\displaystyle\qquad\big|Z(t)-y(t)e^{i\vartheta_{\infty}}\big|=O\big(\rho^{3/2-a}(t)|\log{\varepsilon}|\big).

(3.56)

This implies

\displaystyle\big||W(t)|^{2}-y(t)^{2}\big|=O\big(\rho^{2-a}(t)|\log{\varepsilon}|\big)

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\big||W(s)|^{2}-y(s)^{2}\big|\,ds=O\big(\rho^{1-a}(t_{1})|\log{\varepsilon}|\big)

so that we can extract a limit

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi_{\infty}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(|W(s)|^{2}-y(s)^{2}\big)\,ds,\qquad\psi_{\infty}=O({\varepsilon}^{2(1-a)}|\log{\varepsilon}|),\end{split}

(3.57)

and

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\big(|W(s)|^{2}-y(s)^{2}\big)\,ds-\psi_{\infty}\Big|\lesssim\rho(t)^{1-a}|\log{\varepsilon}|\lesssim\frac{{\varepsilon}^{\delta}}{\langle t\rangle^{\delta}},

(3.58)

for some small
\delta\in(0,1-a)
.
In particular, calculating the integral of
y^{2}
(see (
3.52
)) we see that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|W(s)|^{2}ds=\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\Big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}tY_{0}^{2}\Big)+\psi_{\infty}+O\big({\varepsilon}^{\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}\big).

(3.59)

With (
3.54
) and (
3.57
) we define the total (constant) phase shift by

\displaystyle\Psi_{\infty}=\vartheta_{\infty}+\psi_{\infty}.

(3.60)

Finally, putting together (
3.46
) (which gives us
|A-W|=O(\rho)
),
and the relation between
W
and
Z
in (
3.45
) with the asymptotics for
Z
in (
3.56
)
and for the nonlinear phase in (
3.59
) (see also (
3.60
)),
we can conclude that

\displaystyle\Big|A(t)-y(t)\exp\Big(i\Psi_{\infty}+i\frac{c_{2}\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\Big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}t\Big)\Big)\Big|=O\Big(\rho^{1/2}(t){\varepsilon}^{\delta}\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}\Big),

(3.61)

for some small
\delta\in(0,1-a)
,
where we recall that
y
is given by (
3.52
) with (
3.51
),
and
c_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is defined in (
3.44
).

3.5.
Asymptotic behavior of the Fermi component

In this last subsection we derive asymptotics in
L^{\infty}_{\xi}

for the bad part of the continuous component of the solution,
that is,
g
as defined in (
1.44
). We then prove the growth of the weighted norm (
1.24
) and hence (
1.33
).

3.5.1.
Pointwise asymptotics for
\widetilde{g}

In view of the definition (
1.44
), (
3.61
) and (
3.46
),
we define

\displaystyle G(t,\xi):=-\int_{0}^{t}\exp\Big(is(2\lambda-\langle\xi\rangle)+i\frac{2c_{2}\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\Big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}s\Big)\Big)y(s)^{2}ds\,e^{2i\Psi_{\infty}}\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi).

(3.62)

Then, we see that, for
t_{1}<t_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|\widetilde{g}(t_{1},\xi)-G(t_{1},\xi)-\big(\widetilde{g}(t_{2},\xi)-G(t_{2},\xi)\big)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\delta}t_{1}^{-\delta}.\end{split}

(3.63)

In particular
\widetilde{g}-G
is Cauchy in time and there exists
g_{\infty}\in{\varepsilon}^{\delta}L^{\infty}_{\xi}
such that

\displaystyle{\|\widetilde{g}(t,\xi)-G(t,\xi)-g_{\infty}\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\delta}t^{-\delta},\quad t\geqslant 1.

or, more explicitly, for all
\xi
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{g}(t,\xi)=-\int_{0}^{t}\exp\Big(is(2\lambda-\langle\xi\rangle)+i\frac{2c_{2}\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\Big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}s\Big)\Big)y(s)^{2}ds\,e^{2i\Psi_{\infty}}\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)\\
+g_{\infty}(\xi)+O_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}({\varepsilon}^{\delta})\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}.\end{split}

(3.64)

3.5.2.
Growth of the weighted norm

We now want to prove that the bad component
g
as defined in (
4.5
) has a fast
growing weighted norm, and the following lower bound holds:

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(t)\|}_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\gtrsim\sqrt{t},\qquad t\geqslant C{\varepsilon}^{-2}.

(3.65)

for some large constant
C
, so that
t\geqslant\lambda/(\Gamma Y_{0}^{2})
.

Recall first that, using (
3.34
),
B
satisfies the same asymptotics of
A
in (
3.61
) with (
3.52
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big|B(t)-B_{\infty}(t)\big|\lesssim\rho^{1/2}(t)\frac{{\varepsilon}^{\delta}}{\langle t\rangle^{\delta}},\\
&B_{\infty}(t):=y(t)\exp\Big(i\Psi_{\infty}+i\frac{c_{2}\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\Big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}Y_{0}^{2}t\Big)\Big).\end{split}

(3.66)

Let
\ell
be such that
2^{\ell}=c\langle t\rangle^{-1}
with
c<\Gamma/\lambda
sufficiently small.
Differentiating the formula (
4.5
) we then write,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}=G_{1,\ell}+G_{2,\ell}+G_{3,\ell},\\
&G_{1,\ell}(t,\xi):=\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\frac{\xi}{i\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}\,s\,B_{\infty}^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}ds\,\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi),\\
&G_{2,\ell}(t,\xi):=\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\int_{0}^{t}\,B^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}ds\,\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi),\\
&G_{3,\ell}(t,\xi):=\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\frac{\xi}{i\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}\,s\,\big(B^{2}(s)-B^{2}_{\infty}(s)\big)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}ds\,\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi).\end{split}

(3.67)

We first notice that last two terms above satisfy the estimates

\displaystyle{\|G_{2,\ell}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\,|B(s)|^{2}ds\,{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\log(1+t)

and, using (
3.66
),

\displaystyle{\|G_{3,\ell}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\ell/2}\int_{0}^{t}\,s\rho(s)\frac{{\varepsilon}^{\delta}}{\langle s\rangle^{\delta}}ds\,{\|\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi)\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\langle t\rangle^{1/2-\delta}.

Therefore, using also
L^{2}
-orthogonality, to show main bound (
3.65
) it will suffice to prove

\displaystyle{\|G_{1,\ell}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\langle t\rangle^{1/2}.

(3.68)

Proof of (
3.68
).

Using the explicit expression in (
3.66
),
and
\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi_{0})\neq 0,|\xi_{0}|=\sqrt{4\lambda^{2}-1}
by the FGR (
1.26
), we write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big|G_{1,\ell}(t,\xi)\Big|\\
&\gtrsim\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\,\exp\Big(-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)+ic_{2}\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}sY^{2}_{0}\big)\Big)\,\frac{sY_{0}^{2}}{1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}sY^{2}_{0}}ds\Big|\\
&\gtrsim\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\,\exp\Big(-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)+ic_{2}\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma}\log\big(1+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}sY^{2}_{0}\big)\Big)ds\Big|\\
&-\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)O(\log t).\end{split}

(3.69)

From now on we disregard the lower order
O(\log t)
term above.
We then change variables to
u:=Y_{0}^{-2}+\tfrac{\Gamma}{\lambda}s
and denote

\displaystyle X=X(\xi)=-(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma},\qquad c_{3}:=c_{2}\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma},\qquad T:=Y_{0}^{-2}+\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t,

so that

\displaystyle\Big|G_{1,\ell}(t,\xi)\Big|\gtrsim\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\Big|\int_{Y_{0}^{-2}}^{T}\,\exp\Big(iuX(\xi)+ic_{3}\log u\Big)du\Big|

(3.70)

where the implicit constant is independent of
Y_{0}=O({\varepsilon})
.
Changing variable to
v=uX(\xi)
we get

\displaystyle\Big|G_{1,\ell}(t,\xi)\Big|\gtrsim\varphi_{\ell}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\,2^{-\ell}\,\Big|\int_{Y_{0}^{-2}X}^{TX}\,\exp\big(iv+ic_{3}\log v\big)dv\Big|.

(3.71)

Finally we look at the integral in (
3.71
) and write,
for
X\langle t\rangle\approx XT\in[c/2,2c]
, for small
c
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{Y_{0}^{-2}X}^{TX}\,\exp\Big(iv+ic_{3}\log v\Big)dv&=\int_{Y_{0}^{-2}X}^{TX}\,e^{ic_{3}\log v}dv+\int_{Y_{0}^{-2}X}^{TX}\,\big[e^{iv}-1\big]e^{ic_{3}\log v}dv\\
&\gtrsim TX+O(TX)^{2}\end{split}

(3.72)

where the (real and imaginary parts of the) first of the two integrals
above can be explicitly calculated with the substitution
\log v=w
for example.
Therefore

\displaystyle{\big\|G_{1,\ell}(t,\xi)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\gtrsim 2^{-\ell/2}\,TX\approx\langle t\rangle^{1/2}

(3.73)

as claimed.
∎

4.
Dispersive analysis setup

In this section we set up the analysis for the PDE in (
1.6
).
In Subsection
4.1
we first write out Duhamel’s formula for the profile
f=e^{-itL}w
on the distorted Fourier side.
We then define a new variable
h
by subtracting the most singular contribution, which we call
g
, from
f
.
Lemma
4.1
rearranges all the terms according to the nature of the interactions
and the way they will be treated in the rest of the paper.
In
4.2
we explain our main bootstrap argument for
h
.
We then also give some simple consequences of our bootstrap assumptions
in Lemmas
4.4
and
4.5
.

4.1.
Duhamel formula

We start by writing Duhamel’s formula in Fourier variables and in terms of the profile
f(t):=e^{-itL}w(t).

For any function
a
, let us denote

\displaystyle a_{+}=a,\qquad a_{-}=\bar{a}.

With
\psi
the generalized eigenfunctions from (
2.1
), let us define

\displaystyle\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{9/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\overline{\psi(x,\xi)}\psi(x,\eta)\psi(x,\sigma)dx,

(4.1)

\displaystyle\nu(\xi,\eta):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\overline{\psi(x,\xi)}\psi(x,\eta)\phi(x)dx.

(4.2)

Using the inversion formula from Proposition
2.1
, and the definitions (
4.1
)-(
4.2
),
we write Duhamel’s formula (
1.17
) in terms of
\widetilde{f}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{f}(t)&=\widetilde{f_{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}a^{2}(s)\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)ds\\
&-\frac{i}{2}\int_{0}^{t}a(s)e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\bigg(\frac{e^{is\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)}{\langle\eta\rangle}-\frac{e^{-is\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{\overline{f}}(s,\eta)}{\langle\eta\rangle}\bigg)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds\\
&-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{\big(\widetilde{w}(\eta)-\widetilde{\overline{w}}(\eta)\big)\big(\widetilde{w}(\sigma)-\widetilde{\overline{w}}(\sigma)\big)}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma\,ds.\end{split}

(4.3)

We renormalize the
a
terms via
a(t)=A(t)e^{i\lambda t}+\overline{A}(t)e^{-i\lambda t}
,
see (
1.7
), and keeping in mind (
3.34
), and write (
4.3
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{f}(t)&=\widetilde{f_{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}B^{2}(s)\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)ds\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}(A^{2}(s)-B^{2}(s))\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)ds\\
&+2\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}|A|^{2}(s)\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle+2\lambda)}\overline{A}^{2}(s)\widetilde{{\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}}(\xi)ds\\
&-\frac{i}{2}\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle-\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds\\
&-\frac{i}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(A(s)-B(s))\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle-\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta ds\\
&-\frac{i}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\overline{A}(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle+\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds\\
&+\frac{i}{2}\int_{0}^{t}a(s)e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{e^{-is\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{\overline{f}}(s,\eta)}{\langle\eta\rangle}\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds\\
&-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{\big(e^{is\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{f}(\eta)-e^{-is\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{\overline{f}}(\eta)\big)\big(e^{is\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{f}(\sigma)-e^{-is\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{\overline{f}}(\sigma)\big)}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma\,ds.\end{split}

(4.4)

We introduce the new variable

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{h}:=\widetilde{f}+\widetilde{g},\\
&\widetilde{g}(t,\xi):=-\chi_{C}(\xi)\int_{0}^{t}B^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}ds\,\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi),\qquad\chi_{C}(\xi):=\varphi_{\leqslant-C}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\end{split}

(4.5)

for
C
sufficiently large so that
2^{-C+10}\leqslant 2\lambda-1
.

We now rephrase the above computations in terms of
h
and
g
,
and organize the various contributions into leading and remainder terms,
and according to the nature of the interactions.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have the decomposition

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{f_{0}}+S+M+F,

(4.6)

where:

•

The source terms are defined as, for
\theta:={\bf P_{c}}\phi^{2}
,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}

\displaystyle S_{1}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}(A^{2}(s)-B^{2}(s))\,\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds,

(4.7a)

\displaystyle S_{2}:=(1-\chi_{C}(\xi))\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}B^{2}(s)\,\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds,

(4.7b)

\displaystyle S_{3}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}|A|^{2}(s)\,\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle+2\lambda)}\overline{A}^{2}(s)\,\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds.

(4.7c)

•

The mixed terms are defined as

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\big(M_{1}-M_{2}+RM\big),

\displaystyle M_{1}:=\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle-\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{h}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds,

(4.8a)

\displaystyle M_{2}:=\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle-\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{g}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds,

(4.8b)

where
RM
denotes remainder terms given by

\displaystyle RM:=RM_{1}+RM_{2}+RM_{3},

\displaystyle RM_{1}:=\int_{0}^{t}\overline{A(s)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle+\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds,

(4.9a)

\displaystyle RM_{2}:=-\int_{0}^{t}a(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\overline{\widetilde{f}}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds

(4.9b)

\displaystyle RM_{3}:=\int_{0}^{t}(A(s)-B(s))\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle-\lambda)}\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\widetilde{f}(s,\eta)\nu(\xi,\eta)d\eta\,ds.

(4.9c)

•

The field self-interaction terms are defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&F:=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{\pm\}}{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}F_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}\big(f_{{\epsilon}_{1}},f_{{\epsilon}_{2}}\big),\qquad f_{+}=f,\,\,\,f_{-}=\bar{f},\\
&F_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(a,b):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{a}(s,\eta)\widetilde{b}(s,\sigma)\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.9}{\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}ds},\end{split}

(4.10)

where the phases are defined by

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\langle\xi\rangle-{\epsilon}_{1}\langle\eta\rangle-{\epsilon}_{2}\langle\sigma\rangle.

(4.11)

Proof.

We just need to verify that the terms on the right-hand side (
4.6
)
equal all the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.4
) plus
\widetilde{g}
as defined in (
4.5
).
This is easily checked since the terms in (
4.7
) added to
\widetilde{g}
match the first four integrals
on the right-hand side of (
4.4
),
the term
F
in (
4.10
) matches the last integral in (
4.4
),
and the terms (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) match the remaining four integrals in (
4.4
) (after using
f=h-g
).
∎

4.2.
The main bootstrap

Here is our main bootstrap proposition for the radiation term:

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
u=a(t)\phi+v(t)
be a solution of (
1.6
),
let
f=e^{-itL}(\partial_{t}v+iLv)
, and recall the definition of
h
in (
4.5
). Let
\rho(t)=|A(t)|^{2}
,
where
A
is defined as in (
1.7
), and assume that (
3.17
) holds, that is,
for
T>0,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big|\big(1+\rho_{0}\frac{\Gamma}{\lambda}t\big)\frac{\rho(t)}{\rho_{0}}-1\Big|<\frac{1}{2}.

(4.12)

Then, there exists
\bar{{\varepsilon}}>0
such that, if
C_{0}^{-1}{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}_{0}\leqslant\bar{{\varepsilon}}
, with
C_{0}>1

(sufficiently large) and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle^{-1}\rho^{\frac{2\beta-1}{2}}(t)\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{\beta},\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leqslant 2\varepsilon,\end{split}

(4.13)

for some small
\beta>0
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle t\sqrt{\rho(t)}\rangle^{-1}\rho^{\frac{2\beta-1}{2}}(t)\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\beta},\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leqslant\varepsilon.\end{split}

(4.14)

Let us now explain the structure of the proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

First, when the time is smaller than the natural local time of existence
1/{\varepsilon}_{0}

it is not hard to show the following local-in-time bootstrap:
assume that
T\leqslant 1/{\varepsilon}_{0}
, that (
1.46
) holds and that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon}^{1-\beta},\qquad\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon};

(4.15)

then we have (
3.19
) and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}^{1-\beta},\qquad\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}.

(4.16)

The proof of this is much simpler than for large times.
In particular no precise information about the measure
\mu
is needed
and the crude Lemma
A.3
suffices. See Subsection
9.2
.

The rest of the paper is then dedicated to proving the result for the remaining times.
Note that in the regime
t>\varepsilon_{0}^{-1},
the bootstrap assumptions (
4.13
) read

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1},T]}t^{-1}\rho^{\beta-1}(t)\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon}^{\beta},\qquad\sup_{t\in[{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1},T]}\|h(t)\|_{H^{N}}\leqslant 2{\varepsilon}.

The weighted estimates for the three types of nonlinear terms on the right-hand side of (
4.6
),
that is,
S
,
M
and
F
,
are performed, respectively, in §
4.3.3
, Section
8
and Section
5
.
Together with (
1.42
),
the main inequalities (
4.29
), (
5.2
) and (
8.3
),
show that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
and some
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant{\varepsilon}_{0}+C_{1}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta}\leqslant C_{1}\bigg({\varepsilon}_{0}+{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2\delta}\big(t\rho^{1-\beta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta}\big)\bigg).

Therefore, since
t\rho^{1-\beta}(t)\geqslant(1/2){\varepsilon}_{0}^{1-2\beta}
,
(for
{\varepsilon}_{0}
small enough) it suffices to choose
\bar{{\varepsilon}}
such that
C_{1}\big((\overline{{\varepsilon}}/C_{0})^{\beta}+\overline{{\varepsilon}}^{2\delta})<1,

to conclude that the first inequality in (
4.14
) holds.

Finally, the bound on the
H^{N}
norm in (
4.14
) is a consequence of Proposition
9.1
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In view of the local bootstrap (
4.15
)-(
4.16
),
from now on we will always assume that
t\geqslant{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1}\approx\rho_{0}^{-1/2}.

Note that past this local time of existence, the first estimate in
(
4.14
)
is implied by
\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant t\rho^{1-\beta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta}
,
and it suffices to show that
there exists
\delta>0
such that
\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta}(t){\varepsilon}^{\beta}
(since
\rho(t)\lesssim{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}
).

4.3.
Preliminary estimates

4.3.1.
Consequences of the a priori bounds

We now summarize the main dispersive estimates that are a consequence of the a priori assumption (
4.13
).

Lemma 4.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|e^{itL}g(t)\|_{L^{6}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(t)\ln(1+t)\big(\|(1-\Delta)\phi\|_{L^{12/5}}^{2}+\|\phi\|_{H^{3}_{x}}^{2}\big),\\
&\|e^{itL}g(t)\|_{L^{3}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(t)\ln(1+t)\langle\sqrt{t}\rangle\big(\|(1-\Delta)\phi\|_{L^{12/5}}^{2}+\|\phi\|_{H^{3}_{x}}^{2}\big).\end{split}

(4.17)

More generally,

\displaystyle\|e^{itL}g(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(t)t^{1-3(1/2-1/p)}\ln(1+t),

(4.18)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|e^{itL}h(t)\|_{L^{q}_{x}},\quad\|e^{itL}f(t)\|_{L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(t)^{1-\beta}t^{\frac{5(q-2)}{2q}\delta_{N}+\frac{6-q}{2q}}\varepsilon^{\beta},\end{split}

(4.19)

for
2\leqslant q\leqslant 6.

Proof.

The proofs are standard.
For (
4.17
), splitting the integral
\int_{0}^{t}=\int_{0}^{t-1}+\int_{t-1}^{t}
we treat the main piece using Lemma
2.5
:

\displaystyle\|e^{itL}g(t)\|_{L^{6}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{t-s}\rho(s)ds\|(1-\Delta)\phi\|_{12/5}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t/2}\rho(s)ds+\rho(t)\int_{t/2}^{t-1}\frac{1}{t-s}ds\bigg]\|(1-\Delta)\phi\|_{12/5}^{2}.

For the other piece we use Sobolev embedding to obtain the result. The proof is the same for the second inequality on
g
.

The inequality on
e^{itL}h
in (
4.19
) in the case
q=6

is a consequence of the second decay estimate from Lemma
2.5
for frequencies less than
t^{\delta_{N}}
.
The inequality for the remaining frequencies is obtained using Sobolev’s embedding and Bernstein’s inequality
with the a priori bound on the Sobolev norm in (
4.13
).
The estimate for
f
is obtained from summing the estimates for
h
and
g
.

Finally, to prove the
L^{q}
estimates, we use the interpolation inequality

\|f\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3(q-2)}{2q}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{6-q}{2q}},

and the conclusion follows.
∎

The above bootstrap assumptions also imply a decay bound on
\|\partial_{s}\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{2}}:

Lemma 4.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{s}h\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(s).

(4.20)

Also,

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim\rho(s),\qquad 1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty.

(4.21)

Proof.

From the definition (
4.5
), and using the equation
\partial_{s}f=e^{-isL}(\partial_{s}-iL)w
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h}(s,\xi)&=\partial_{s}\widetilde{f}-\chi_{C}(\xi)B^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}\,\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi)\\
&=e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\Big(\big(a\phi+\dfrac{1}{L}\Im w\big)^{2}\Big)-\chi_{C}(\xi)B^{2}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}\,\widetilde{\phi^{2}}(\xi).\end{split}

(4.22)

Then, from Hölder, the apriori bounds (
4.13
)
and Lemma
4.4
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial_{s}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\rho(s)+\rho(s)^{\frac{3-\beta}{2}}s^{3\delta_{N}}\varepsilon^{\beta}+\rho(s)^{2-\beta}s^{1/2+3\delta_{N}}\varepsilon^{2\beta},

(4.23)

from which (
4.20
) directly follows.

(
4.21
) follows directly from the definition (
4.5
) and the a priori bound (
4.12
)
transferred to
B
via (
3.34
).
∎

4.3.2.
Basic bounds on the Fermi/bad component

We collect basic properties satisfied by
g
:

Lemma 4.6
.

For
s\approx 2^{m}
,
m=1,2,\dots
, we have

\displaystyle{\|\widetilde{g}(s)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}}\lesssim m\rho(2^{m})2^{m},

(4.24)

and, for integers
0\leqslant n\leqslant N
,

\displaystyle{\|D^{n}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{g}(s)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{1-},\qquad{\|D^{n}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(s)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{m/2}.

(4.25)

Moreover

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(s,\xi)\|}_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim m^{2}2^{3m/2}\,\rho(2^{m}),

(4.26)

and

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(s,\xi)\|}_{L^{1}_{\xi}}\lesssim 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})m^{2}.

(4.27)

Finally

\displaystyle\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}{\|\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widetilde{g}(s,\xi)\|}_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim\rho(2^{m})2^{3m}.

(4.28)

Proof.

The property (
4.24
) is straightforward from the definition of
g
in (
4.5
) and using (
3.17
).

The first inequality in (
4.25
) follows from (
1.55
), and the second can be proved similarly.

To prove (
4.26
) we split, for some
A<-C-1
to be determined later,

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}(s,\xi)

\displaystyle=I_{A}(s,\xi)+\sum_{k\geqslant A+1}^{-C}II_{k}(s,\xi)+\{\textrm{easier terms}\},

\displaystyle I_{A}(s,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{\leqslant A}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\int_{0}^{s}\tau B^{2}(\tau)e^{-i\tau(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}d\tau\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)

\displaystyle II_{k}(s,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\int_{0}^{s}\tau B^{2}(\tau)e^{-i\tau(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}d\tau\widetilde{\theta}(\xi),

where
easier terms
refers to cases where the derivative falls
on either the
\varphi_{\leqslant-C}
cut-off or on
\widetilde{\theta}.

For the first piece we have the estimate
\|I(s,\xi)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim 2^{A/2+m}.

For the second we integrate by parts in time and bound the two resulting pieces using Lemma
3.5
and (
3.17
):

\displaystyle\|II_{k}(s,\xi)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg\|\frac{e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}}{\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda}\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}sB^{2}(s)\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)\varphi_{k}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle+\bigg\|\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k}(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)\int_{0}^{s}\tau B^{\prime}(\tau)B(\tau)e^{-i\tau(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}d\tau\frac{\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)}{\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda}\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\int_{|\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda|\approx 2^{k}}d\xi\bigg)^{1/2}2^{-k}\bigg[s\rho(s)+\ln(s\rho_{0}+1)\bigg].

Optimizing, we choose
A=-m,
and obtain (
4.26
).

For the (
4.27
) we have similarly

\displaystyle\|I_{A}(s,\xi)\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}\lesssim 2^{A+m},\qquad\|II_{k}(s,\xi)\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}\lesssim\big[s\rho(s)+\ln(s\rho_{0}+1)\big](-A),

and the result follows with the same choice of
A
as above.

The last bound is obtained more crudely just by differentiating the definition of
\widetilde{g}
in (
4.5
).
∎

4.3.3.
Source terms

We have the following bound on the ‘good’ source terms (
4.7
):

Lemma 4.7
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
1.46
), we have, for some
\delta\in(0,\beta/2)
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}S\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim t\rho(t)^{1-\beta+\delta}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}.

(4.29)

Proof.

We start with the easier term
S_{2}
.
We localize in time using (
1.74
) and treat the main contribution
integrating by parts in
s
and using Lemma
3.5
:

\displaystyle\bigg\|(1-\chi_{C}(\xi))\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)s\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\,e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}B^{2}(s)\,\widetilde{\theta(\xi)}ds\bigg\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg\|\tau_{m}(t)tB^{2}(t)\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\frac{e^{-it(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}}{\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda}(1-\chi_{C}(\xi))\,\widetilde{\theta(\xi)}\bigg\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\bigg\|\frac{1-\chi_{C}(\xi)}{\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda}\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}\big(2sB^{\prime}(s)B(s)\tau_{m}(s)+sB^{2}(s)2^{-m}\tau^{\prime}_{m}(s)+B^{2}(s)\tau_{m}(s)\big)ds\,\widetilde{\theta(\xi)}\bigg\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{s\approx 2^{m}}\rho(s)ds\lesssim\ln\langle 2^{m}\rho_{0}\rangle.

Summing over
m
gives a bound by
\log\langle t\rho_{0}^{2}\rangle

which is acceptable for the right-hand side of (
4.29
).
The above integration by parts in time
is the typical strategy that we can use to treat non-resonant terms with non-vanishing phases.

For the term
S_{1}
in (
4.7a
) we can estimate

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}S_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{m}\Bigg\|\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)se^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}\big(A^{2}(s)-B^{2}(s)\big)\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds\Bigg\|_{L^{2}},

having disregarded the term where the derivative falls on
\widetilde{\theta}
which is faster decaying
and easy to estimate.
Now we write
A^{2}-B^{2}=2B(A-B)+(A-B)^{2}.

Using (
3.34
), we see that
(A-B)^{2}=O(A^{4}),
hence

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}s\tau_{m}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-2\lambda)}\big(A(s)-B(s)\big)^{2}\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{2m}\rho^{2}(2^{m}),

(4.30)

which is sufficient. For the other term with
B(A-B)
, we also use (
3.34
) to expand
A-B
and obtain three terms.
The cases of the terms
B\overline{A}^{2}
and
B|A|^{2}
are non-resonant,
and the associated phase does not vanish and can be handled as
S_{2}
above.
We focus on the more difficult term with
BA^{2}
:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}S_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{m}\Bigg\|\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\int_{0}^{t}s\tau_{m}(s)e^{-is(\langle\xi\rangle-3\lambda)}B(s)A^{2}(s)\widetilde{\theta}(\xi)ds\Bigg\|_{L^{2}}.

As we did above, we can write
BA^{2}=B(A^{2}-B^{2})+B^{3},
the first term having enough
decay to be treated as (
4.30
) above.
For the remaining piece, we can proceed exactly as in the proof of
(
4.26
) above, replacing
2\lambda
by
3\lambda
,
and obtain the desired bound.

For the remaining term
S_{3}
in (
4.7c
), we start by replacing the
A
terms by
B
terms.
The error terms are then of the same type as
S_{1},
but easier to treat since the phases are not resonant.
The terms that remain are then of the same type as
S_{2},
in the sense that they
are quadratic in
B
with a non-resonant phase. Therefore, the same argument applies
and we can skip the details.
∎

5.
Field self-interactions

Our main goal is to prove the main weighted bound for the terms that
are quadratic in the continuous component
f
of the solution. In (distorted) Fourier space this is the term
F
from (
4.10
)
(or equivalently the last term of (
4.3
))
which, recall, is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&F=F(t,\xi)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}e^{-isL}\Big(\frac{\Im w(s)}{L}\Big)^{2}ds\Big)(\xi),\qquad w=e^{itL}f.\\
\end{split}

(5.1)

The main result we want to prove is:

Proposition 5.1
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
4.12
)-(
4.13
) we have

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}F(t)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta}(t),

(5.2)

for some
\delta\in(0,\beta/2).

Moreover, as mentioned in Section
3
, we also need to prove
the estimate (
3.27
), which was used to obtain Lemma
3.4
,
and whose proof was postponed to the current section.
Expressed in terms of
F
, see (
5.1
), the estimate we need is contained in the following:

Proposition 5.2
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
4.12
)-(
4.13
)
there exists
\frac{1}{2}<a<1
such that

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(F)(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a}}.

(5.3)

The proof of the main Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
occupies all of
the current section and Sections
6
and
7
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that using the sharp linear estimate (
2.15
) (and Bernstein’s inequality),
together with (
5.2
), we could obtain a bound similar to (
5.3
)
but with a decay rate slightly slower than
\langle t\rangle^{-1}
.
Unfortunately, this is insufficient for the arguments in Section
3
where the rate needs to be integrable.
As it turns out, proving (
5.3
) requires additional arguments
from those in the proof of (
5.2
).

5.1.
Set-up

In this set-up section we first explain how to deal with high and (very) low frequencies.
More precisely, we state results that prove (
5.2
) and (
5.3
)
when the input and output frequencies
are larger than a small power of time or smaller than a large negative power of time.
This is convenient as it will reduce the analysis to the case when all
the input and output frequencies involved are bounded above by a small power of the time variable,
and are lower bounded as well, so that summations over their respective dyadic scales
can then be done at the expense of innocuous logarithmic factors in time.

At the end of this subsection we discuss the different types of quadratic interactions that we are going
to need to estimate, and the strategy for the proofs of the main Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
.

5.1.1.
High frequencies

We distinguish between (very) high frequency and mid-low frequencies
by introducing a parameter
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2^{M_{0}}\approx\langle s\rangle^{\delta_{N}}

\displaystyle M_{0}=\lfloor\delta_{N}\log_{2}\langle s\rangle\rfloor,\qquad\delta_{N}=5/N,

(5.4)

and splitting

F
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-4F:=F_{H}+F_{L},\\
&F_{L}(t,\xi):=-4\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\xi)\,e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\Big(\frac{P_{\leqslant M_{0}}\Im w(s)}{L}\Big)^{2}ds\end{split}

(5.5)

with the natural definition of
F_{H}
.
Recall that
P_{\leqslant k}
denotes the distorted Littlewood-Paley projector
with symbol
\varphi_{\leqslant k}=\sum_{k^{\prime}\leqslant k}\varphi_{k^{\prime}}
; see §
1.8
.
Using the Fourier inversion (Proposition
2.1
) as done before, we can write
the above expression in distorted Fourier space as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(2\pi)^{9/2}F_{\ast}=\sum_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{+,-\}}{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}F_{\ast,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}\big(f_{{\epsilon}_{1}},f_{{\epsilon}_{2}}\big),\qquad f_{+}=f,\,\,f_{-}=\bar{f},\qquad\ast\in\{L,H\},\end{split}

(5.6)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&F_{\ast,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(a,b):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{a}(s,\eta)\widetilde{b}(s,\sigma)\mu_{\ast}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma\,ds,\\
&\qquad\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\langle\xi\rangle-{\epsilon}_{1}\langle\eta\rangle-{\epsilon}_{2}\langle\sigma\rangle,\end{split}

(5.7)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{L}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle),\qquad\mu_{H}:=\mu-\mu_{L},\end{split}

(5.8)

recall (
4.1
).
Note that we are suppressing the dependence of
F_{\ast,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(a,b)
from its independent variables
(t,\xi)
;
we may sometimes indicate this dependence but will usually omit it, as it will be clear from the context.
Similarly, we may omit the dependence on the inputs
(f_{{\epsilon}_{1}},f_{{\epsilon}_{2}})
.
We remark that in what follows the signs
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}
will not play an essential role,
since the phases
\Phi_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}

will have similar properties (in particular we will show they are suitably lower bounded
on a certain region of frequency space, see Lemma
5.10
),
and the conjugation on
f
(or
h
or
g
) is irrelevant in view of Lemma
2.3
.

The estimate for the high frequency part is the following:

Lemma 5.4
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
4.12
)-(
4.13
),
there exists
\delta^{\prime}\in(\delta,\beta/2)
such that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}F_{H}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(t),

(5.9)

and
a\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(F_{H})(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a}}.

(5.10)

The proof of this lemma is postponed to Subsection
9.2
.

5.1.2.
Low frequencies

In view of Lemma
5.4
, from (
5.5
)-(
5.8
),
we see that for (
5.2
) it suffices to show that for all
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}=\{+,-\}

we have

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(t);

(5.11)

similarly, (
5.3
) would follow from

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\left(F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}\right)(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a}}.

(5.12)

The next result, whose proof is postponed to Subsection
A.1
,
reduces matters to profiles that are supported at frequencies that are not too small;
more precisely, we have:

Lemma 5.5
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
4.12
)-(
4.13
),
there exists
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta/2)
and
a\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi}F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(A_{{\epsilon}_{1}},B_{{\epsilon}_{2}})(t)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\beta}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(t),

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\left(F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(A_{{\epsilon}_{1}},B_{{\epsilon}_{2}})\right)(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a}},

\displaystyle\mbox{for all}\quad(A,B)\in\big\{(f_{\leqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}},f_{\leqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}}),\,(f_{\leqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}},f_{\geqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}}),\,(f_{\geqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}},f_{\leqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}})\big\}.

Here
f_{\ast}:=P_{\ast}f
, where
P
is the distorted Littlewood-Paley projection (see §
1.8
).

As a consequence of Lemma
5.5
the profiles we consider from now on
carry a localization to frequencies larger than
\langle s\rangle^{-5}.

Furthermore, since

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}\big(\widetilde{P_{\geqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}}f}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{f}\|}_{L^{2}},

which is obtained as a direct consequence of Hardy’s inequality,
we can safely suppress the notation
P_{\geqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}}
on the profiles,
and still denote by
F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(t)
the bilinear operator as in (
5.7
)-(
5.8
)
with this additional restriction to
|\eta|,|\sigma|\gtrsim\langle s\rangle^{-5}.

5.1.3.
Types of quadratic interactions and organization of the proof

Since
f=h-g
, we need to estimate the quadratic terms

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(a,b)\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad a\in\{h_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}\},\,\,b\in\{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}\}.\end{split}

(5.13)

We then have three main kinds of interactions to analyze
which we denote as follows:

•

Purely Fermi-type interactions
when
a,b=g_{\pm}
;

•

Fermi-continuous interactions
when
\{a,b\}=\{h_{\pm},g_{\pm}\}
;

•

Purely continuous interactions
when
a,b=h_{\pm}
.

These three types of interactions require different treatments and we dedicate to each of them separate
subsections within Sections
6
and
7
.
One important aspect of our analysis will be the splitting of the bilinear terms (
5.13
)
- more precisely of their leading order contribution, corresponding to the leading order contribution
of
\mu
-
into a
singular
and a
regular
part; see Proposition
5.6
below,
and the consequent definition of a singular part
F^{S}
and a regular part
F^{R}
in (
5.46
).
The singular, resp. regular, parts are treated in Section
6
, resp.
7
.

For the singular part of the bilinear interactions we can perform a normal form transformation.
The (time)-boundary terms are estimates in Subsection
6.2
,
and the three types of interactions described above are dealt with
in Subsections
6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
.
For the regular part, the three different kinds of interactions are
estimated in Subsections
7.2
,
7.3
and
7.4
.

5.2.
Decomposition of the NSD

In the previous subsection we have reduced the problem to the mid-low frequencies interaction term
F_{L}
;
in particular, all the bilinear operators that we are going to estimate will be supported (before time integration)
in a region where all input and output frequencies are less than
2^{M_{0}}\approx\langle s\rangle^{\delta_{N}}
,
with an additional restriction on the inputs being projected to not-very-low frequencies via
implicit
P_{\geqslant\langle s\rangle^{-5}}
operators.
Then, our goal is to prove (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) for
F_{L}
,
which, together with Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
,
will imply Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
.

To prove our main results we first need a precise description of the interaction
of three distorted complex exponentials, encoded by the distribution
\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)
,
and a splitting of it into a singular and regular part, plus a suitable remainder.
This is contained in the next proposition, which is mostly based on results from

[
41
]
(see Proposition
B.1
in Appendix
A
):

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mu
be the distribution defined in (
4.1
),
with
\psi
satisfying (
2.1
)
for a generic potential
8
8

8

As already mentioned, a bound on sufficiently
many Schwartz semi-norms of
V
is enough.
For example, we can assume that
V
is in a weighted Sobolev space, say
\langle x\rangle^{N}V\in H^{N}
, where
N

is the Sobolev regularity parameter in (
1.45
); see also Theorem
2.2
.

V\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
9
9

9

In our application the parameter
M_{0}\geqslant 1
that appears here
will coincide with the one in (
5.4
).

M_{0}
be a fixed parameter, and let
10
10

10

The parameter
N_{2}

can be fixed large enough as a fraction of
N
.

N_{2}
be a sufficiently large integer.

We can decompose
\mu
into a singular part (apex
S
), a regular part (apex
R
)
and a remainder part (apex
Re
)

\displaystyle\mu=\mu^{S}+\mu^{R}+\mu^{Re}.

(5.14)

These three components can be further decomposed as follows,
up to irrelevant multiplicative constants:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\delta_{0}(\xi-\eta-\sigma)+\mu_{1}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{2}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{3}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\\
&\mu^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\mu_{1}^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\end{split}

(5.15)

where the right-hand sides are defined as follows:

•

\mu_{1}^{S}
and
\mu_{1}^{R}
are given by the formulas

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{\ast}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

\displaystyle:=\nu_{1}^{\ast}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{1}^{\ast}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)+\overline{\nu_{1}^{\ast}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)},\qquad\ast\in\{S,R\}

(5.16)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{1}^{S}(p,q)&:=\varphi_{\leqslant-M_{0}-5}(|p|-|q|)\Big[\nu_{0}(p,q)\\
&+\frac{1}{|p|}\sum_{a=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{a,J}(p,q)\cdot 2^{J}K_{a}\big(2^{J}(|p|-|q|)\big)\Big]\end{split}

(5.17)

with

\displaystyle\nu_{0}(p,q):=\frac{b_{0}(p,q)}{|p|}\Big[i\pi\,\delta(|p|-|q|)+\mathrm{p.v.}\frac{1}{|p|-|q|}\Big],

(5.18)

where
b_{0}
is a symbol satisfying (
B.5
),

K_{a}
are Schwartz functions,
b_{a,J}
are symbols satisfying the bounds stated in (
B.7
),
and
\nu_{1}^{R}
satisfies, for all
|p|\approx 2^{P}
,
|q|\approx 2^{Q}
, with
P,Q\leqslant M_{0}

and for all
|a|+|b|\leqslant N_{2}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\nabla_{p}^{a}\nabla_{q}^{b}\nu_{1}^{R}(p,q)|\\
&\lesssim\begin{cases}2^{-2P}\,2^{-(|a|+|b|)P}\cdot 2^{(2+|a|+|b|)5M_{0}}&\textrm{if}\quad|P-Q|<5\\
2^{-2(P\vee Q)}\cdot 2^{-|a|(P\vee Q)}\max\{1,2^{(1-|b|)Q^{-}}\}\cdot 2^{(|a|+|b|+2)5M_{0}}&\textrm{if}\quad|P-Q|\geqslant 5.\end{cases}\end{split}

(5.19)

•

\mu_{2}^{S}
is given by the formulas

\displaystyle\mu_{2}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

\displaystyle:=\nu_{2}^{S,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{S,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{S,2}(\xi,\sigma,\eta),

(5.20)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{2}^{S,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\frac{1}{|\xi|}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{\leqslant-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\\
\times b_{i,J}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),\end{split}

(5.21)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{2}^{S,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\frac{1}{|\eta|}\sum_{\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{\leqslant-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|+\epsilon|\eta|-|\sigma|)\\
\times b_{i,J}^{\epsilon}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|+\epsilon|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),\end{split}

(5.22)

where
K_{i}
are Schwartz functions and
b_{i,J}
and
b_{i,J}^{\epsilon}

are symbols satisfying the bounds stated in (
B.13
) and (
B.17
).

•

\mu_{3}^{S}
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{3}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{\leqslant-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\\
\times b_{i,J}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),\end{split}

(5.23)

where
K_{i}
are Schwartz functions
and
b_{i,J}
are symbols satisfying the bounds stated in (
B.7
).

•

\mu^{Re}
satisfies the following estimate:
for all
|\xi|\approx 2^{k_{1}}
,
|\eta|\approx 2^{k_{2}}
and
|\sigma|\approx 2^{k_{3}}
with
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leqslant M_{0}

and for all
|a|+|\alpha|+|\beta|\leqslant N_{2}
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\xi}^{a}\nabla_{\eta}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\sigma}^{\beta}\mu^{Re}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)|

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-2\max\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}}\cdot 2^{-(|\alpha_{a}|+|\alpha_{e}|)\max\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}}

(5.24)

\displaystyle\cdot\max\big(1,2^{(1-|\alpha_{i}|)\min\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}}\big)\cdot 2^{5M_{0}(|a|+|\alpha|+|\beta|+1)},

where
\alpha_{i}
denotes the order of differentiation of the variable that is located at the smallest frequency,

\alpha_{e}
is similarly defined for the middle frequency and
\alpha_{a}
for the largest one.

The above proposition essentially follows from Proposition
B.1
,
whose content is taken from
[
41
]
, and which we rewrote in the Appendix for the convenience of the reader.
Proposition
B.1
gives a decomposition of
\mu(\xi,\eta,\sigma)
in terms of the singularities
in
|\xi-\eta|-|\sigma|
,
|\xi-\sigma|-|\eta|
and so on.
Understanding the precise structure of these singularities is one of the keys to
extracting decay in distorted frequency space from the integrals (
5.7
).

Here are a few comments on the statement of Proposition
5.6
:

•

Proposition
5.6
goes a step further compared to Proposition
B.1
,
and adapts
\mu
and its various components to the nonlinear structure of the Klein-Gordon flow.
In particular, the various pieces are decomposed into singular and regular parts.
There is also an even more regular remainder part which we omit for the sake of this discussion.

The distinction between singular and regular parts of
\mu
,
which also gives an analogous splitting for the nonlinear terms (see (
5.46
))
is made so that, on the support of the singular parts, we will be able to lower bound the
nonlinear Klein-Gordon phase
\Phi_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}
(see (
5.7
));
this is done in Lemma
5.10
.

•

The estimate (
5.19
), implies that, up to small losses for large frequencies
(which will be innocuous for our arguments), the regular measure
\nu_{1}^{R}(p,q)
behaves
almost like a Coifman-Meyer multiplier times a factor of
(|p|\vee|q|)^{-2}
,
and better (upon localization) than a multiplier in
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{(x,y)\rightarrow(p,q)}L^{1}_{x,y}

times a factor of
(|p|\vee|q|)^{-2}
.
More importantly, it is not very singular so that we can also use these estimates pointwise.

•

The distributions
\nu_{1}^{S}
are the most singular and arise from the interaction (in the definition
of
\mu
) of two plane waves and one ‘perturbed’ wave
\psi(x,\zeta)-e^{ix\cdot\zeta}
; see (
2.1
).
The distributions (measures, in fact)
\nu_{2}^{S,1}
and
\nu_{2}^{S,2}

are a little more regular since they arise from the interaction of at least two perturbed waves.
Estimates on the bilinear operators associated to these distributions are given in Theorem
5.7
.
The statement also contains bilinear estimates for the operators associated to

\mu^{R}
and
\mu^{Re}
.

Proof.

We look at Proposition
B.1
and extract all the distributions in the statement from the formulas there.

We first inspect the formula for
\mu_{1}
in (
B.2
), which gives

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{1}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)+\overline{\nu_{1}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)}

with the decomposition of
\nu_{1}
in (
B.3
), with (
B.4
) and (
B.5
),
(
B.6
) and (
B.7
), and the bounds (
B.8
)-(
B.9
).
Then, with the definition (
5.17
)-(
5.18
) for
\nu_{1}^{S}

and letting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{1}^{R}(p,q)&:=\varphi_{>-M_{0}-5}(|p|-|q|)\Big[\nu_{0}(p,q)\\
&+\frac{1}{|p|}\sum_{a=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{a,J}(p,q)\cdot 2^{J}K_{a}\big(2^{J}(|p|-|q|)\big)\Big]+\nu_{R}(p,q),\end{split}

(5.25)

where
\nu_{R}(p,q)
is the symbol satisfying (
B.8
)-(
B.9
),
we have that the sum of (
5.17
) and (
5.25
) gives us the term
\nu_{1}
in (
B.3
),
and therefore

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\mu_{1}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{1}^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma).

(5.26)

The bound (
5.19
) for the expression in (
5.25
)
holds as a result of (
B.8
)-(
B.9
) (which is the desired bound for
\nu_{R}
)
and since the same can be verified directly for the first expression on the right-hand side of (
5.25
).

To verify the properties in the second bullet,
we start by inspecting the formulas (
B.11
)-(
B.12
) for
\nu_{2}^{1}
,
with the bounds (
B.13
) and (
B.14
),
and the formulas (
B.15
)-(
B.16
) for
\nu_{2}^{2}
, with the bounds (
B.17
)-(
B.18
).
We then set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{2}^{R,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\frac{1}{|\xi|}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{>-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\\
\times b_{i,J}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),\end{split}

(5.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\frac{1}{|\eta|}\sum_{\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{>-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|+\epsilon|\eta|-|\sigma|)\\
\times b_{i,J}^{\epsilon}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|+\epsilon|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),\end{split}

(5.28)

and see that we can write

\displaystyle\nu_{2}^{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\nu_{2}^{S,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

(5.29)

where (note the different placing of the indexes)
\nu_{2,R}^{1}
is the remainder in (
B.11
)
satisfying the estimate (
B.14
).
Similarly,

\displaystyle\nu_{2}^{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\nu_{2}^{S,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

(5.30)

where (note again the different placing of the indexes)
\nu_{2,R}^{2}
is the remainder
appearing at the end of the formula (
B.15
) which satisfies the estimates (
B.18
).
From (
B.10
) and the definitions in (
5.20
),
we then deduce that the
\mu_{2}
component in (
B.10
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=\mu_{2}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\\
&+\nu_{2}^{R,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\sigma,\eta)\\
&+\nu_{2,R}^{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{2}(\xi,\sigma,\eta).\end{split}

(5.31)

Similarly, by letting

\displaystyle\mu_{3}^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{2}}\sum_{J\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\varphi_{>-M_{0}-5}(|\xi|+|\eta|-|\sigma|)b_{i,J}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\cdot K_{i}\big(2^{J}(|\xi|-|\eta|-|\sigma|)\big),

we can see that the term
