\mu_{3}
in (
B.19
) can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{3}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=\mu_{3}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{3}^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{3,R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\end{split}

(5.32)

where
\mu_{3,R}
(note again the different placing of the indexes)
is the last term in (
B.19
) and satisfies (
B.22
).

We then let

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu^{Re}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&:=\nu_{2,R}^{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2,R}^{2}(\xi,\sigma,\eta)\\
&+\nu_{2}^{R,1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\nu_{2}^{R,2}(\xi,\sigma,\eta)\\
&+\mu_{3}^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)+\mu_{3,R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\end{split}

(5.33)

so that the identities (
5.14
)-(
5.15
) hold true in view of
(
B.1
) and (
5.26
), (
5.31
) and (
5.32
).

To conclude we need to verify the bound (
5.24
) for the remainder (
5.33
).
Notice first that (
B.22
) gives a stronger bound for
\mu_{3,R}
.
Using the formula (
5.32
) we can directly verify the bound for
\mu_{3}^{R}
.
We can then inspect the formulas (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) to check the bounds for the terms in
the second line of (
5.33
).
Finally, for the terms on the first line of the right-hand side of (
5.33
)
we invoke (
B.14
) and (
B.18
).
∎

5.3.
Bilinear operators: identities and bounds

Recall that our main term to estimate is
F_{L}
from (
5.6
)-(
5.7
).
Below we give some useful identities and bounds for
bilinear terms as in (
5.7
) that we are going to use in the rest of the arguments.

First, in view of Proposition
5.6
,
we define several bilinear operators, which correspond to the various pieces in which
\mu
is decomposed.
For a general symbol
b=b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)
let us denote

\displaystyle T_{0}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(\eta)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\delta_{0}(\xi-\eta-\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma,

(5.34a)

\displaystyle T_{1}^{\ast,1}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(\xi-\eta)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\nu_{1}^{\ast}(\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma,\quad\ast\in\{S,R\},

(5.34b)

\displaystyle T_{1}^{\ast,2}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(-\eta-\sigma)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\overline{\nu_{1}^{\ast}(\eta,\xi)}\,d\eta d\sigma,\quad\ast\in\{S,R\},

(5.34c)

\displaystyle T_{2}^{S}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(\eta)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu_{2}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma,

(5.34d)

\displaystyle T_{3}^{S}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(\eta)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu_{3}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma,

(5.34e)

\displaystyle T^{Re}[b](g,h)(x)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}g(\eta)h(\sigma)\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu^{Re}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma.

(5.34f)

The distributions appearing above are defined in Proposition
5.6
and in its proof;
see (
5.17
), (
5.20
) and (
5.23
), (
5.25
) and (
5.33
).

Second, for a general symbol
b
, let us adopt the following notation for
b
evaluated at
different inputs:

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=b(\xi,\xi-\eta,\sigma),\\
b_{1}^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=b(\xi,\sigma,\xi-\eta),\\
b_{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=b(\xi,-\eta-\sigma,\sigma).\end{split}

(5.35)

With the definitions (
5.34
) and the notation (
5.35
),
using the decomposition from Proposition
5.6
and applying changes of variable,
we see that we can write bilinear operators of the type (
5.6
)-(
5.7
)
as follows:
with
\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}:=e^{is{\epsilon}_{1}\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{a}
and
\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}:=e^{is{\epsilon}_{2}\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{b}
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&e^{is\langle\nabla\rangle}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi\mapsto x}^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\widetilde{a}(s,\eta)\widetilde{b}(s,\sigma)\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,m(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma\\
&=T_{0}[m]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x)\\
&+T_{1}^{S,1}[m_{1}]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x)+T_{1}^{S,1}[m_{1}^{\prime}]\big(\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)(x)+T_{1}^{S,2}[m_{2}]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x)\\
&+T_{2}^{S}[m]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x)+T_{3}^{S}[m]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x),\end{split}

(5.36)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&e^{is\langle\nabla\rangle}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi\mapsto x}^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{a}(s,\eta)\widetilde{b}(s,\sigma)\mu^{R}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,m(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma\\
&=T_{1}^{R,1}[m_{1}]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x)+T_{1}^{R,1}[m_{1}^{\prime}]\big(\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)(x)+T_{1}^{R,2}[m_{2}]\big(\widetilde{A_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},\widetilde{B_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)(x).\end{split}

(5.37)

In particular, we can write
F_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(a,b)

as the time integral of the sum of the expressions in (
5.36
) and (
5.37
)
with
m(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\langle\eta\rangle^{-1}\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1}
,
with similar formulas for
F_{L,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}
(inserting the cutoffs
\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}
).

We now state the main bilinear estimates satisfied by the operators in (
5.34
):

Theorem 5.7
.

Let a symbol
b=b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)
be given such that

\displaystyle\textrm{supp}(b)\subseteq\big\{(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{9}\,:\,|\xi|+|\eta|+|\sigma|\leqslant 2^{A}\big\},

for some
A\geqslant 1
and such that,
for
|\xi|\approx 2^{K},|\eta|\approx 2^{L},|\sigma|\approx 2^{M}
,

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{\xi}^{a}\nabla_{\eta}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\sigma}^{\beta}b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\big|\lesssim 2^{-|a|K-|\alpha|L-|\beta|M}\cdot 2^{(|a|+|\alpha|+|\beta|)A},\qquad|a|,|\alpha|,|\beta|\leqslant 4.

(5.38)

Let
p,q,r\in(1,\infty),
with

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}>\frac{1}{r},

and assume there is
10A\leqslant D\leqslant 2^{A/10}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(g,h):=\|g\|_{L^{2}}\|h\|_{L^{2}}+\min\big(\|\partial_{\xi}g\|_{L^{2}}\|h\|_{L^{2}},\|g\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{\xi}h\|_{L^{2}}\big)\leqslant 2^{D}.

(5.39)

Then, there exists an absolute constant
C_{0}
(for example,
C_{0}:=65
is suitable), such that
the following bilinear bounds hold for the operators defined in (
5.34
):

\displaystyle\big\|T_{0}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\widehat{g}\|_{L^{p}}\|\widehat{h}\|_{L^{q}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}A},

(5.40a)

\displaystyle\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}+\big\|P_{K}T_{1}^{S,2}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\widehat{g}\|_{L^{p}}\|\widehat{h}\|_{L^{q}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}A}+2^{-D}\mathcal{D}(g,h),

(5.40b)

\displaystyle\big\|T_{1}^{R,1}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}+\big\|P_{K}T_{1}^{R,2}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\widehat{g}\|_{L^{p}}\|\widehat{h}\|_{L^{q}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}A}

(5.40c)

\displaystyle\big\|P_{K}T_{i}^{S}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\widehat{g}\|_{L^{p}}\|\widehat{h}\|_{L^{q}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}A},\qquad i=2,3,

(5.40d)

\displaystyle\big\|P_{K}T^{Re}[b](g,h)\big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\widehat{g}\|_{L^{p}}\|\widehat{h}\|_{L^{q}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}A}.

(5.40e)

Proof.

For the convenience of the reader we include a short explanation
on how these estimates follow from (the proof of) Theorem
B.2
, which is in
[
41
]
.

Consider first the operators
T_{1}^{i}
,
i=1,2
, as they appear in (
B.24
)-(
B.25
).
The proof of the estimates (
B.32
)-(
B.33
)
is based on a decomposition made according to the size/distance from the singularity
(e.g. depending on the size of
|\eta|-|\sigma|
in the case of
T_{1}^{1}
).
The only difference with the operators
T_{1}^{S,i}
,
i=1,2
, is the presence of an additional cut-off

\varphi_{\leqslant-M_{0}-5}
in (
5.17
);
compare with the definition of
\nu_{1}
in (
B.3
).
These slight modifications do not change the estimate for the corresponding bilinear operators, and only restrict some indices of summation in the proof of Theorem
B.2
.
The same holds true for the (complementary) operators
T_{1}^{R,i}
associated to
\nu_{1}^{R}
;
see (
5.16
) and (
5.25
).

The proofs for the other operators
T_{2}^{S}
and
T_{3}^{S}
are also identical
to those for the similar operators associated to (
B.10
) and (
B.20
),
since the extra cutoffs are again harmless.

Finally, note that the
\mathcal{D}
factor is only introduced to deal with the principal value singularity
and is absent in (
5.40c
), (
5.40d
) and (
5.40e
).
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Using Theorem
5.7
in combination with the identity (
5.36
),
and the boundedness of the wave operator
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, see Theorem
2.2
,
will let us estimate the bilinear expressions that arise from
\partial_{\xi}F_{L}
,
and establish the desired bound (
5.11
), as well as (
5.12
).

In our argument we will also need to deal with derivatives of the components of the measure
\mu

and use the following remark.

Remark 5.9

(Derivative of
\mu_{1}^{S}
)
.

When estimating
\partial_{\xi}
of the expressions in (
5.6
) we also need to
take care of the differentiation of the measure
\mu
, and, in particular, of the leading order
singular component
\mu_{1}^{S}
, see (
5.16
).
Notice that derivatives of the regular component are not so harmful, essentially by definition;
see, for example, (
5.19
).

Note first that, for a function
b
, we can write

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}b(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma-\xi}{|\sigma-\xi|}\,\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}b(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}b(|\eta+\sigma|-|\xi|)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\,\frac{\eta+\sigma}{|\eta+\sigma|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}b(|\eta+\sigma|-|\xi|).

Second, from (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) we see that
\mu_{1}^{S}
is a linear combination
of terms of the form

\displaystyle\nu_{S}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma)=\frac{1}{|-\xi+\eta|}\nu(|-\xi+\eta|-|\sigma|)m_{0}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma),

\displaystyle\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)=\frac{1}{|-\xi+\sigma|}\nu(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)m_{0}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta),

\displaystyle\nu_{S}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)=\frac{1}{|-\eta-\sigma|}\nu(|-\eta-\sigma|-|\xi|)m_{0}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi),

where
\nu
is some distribution (either
\delta
or
\mathrm{p.v.}
) or a Schwartz function,
and
m_{0}
denote suitable symbols, as described in Proposition
5.6
above;
for example,
m_{0}
may be the coefficient/symbol
b_{0}
in (
5.18
) satisfying (
B.5
),
or any of the other rather harmless coefficients/symbols appearing in the statement.

Then, we have the following distributional identities
for the derivatives of these singular expressions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{\xi}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma)&=-\nabla_{\eta}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma),\\
\nabla_{\xi}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)&=\frac{\xi-\sigma}{|\xi-\sigma|}\,\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\Big[\nu(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)\Big]\frac{m_{0}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)}{|-\xi+\sigma|}\\
&+\nu(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)\nabla_{\xi}\Big[\frac{m_{0}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)}{|-\xi+\sigma|}\Big],\\
\nabla_{\xi}\nu_{S}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)&=-\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\,\frac{\eta+\sigma}{|\eta+\sigma|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\Big[\nu(|\eta+\sigma|-|\xi|)\Big]\,\frac{m_{0}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)}{|\eta+\sigma|}\\
&+\nu(|\eta-\sigma|-|\xi|)\frac{\nabla_{\xi}m_{0}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)}{|\eta+\sigma|}.\end{split}

(5.41)

The first formula above can be used directly to integrate by parts,
thus removing the
\nabla_{\xi}
on the distribution by transforming it into a
\nabla_{\eta}
derivative.

Next, let us inspect the second formula in (
5.41
);
the analysis for the third one is identical.
We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi-\sigma}{|\xi-\sigma|}\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)+\nu_{S}^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)

(5.42)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{S}^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=&-\frac{\xi-\sigma}{|\xi-\sigma|}\,\nu(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)\,\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\cdot\frac{\nabla_{\eta}m_{0}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)}{|-\xi+\sigma|}\\
&+\nu(|-\xi+\sigma|-|\eta|)\nabla_{\xi}\Big[\frac{m_{0}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)}{|-\xi+\sigma|}\Big]\end{split}

(5.43)

In other words,

\nabla_{\xi}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)
can be written as the sum of two terms:

1.
\nabla_{\eta}\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)
times harmless factors involving symbols of Riesz transforms, and

2. A distribution,
\nu^{\prime}_{S}
, that looks exactly like
\nu_{S}
itself
(it has the same singular structure) up to possible extra singular factor of
1/|\eta|
or
1/|-\xi+\sigma|

(these have the same size on the support of the
\nu_{S}
-type distributions under consideration).

The first term will be handled by integrating by parts in
\eta
, and we will detail
how to bound the resulting contributions. The presence of the symbols of the Riesz transforms
is harmless since we will always be in a situation where the respective variables
are localized at fixed dyadic scales.
Also, the differentiation of the factor
\eta/|\eta|
upon integration by parts
can be handled via Hardy’s inequality;
in particular it has the same effect of differentiating the input profile,
which is already included in our treatment in view of the integration by parts in
\eta
just described above.

The operators associated to the distribution
\nu_{S}^{\prime}
satisfy the same estimates as that of the operators associated to
\nu_{S}
up possible extra singular factors
of
2^{-K},
where
2^{K}
is the size of the input
variables
|-\xi+\sigma|\approx|\eta|
.
Once again, this extra factor can be handled through Hardy’s inequality.
Therefore, we can systematically disregard the remainder-type distributions
\nu^{\prime}_{S}
.
The above considerations show that we can effectively utilize the identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\nabla_{\xi}\mu_{1}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\approx\nabla_{\eta}\mu_{1}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\approx\nabla_{\sigma}\mu_{1}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\end{split}

(5.44)

where
\approx
is up to harmless terms as described above.

Finally, since we can write

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}b(|\xi|+\epsilon_{1}|\eta|+\epsilon_{2}|\sigma|)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{1}\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}b(|\xi|+\epsilon_{1}|\eta|+\epsilon_{2}|\sigma|)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{2}\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\frac{\sigma}{|\sigma|}\cdot\nabla_{\sigma}b(|\xi|+\epsilon_{1}|\eta|+\epsilon_{2}|\sigma|),

we deduce, in the same sense of (
5.44
), that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\nabla_{\xi}\mu_{i}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\approx\nabla_{\eta}\mu_{i}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\approx\nabla_{\sigma}\mu_{i}^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\qquad i=2,3.\end{split}

(5.45)

5.4.
Singular vs. Regular decomposition of nonlinear terms

Consistently with the decomposition in Proposition
5.6
, we now split the nonlinear terms
F_{L}

(which we will often just call
F
for better legibility). Recalling (
5.6
)-(
5.8
), and recall the mid-low frequency localizations,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&F_{L}=F^{S}+F^{R}+F^{Re},\qquad F^{\ast}=\sum_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{\pm\}}{\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}F_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}^{\ast}\big(f_{{\epsilon}_{1}},f_{{\epsilon}_{2}}\big),\\
&F_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}^{\ast}(a,b):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{a}(s,\eta)\widetilde{b}(s,\sigma)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)\,\mu^{\ast}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,d\eta d\sigma ds,\qquad\ast\in\{S,R,Re\}.\end{split}

(5.46)

The next lemma shows that, on the support of the singular distribution
\mu^{S}
the phases of the operators (
5.13
) are lower bounded, up to a small loss depending
on the high-low frequencies cutoff at scale
2^{M_{0}}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

On the support of the integral defining
F^{S}
,
see (
5.46
) and the definition of
\mu^{S}
in Proposition
5.6
,
we have (recall also the notation (
5.7
))

\displaystyle\big|\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\big|\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle},

(5.47)

for all
{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\in\{+,-\}
.
Moreover we have the (weak) symbol type bounds

\displaystyle\bigg|\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)\nabla_{\xi}^{a}\nabla_{\eta}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\sigma}^{\beta}\frac{1}{\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\bigg|\lesssim 2^{M_{0}(|a|+|\alpha|+|\beta|+1)}.

(5.48)

Remark 5.11
.

The bound (
5.48
) is far from optimal since we allow growth for large frequencies,
but it suffices for our purposes since frequencies are not very large compared to the time variable.
Moreover, we need to allow this type of weak symbol bounds in any case
when we differentiate the coefficients appearing in the distribution
\mu^{S}
.

Proof.

The estimate (
5.47
) in the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(-,-)
is obvious.
To prove (
5.47
) on the support of
\delta_{0}(\xi-\eta-\sigma)
for the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(-,+)
we write

\displaystyle\Phi_{-,+}(\xi,\eta,\xi-\eta)

\displaystyle=\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle-\langle\eta\rangle\geqslant\frac{1+2\big(-|\xi||\xi-\eta|+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle\langle\xi\rangle\big)}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle}\bigg(1+2\frac{1+|\xi-\eta|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}}{|\xi||\xi-\eta|+\langle\xi\rangle\langle\xi-\eta\rangle}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle}.

(
5.47
) for the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(+,-)
follows by exchanging the roles of
\eta
and
\xi-\eta
,
and for the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(+,+)
by exchanging the role of
\xi
and
\eta
.

Let us now look at the case of the principal value distribution
\mu_{1}^{S}
,
see (
5.16
)-(
5.18
).
We begin with the estimate on the support of
\nu_{1}^{S}(-\xi+\eta,\sigma)
.
In this region we have

\displaystyle|\langle\xi-\eta\rangle-\langle\sigma\rangle|\leqslant||\eta-\xi|-|\sigma||\leqslant c2^{-M_{0}}\leqslant c\langle\max(|\xi|,|\eta|,|\sigma|)\rangle^{-1},

for some
c\leqslant 1/8
, so that

\displaystyle|\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle|\leqslant|\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle|+c\langle\max(|\xi|,|\eta|,|\sigma|)\rangle^{-1}\leqslant 2|\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle|.

For the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(-,+)
, using the inequalities above, we have

\displaystyle\big|\Phi_{-,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant\big|\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle-\langle\xi-\eta\rangle\big|-\big|\langle\xi-\eta\rangle-\langle\sigma\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle}-c\langle\max(|\xi|,|\eta|,|\sigma|)\rangle^{-1}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle}.

Since
\Phi_{+,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=-\Phi_{+,-}(\eta,\xi,\sigma)
,
we are left with the case
({\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}})=(+,+)
. We can use an argument similar to the one above
by writing
\Phi_{+,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\Phi_{+,+}(\xi,\eta,\xi-\eta)-\langle\sigma\rangle+\langle\xi-\eta\rangle
,
and using the lower bound (
5.47
) with
\sigma=\xi-\eta
just proved above.

The proof of (
5.47
) on the support of
\nu_{S}(-\xi+\sigma,\eta)
follows similarly by writing

\displaystyle\big|\Phi_{-,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant\big|\Phi_{-,+}(\xi,\xi-\sigma,\sigma)\big|-\big|\langle\eta\rangle-\langle\xi-\sigma\rangle\big|

and using the bound (
5.47
) with
\eta=\xi-\sigma

and
\big|\langle\eta\rangle-\langle\xi-\sigma\rangle\big|\leqslant c\langle\max(|\xi|,|\eta|,|\sigma|)\rangle^{-1}

on the support of the distribution.
Exchanging the roles of
\xi
and
\sigma
proves the desired lower bound for

\Phi_{+,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=-\Phi_{-,+}(\sigma,\eta,\xi)
.
The remaining case of
\Phi_{+,-}
can be treated relying again on
the lower bound for
\Phi_{+,-}(\xi,\xi-\sigma,\sigma)
.
Also on the support of
\nu_{S}(-\eta-\sigma,\xi)
analogous arguments apply.

Now we prove (
5.47
) on the support of
\mu_{i}^{S},i=2,3
; see (
5.20
) and (
5.23
).
We start with the basic inequality

\displaystyle\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle-\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma\rangle-\frac{|\sigma|^{2}+2|\eta||\sigma|}{\langle\eta\rangle+\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle+\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle}\Bigg(\frac{1+|\sigma|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}{\langle\sigma\rangle\langle\eta\rangle+|\sigma||\eta|}+\frac{1+|\sigma|^{2}+||\eta|+|\sigma||^{2}}{\langle\sigma\rangle\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle+|\sigma|(|\sigma|+|\eta|)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle+\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle}.

Next, note that on the support of
\mu_{i}^{S}

there exist
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}\in\{+,-\},(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon_{2}^{\prime})\neq(+,+)

such that
\big||\xi|+\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}|\eta|+\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}|\sigma|\big|\leqslant c2^{-M_{0}}

for some small constant
c
.
Starting with the case
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}=\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}=-
, we write that

\displaystyle|\Phi_{+,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)|

\displaystyle\geqslant\big|\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle+\epsilon_{1}\langle\eta\rangle+\epsilon_{2}\langle\sigma\rangle\big|-\big|\big\langle\xi\rangle-\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle+\langle|\eta|+|\sigma|\rangle}-c2^{-M_{0}}\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle}.

The other cases are deduced from this computation after permutation of the variables.
To treat the case
({\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(-,+)
we exchange the roles of variables
\xi
and
\sigma
and write,
using the previous inequality,

\displaystyle|\Phi_{-,+}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)|=|\Phi_{+,+}(\sigma,\eta,\xi)|\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\eta\rangle+\langle\sigma\rangle}.

To treat the case
({\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(+,-)
we write
|\Phi_{+-}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)|=|\Phi_{++}(\eta,\xi,\sigma)|

and obtain the result exchanging the roles of
\xi
and
\eta.

The other combinations of signs
{\epsilon}_{1}^{\prime},{\epsilon}_{2}^{\prime}
are obtained similarly.

The weak symbol bound (
5.48
) directly follows from (
5.47
),
simply by noting the basic fact that
\nabla_{x}^{a}\langle x\rangle
is uniformly bounded for
a\geqslant 1.

∎

6.
Singular quadratic terms

The goal of this section is to estimate as in (
5.11
) and (
5.12
)
the singular terms
F^{S}
defined in (
5.46
).

6.1.
Set-up

We begin by decomposing the integrals inserting a time localization
(see the notation in §
1.8
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{S}_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}(G,H)&=\sum_{m=0}^{L+1}F^{S}_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},m}(G,H)\\
F^{S}_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},m}(G,H)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{G}(s,\eta)\widetilde{H}(s,\sigma)\\
&\qquad\times\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma ds,\end{split}

(6.1)

recall (
5.4
).

Remark 6.1

(Notation simplification)
.

We may sometime dispense of some of the indexes, such as the ‘
m
’ indicating the dyadic time scale
in our notation for the quadratic terms (
6.1
), and for similar ones, in order to improve the legibility.
Also, since all bounds are obvious for
m=0
(or finite fixed
m
), we may assume without loss of generality
that
m\geqslant 1
.

Since the signs
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}
also play a minor role we may sometimes omit them too.
We are also often omitting the dependence on
(t,\xi)
when this is obvious, for example in the case of expressions like the one on the left-hand side of (
6.1
).
We may adopt other similar simplifications in the course of our argument to lighten up the notation.

We learn from the inequalities in Lemma
5.10

that the phases in the operators
F^{S}_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},m}
are lower bounded;
more precisely they are lower bounded by
C2^{-M_{0}}
.
Therefore, we can integrate by parts in
s
to see that

\displaystyle F^{S}_{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},m}(G,H)=T_{m}^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}(G,H)+T_{m}^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}(G,H)+\mbox{`symmetric and easier'},

(6.2)

where we defined

\displaystyle T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(G,H)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{i\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)

(6.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\partial_{s}\widetilde{G}(s,\eta)\,\widetilde{H}(s,\sigma)\,\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma ds,

‘symmetric’ denotes an analogous term where
\partial_{s}
hits
\widetilde{H}
instead of
\widetilde{G}
,
and

\displaystyle T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}_{m}(G,H)

\displaystyle:=\tau_{m}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{e^{-it\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}}{-i\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)

(6.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\widetilde{G}(t,\eta)\widetilde{H}(t,\sigma)\,\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma;

the ‘easier’ term is the one where
\partial_{s}
hits the cutoff
\tau_{m}
.
The first operator is a bulk term (an integral over space and time),
while the second is a (time-)boundary term.
We can obviously disregard the ‘symmetric and easier’ terms without loss of generality.

To obtain the desired bounds in Propositions
5.1
and
5.2

for the singular bilinear terms
F^{S}
in (
5.46
),
also
according to (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)-(
6.4
), it then suffices to prove

Proposition 6.2

(Estimates for the Singular part)
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
4.13
), for all
m=0,1,\dots,L+1
, for all
{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\in\{+,-\}
, and for
j=1,2
,
we have

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(j)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(2^{m}),\qquad G,H\in\{g,h\},

(6.5)

for some
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta/2).

Moreover, there exists
0<a<1
such that, for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle{\left\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}\big[T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(j)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})\big]\right\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a+\delta^{\prime}}},\qquad G,H\in\{g,h\}.

(6.6)

Let us introduce the following convenient quantity:

\displaystyle Z=Z_{\beta}({\varepsilon},m):=\big(2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\big)^{1-\beta}\big(2^{m}\varepsilon\big)^{\beta}.

(6.7)

Recall that
\rho(2^{m})\approx\min(2^{-m},{\varepsilon}^{2})

so that we have, for
2^{m}\approx t
,

\displaystyle Z\approx\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}t^{\beta},&\quad t\gtrsim{\varepsilon}^{-2},\\
\\
{\varepsilon}^{2-\beta}t,&\quad t\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{-2}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 6.3

(About (
6.7
), (
6.5
) and “acceptable” upperbounds)
.

First note that the a priori assumption (
1.47
) can be written as follows:
for all
t\approx 2^{m}
, with
2^{m}\gtrsim\rho_{0}^{-1/2}\approx{\varepsilon}^{-1}

(i.e., past the trivial local-existence time)

\displaystyle{\big\|\nabla_{\xi}\widetilde{h}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\leqslant C{\varepsilon}^{\beta}2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta}(2^{m})=CZ_{\beta}({\varepsilon},m).

(6.8)

For
t\approx 2^{m}
, the right-hand side of (
6.5
)
in terms of
Z
reads

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}^{\beta}t\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(t)\approx Z\cdot\rho^{\delta^{\prime}}(t).

(6.9)

In the sequel we will encounter two types of
“acceptable” upperbounds for the left-hand side of (
6.5
):

•

Bounds by
2^{-am}Z
for some
a>0,

which are stronger than the right-hand side of (
5.11
) for
a>2\delta^{\prime}
, see (
6.9
),
and since we will only consider times past
\rho_{0}^{-1/2}
;
see Remark
4.3
which deals with the local existence times
t\lesssim\rho_{0}^{-1/2}\approx{\varepsilon}^{-1}
.

•

Bounds like
2^{-am}Z^{2}

which are again stronger than the right-hand side of (
6.5
)
provided
a>\beta+\delta^{\prime}
, since
Z\lesssim 2^{\beta m}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
,
and we can choose
2\delta^{\prime}<\beta
.

Notice that we can pick
\beta
and
\delta^{\prime}
arbitrarily small,
and, in particular, small enough depending on the exponent ‘
a
’ appearing in the
two types of bounds described above, which we will obtain from the nonlinear estimates.
A convenient choice is to let
2\delta^{\prime}<\beta<a/2
.

To prove (
6.6
) in the case of
j=1
, that is, for the bulk operator (
6.3
),
we will use the following result:

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume that for some
a\ll 1
such that
a>5\delta_{N}
we have

\displaystyle{\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}I(s)\right\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle s\rangle^{1+2a}},

(6.10)

then

\displaystyle{\Big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{\leqslant\delta_{N}m+5}(\xi)e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}I(s,\xi)\,\tau_{m}(s)ds\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a+\delta^{\prime}}},

(6.11)

for
\delta^{\prime}<\delta_{N}
.

We will apply this Lemma with

\displaystyle I(t)

\displaystyle=e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})(t),

(6.12)

so that, after verifying the bound (
6.10
) for this choice of
I
,
and using the fact that for
s\approx 2^{m}
the frequency integral in (
6.3
) is already localized
in
|\xi|\leqslant 2^{\delta_{N}m+3}
, i.e.

\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(s)=\varphi_{\leqslant\delta_{N}m+5}(\xi)\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(s),

we will obtain the desired
(
6.6
).

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

Using the (distorted) Bernstein’s inequality (with
|\xi|\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{\delta_{N}}
),
the
L^{8}
decay estimate from Lemma
2.5
,
and Bernstein again, we find that

\displaystyle{\Big\|e^{itL}\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi\mapsto x}^{-1}\big(\varphi_{\leqslant\delta_{N}m+5}(\xi)e^{-is\langle\xi\rangle}I(s,\xi)\big)\,\tau_{m}(s)ds\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{3\delta_{N}/8}{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)L}P_{\leqslant\delta_{N}m+5}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi\mapsto x}^{-1}\big(I(\xi,s)\big)\,\tau_{m}(s)ds\Big\|}_{L^{8}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{2\delta_{N}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{9/8}}\langle s\rangle^{2\delta_{N}}{\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi\mapsto x}^{-1}I(\xi,s)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\,\tau_{m}(s)ds.

Notice that in the inequality above we have also used Bernstein to deal with the integral
from
t-1
to
t
, passing from
L^{8}_{x}
to
L_{x}^{2}
, using Minkowski, the unitarity of
e^{itL}

and then Bernstein again from
L^{2}_{x}
to
L^{8/7}_{x}
.
Then we conclude using the boundedness of wave operators, the assumption (
6.10
)
and the fact that

\displaystyle\langle t\rangle^{2\delta_{N}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\langle t-s\rangle^{9/8}}\langle s\rangle^{2\delta_{N}}\frac{1}{\langle s\rangle^{1+2a}}ds\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1+2a-4\delta_{N}}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a+\delta^{\prime}}},

for
\delta^{\prime}+4\delta_{N}<a
.
∎

Lemma
6.4
allows us to systematically reduce the proof of (
6.6
)
to
L^{8/7}_{x}
bounds for the inverse transform of the right-hand side of (
6.12
),
that is:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tau_{m}(s)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}&\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)}{\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\,e^{i{\epsilon}_{1}s\langle\eta\rangle}e^{i{\epsilon}_{2}s\langle\sigma\rangle}\\
&\times\partial_{s}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}(s,\eta)\,\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}(s,\sigma)\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma,\qquad G,H\in\{g,h\}.\end{split}

(6.13)

Note that this expression gives bilinear terms like those appearing in (
5.34
), where the inputs are (up to complex conjugation) of the form

(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(e^{-isL}\partial_{s}G),\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(e^{-isL}H))
.
Therefore, since
e^{-isL}\partial_{s}g
and
e^{-isL}\partial_{s}h

are quadratic in
A
and
f
, and in view of the product estimates of Theorem
5.7

the terms (
6.13
) should be essentially thought of as cubic terms in
A
and
f
.

A similar lemma that we will use to prove (
6.6
)
for the boundary-type operators (
6.4
) is the following:

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume that

\displaystyle{\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}J(t)\right\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\rho_{0}^{a},

(6.14)

then, for
\delta_{N}
small enough, one has

\displaystyle{\Big\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}\big[\varphi_{\leqslant\delta_{N}\log_{2}\langle t\rangle+10}(\xi)J(t,\xi)\big]\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{10/9}}.

(6.15)

The proof follows the same steps as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
so we skip it.

In the rest of this subsection, the goal is to prove Proposition
6.2
, which will then imply the main bounds
(
5.2
) and (
5.3
) for the terms
F^{S}
through (
6.2
).

6.2.
Estimates for the boundary terms

We start by treating the easier terms that involve the operators (
6.4
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
6.2
, we have

\displaystyle{\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})\big\|}_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(2^{m}),\qquad G,H\in\{g,h\},

(6.16)

for some
0<\delta^{\prime}<\beta/2
.

Moreover, there exists
a>\delta^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle{\left\|e^{itL}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})\right\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{a}}{\langle t\rangle^{1+a+\delta^{\prime}}},\qquad G,H\in\{h,g\}.

(6.17)

Proof.

We first prove the more difficult estimate (
6.16
), and then the simpler (
6.17
).

Proof of (
6.16
)
.
We subdivide the proof into three steps.

Step 1
.
When applying
\partial_{\xi}
to
T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}
,
we claim that is suffices to focus on the worst case when the derivative hits the exponential.
In fact, the terms where the frequency derivative hits the symbol
1/\Phi
or the cutoff
\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}

are clearly much easier to treat, also in view of (
5.47
). When instead
\partial_{\xi}
hits the measure
\mu
we can use Remark
5.9
to argue as follows:

1. For the
\delta
contribution we integrate by parts using the distributional identity

\partial_{\xi}\delta(\xi-\eta-\sigma)=-\partial_{\eta}\delta(\xi-\eta-\sigma)
.
This generates:

(a) a term where
\partial_{\eta}
hits the exponential,
which is essentially identical to the leading order term that we will treat,

(b) some other easier terms where the symbols and cutoff are differentiated,
and

(c) a term where
\partial_{\eta}
hits
\widetilde{f}
.
This latter is the only non-trivial term, although it is still easier to estimate than
the term where the differentiation falls on the exponential;
we will treat this term in details together with the main one.

2. For the
\mu_{1}^{S}
parts, we can use the identities (
5.41
)
to do similar integration by parts and obtain terms like those described above.
The analogues of (a) and (b) above are all substantially easier to bound
than the main term (where the derivative hits the phase) and the analogue of the term in (c);
below we are going to treat in full details these two types of terms.

According to the discussion above,
the main terms to estimate from
\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(2)}_{m}(G_{{\epsilon}_{1}},H_{{\epsilon}_{2}})
are

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{1}(t,\xi)&:=t\,\tau_{m}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}e^{-it\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\widetilde{G}(t,\eta)\widetilde{H}(t,\sigma)\,\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma,\\
&b(\xi,\eta,\sigma):=\frac{\xi}{\langle\xi\rangle}\frac{\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)}{\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)},\end{split}

(6.18)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{2}(t,\xi)&:=\tau_{m}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}e^{-it\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\partial_{\eta}\widetilde{G}(t,\eta)\widetilde{H}(t,\sigma)\,\,b^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma,\end{split}

(6.19)

where
b^{\prime}
denotes any of the symbols that are
obtained when performing the integration by parts manipulation described above;
see Remark
5.9
and (
5.44
),
and the exact definitions in (
6.26
) below.

Step 2
.
Next, we compare the formula in (
6.18
) and the identity (
5.36
)
with the definitions of the operators in (
5.34
) and insert additional localization in
|\eta|\approx 2^{k_{1}}
and
|\sigma|\approx 2^{k_{2}}

in the integrals in their definitions; we can then estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}\big(e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}B_{1}(t)\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\big\|t\,T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}+5}\Big(\big\|t\,T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|t\,T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)\\
&+\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big(\big\|t\,T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{2}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\big\|t\,T_{2}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|t\,T_{3}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)\end{split}

(6.20)

where, using a notation similar to that of (
5.35
) (but with the additional frequency localization) we let

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{0}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&:=b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\sigma),\\
b_{1}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&:=b(\xi,\xi-\eta,\sigma)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\sigma),\\
b_{1}^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&:=b(\xi,\sigma,\xi-\eta)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\sigma),\\
b_{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&:=b(\xi,-\eta-\sigma,\sigma)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\sigma).\end{split}

(6.21)

Note the following:

1.

We are not indicating explicitly the dependence of the symbols in (
6.21
) on
k_{1},k_{2}
,
but we are dealing with integrals in
d\eta d\sigma
(as in (
5.34
)) with localization
to
|\eta|\approx 2^{k_{1}}
and
|\sigma|\approx 2^{k_{2}}
.

2.

The restriction
|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5

for the
T_{1}^{S,1}
operators comes from the formula (
5.17
), and the indicator functions
in the bounds (
B.5
) and (
B.7
).

3.

The restriction
k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}
(and, similarly, the restriction
k_{1}\leqslant M_{0}
for the other terms)
comes from the restriction to inputs and outputs in the symbol of (
6.18
).
Note that, to be precise, we should write
M_{0}+3
instead of
M_{0}
in some cases,
but we do not make such a distinction as it is clearly not relevant.

Using (
5.48
),
we see that
b
as in (
6.18
)
satisfies (
B.29
) in Theorem
B.2
with the choice of
A=M_{0}
,
and so do all the symbols (
6.21
).
In particular, we can apply Theorem
5.7
to the terms in (
6.20
).

More precisely, using (
5.40b
) we can write, for
|t|\approx 2^{m}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&t\,{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{m}\cdot{\big\|e^{itL}G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}\big\|}_{L^{3}}{\|e^{itL}P_{k_{2}}H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}\|}_{L^{6-}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big),\end{split}

(6.22)

having used the boundedness of wave operators as well.
To estimate the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.22
) for any
G,H\in\{g,h\}
,
we use the bounds (
4.19
) from Lemma
4.4
to get, for
|t|\approx 2^{m}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|e^{itL}G\big\|}_{L^{3}}{\|e^{itL}P_{k_{2}}H\|}_{L^{6-}}&\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}2^{m/2+2\delta_{N}m}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\cdot\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}2^{2\delta_{N}m}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\\
&\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2-2\beta}2^{3m/4}\varepsilon^{2\beta}=2^{-5m/4}Z^{2},\end{split}

(6.23)

where
Z
is defined in (
6.7
).
Note that this bound is consistent with a bound by
2^{-am}Z^{2}
for the term in (
6.22
), with
a=1/4
,
which is an acceptable bound (see Remark
6.3
).

Remark 6.7
.

On the support of
T^{S,1}_{1}[b_{1}]
we have that either
k_{2}\sim 1
when
H=g
,
or that
-5m\leqslant k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}
when
H=h
(see Lemma
5.5
).
In all cases the sum has at most
O(m)
terms since
|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5.

An acceptable bound like those in Remark
6.3
for each fixed pair
k_{1},k_{2}

will then yield the desired estimates.
The same reasoning holds for
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]
with
k_{1}
instead of
k_{2}
and
G
instead of
H.

For the
\mathcal{D}
term in (
6.23
), recall the definition (
5.39
), we use the a priori bound on the weighted norm of
h
from (
4.13
),
and the larger bound on the weighted norm of
g
from (
4.26
), as well as (
4.24
) and the

L^{2}
bound on
h
in (
4.13
),
to see that, for all
G,H\in\{g,h\}
and
|t|\approx 2^{m}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}P_{k_{1}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}P_{k_{2}}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)&\lesssim\Big({\big\|\partial_{\xi}\big(\varphi_{k_{1}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\|\varphi_{k_{1}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\|}_{L^{2}}\Big){\big\|\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim m^{2}2^{3m/2}\rho(2^{m})\cdot\big[m2^{m}\rho(2^{m})+{\varepsilon}^{\beta}2^{m}\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}\big]\\
&\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2-2\beta}2^{5m/2+}\varepsilon^{2\beta}\lesssim 2^{m/2+}Z^{2}.\end{split}

(6.24)

This bound is consistent with the assumption on
D
(see above (
5.39
))
if we choose, for example,
D=5m
; indeed, our current choice is
A=M_{0}
,
where
2^{M_{0}}\approx 2^{\delta_{N}m}
(see (
5.4
))
with
\delta_{N}\ll 1
that can be chosen sufficiently small
so to ensure
20M_{0}\leqslant 5m\leqslant 2^{M_{0}/5}
which suffices.
The second term on the right-hand side of (
6.22
) is then bounded by
2^{-4m}Z^{2}
.

The same argument used for
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]
above applies verbatim to
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]
,
and to the simpler case of
T_{0}[b_{0}]
.
A similar argument can be applied to the operators
T_{i}^{S}[b_{0}],i=2,3
as well.
The only difference is that there is no additional restriction on indices in the sum for these terms,
except for
-5m\leqslant k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}
;
see the formulas (
5.34d
)-(
5.34e
), the definition of
b_{0}
in (
6.21
)
and Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
.
We can then use (
5.40d
) for each fixed pair
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
and
sum over these indexes at the expense of a harmless
O(m^{2})
factor.
Note that there are no
\mathcal{D}
terms in (
5.40d
).

For the last term
T_{1}^{S,2}
, we can use an almost identical argument
based on the bilinear bound (
5.40b
);
the only minor difference is the need to additionally localize in
|\xi|\approx 2^{K}

to be able to use (
5.40b
). But this can also be easily taken care of applying Bernstein for
K\leqslant 0
and estimating the
L^{2-}
norm
of
P_{K}T_{2}[b_{2}]
, as well as using that
K\leqslant M_{0}
;
the (arbitrarily) small loss of
2^{M_{0}}
resulting from this
can be absorbed by the bounds (
6.23
) and (
6.24
).

In conclusion,
putting together (
6.23
) and (
6.24
), and the similar bounds for all the other terms
on the right-hand side of (
6.20
), we have obtained

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|B_{1}(t)\|}_{L^{2}}&\lesssim\big(2^{m}2^{C_{0}M_{0}}\cdot 2^{-5m/4}Z^{2}+2^{-4m}Z^{2}\big)2^{(0+)m}\lesssim 2^{-m/5}Z^{2},\end{split}

provided
C_{0}M_{0}
is small enough (that is,
\delta_{N}
is small enough), which is sufficient.

Step 3.

To estimate the
L^{2}
norm of (
6.19
) we can use very similar arguments.
Note that this terms does not carry a factor of
t
in front, and is therefore easier to handle.
We begin with the analogue of (
6.20
), that is,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}\big(e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}B_{2}(t)\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\big\|\,T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\big\|T_{1}^{S,2}[m_{2}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}},\\
&+\big\|T_{2}^{S}[m_{0}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|T_{3}^{S}[m_{0}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big(\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\big\|\,T_{1}^{S,1}[m_{1}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)\end{split}

(6.25)

where we are implicitly summing over
j=1,2,3
in the last three lines,
and the symbols are

\displaystyle\begin{split}m_{0}^{j}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\frac{\eta_{j}}{|\eta|}b_{0}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\qquad m_{1}^{j}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)=\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{|\sigma|}b_{1}^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma),\\
m_{2}^{j}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)&=\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\frac{\eta_{j}}{|\eta|}b_{2}(\xi,\eta,\sigma);\end{split}

(6.26)

see Remark
5.9
and the definitions (
6.21
).

To estimate the terms in (
6.25
) we use the bilinear bounds (
5.40
)
proceeding as follows:
For the case when
G=h
in (
6.25
) we do an
L^{2}\times L^{6}
estimate
using the a priori bound on
\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}
from (
4.13
)
and the
L^{6}
decay bounds in Lemma
4.4
;
for the case when
G=g
in (
6.25
) we can use instead (
4.26
)
and again the
L^{6}
estimates in Lemma
4.4
.
The term
\mathcal{D}
that appears on the right-hand side of (
5.40b
)
is a fast decaying remainder that
can be handled similarly to (
6.24
); exploiting the minimum in the definition
(
5.39
) we only differentiate the input that does not already carry the weight,
so to never have the appearance of
\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widetilde{h}
.

Proof of (
6.17
)
.
In view of Lemma
6.5
it suffices to prove, see the formula (
6.4
), that

\displaystyle{\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}J(t)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\rho_{0}^{a},

(6.27)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}J(t)&=\tau_{m}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}\frac{\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\xi\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\eta\rangle)\varphi_{\leqslant M_{0}}(\langle\sigma\rangle)}{\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\langle\eta\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\\
&\qquad\times e^{i{\epsilon}_{1}t\langle\eta\rangle}\widetilde{G}(t,\eta)e^{i{\epsilon}_{1}t\langle\sigma\rangle}\widetilde{H}(t,\sigma)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma.\end{split}

(6.28)

Choosing
b
as in (
6.18
), but without the
\xi/\langle\xi\rangle
factor,
and with the same definitions (
6.21
), we have identities analogous to (
6.20
).
It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}J(t)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\\
&\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\big\|T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}+\big\|T_{2}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\\
&+\big\|T_{3}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}+\big\|T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{2}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\\
&+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}+\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}.\end{split}

(6.29)

The estimate (
5.40b
) gives us

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|e^{itL}G\big\|}_{L^{8/3-}}{\|e^{itL}P_{k_{2}}H\|}_{L^{2}}\cdot 2^{(C_{0}+1)M_{0}}+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{G_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{H_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big).\end{split}

(6.30)

Then we can use the
L^{q}
dispersive estimate in Lemma
4.4

to obtain (recall Remark
6.7
), for all
t\approx 2^{m}
,

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{itL}G\big\|}_{L^{8/3-}}{\|e^{itL}P_{k_{2}}H\|}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}2^{5m/8+10\delta_{N}m}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\cdot\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}2^{m}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2-2\beta}2^{7m/4}{\varepsilon}^{2\beta}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{2a},

for some
a>2\beta
, which is consistent with (
6.27
).
All the other terms in (
6.29
) can be estimated similarly as above,
using (
5.40a
)-(
5.40d
).
The
\mathcal{D}
factor is estimated by (
6.24
).
These bounds and (
6.30
)
give us (
6.27
) for
a
small enough, provided
M_{0}
is small enough.
∎

In the remainder of this section we estimate the integrated terms (
6.3
) by distinguishing the three different types of interactions described in §
5.1.3
.
Recall the definition of the bulk operator (
6.3
),
and that we are aiming to prove the
L^{2}
weighted bound (
6.5
)
and the decay bound (
6.6
) (via Lemma
6.4
).

6.3.
Fermi interactions

We first look at the interactions that involve only the Fermi component
g
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
6.2
, for
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{+,-\}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,g)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(2^{m}){\varepsilon}^{\beta},

(6.31)

\displaystyle{\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,g)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{0}^{1/2}}{\langle t\rangle^{11/8}},

(6.32)

for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
.

Proof.

Proof of (
6.31
)
.
Arguing as at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
6.6

we can reduce to bounding the
L^{2}
-norm of the two main terms,
that is those where
\partial_{\xi}
hits the exponential in the formula for
T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,g)
, see (
6.3
),
and where the derivatives hits
\widetilde{g}
.
Let us denote these two types of terms by

\displaystyle C_{1}(t,\xi):=C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}](t,\xi),\qquad C_{2}(t,\xi):=C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}](t,\xi),

(6.33)

where we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[G,H](t,\xi):=\int_{0}^{t}s\,\tau_{m}(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\partial_{s}\widetilde{G}(s,\eta)\,\widetilde{H}(s,\sigma)\,\\
\times\,b(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma\,ds,\\
C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[G,H](t,\xi):=\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{6}}e^{-is\Phi_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)}\partial_{s}\widetilde{G}(s,\eta)\,\partial_{\sigma}\widetilde{H}(s,\sigma)\,\\
\times\,b^{\prime}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)\,\mu^{S}(\xi,\eta,\sigma)d\eta d\sigma\,ds,\end{split}

(6.34)

and
b,b^{\prime}
are defined as in (
6.18
), (
6.19
) (see also
the more precise (
6.26
)).
Notice that, as done before, when reducing to the terms (
6.33
)-(
6.34
) we have
taken into account the case where
\partial_{\xi}
hits the distribution
\mu^{S}
;
in this case the derivative can be converted
into
\partial_{\sigma}
through formulas like (
5.41
)-(
5.44
) with the roles of
\eta
and
\sigma
exchanged,
and an integration by parts in
\sigma
can be performed without the need to differentiate
\partial_{s}\widetilde{g}
in
\eta
.

Estimates for the
C_{1}
terms
.
We can proceed similarly to the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
In particular, we let
b
be the same symbol defined in (
6.18
)
and use the same notation in (
6.21
)
so that we can estimate similarly to (
6.20
).
As in (
6.21
), we introduce frequency localizations
in
\eta,\sigma
.
Notice that since
g
is localized at frequency
\sim 1
(see (
4.5
)),
the sums over
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are finite for all the terms except
T_{1}^{S,2}.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}C_{1}(t)\Big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{2m}\sup_{k_{1},k_{2}\approx 1}\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[{\big\|T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&+\sum_{i=2,3}{\big\|T_{i}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&+{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}+{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&+2^{2m}\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\sum_{k_{1}<5,k_{2}\approx 1}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\Big].\end{split}

(6.35)

Let us look at the
T_{1}^{S,1}
term on the right-hand side above first. Using (
5.40b
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}&\lesssim{\big\|e^{isL}P_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{3-}}{\|e^{isL}P_{k_{2}}g\|}_{L^{6}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}\\
&+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big).\end{split}

(6.36)

From the dispersive estimates (
4.17
) and (
4.21
), we see that, for all
s\approx 2^{m}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{3-}}{\|e^{isL}g\|}_{L^{6}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}&\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2}\cdot m\cdot 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\\
&\lesssim 2^{-2m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}m2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\cdot 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\cdot Z,\end{split}

having used
{\varepsilon}^{-\beta}\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}\lesssim 1
.
Multiplied by the factor of
2^{2m}
which appears on the right-hand side of (
6.35
),
the above bound is acceptable (see Remark
6.3
) provided
\delta_{N}
is small enough.

To estimate the second line of (
6.36
) we use (
4.26
)
to bound
\partial_{s}\widetilde{g}
in
L^{2}
,
and use again (
4.21
) to see that, recall the definition (
5.39
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2}\cdot 2^{3m/2}m^{2}.

(6.37)

We can then pick, say,
D=5m
(here
A=M_{0}=m\delta_{N}
) so that the second line of (
6.36
)
is a fast decaying remainder.

Bounds like those just proved for

T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)

can be obtained identically for all the other terms in (
6.35
), which concludes the estimate for
C_{1}
.

Estimates for the
C_{2}
terms
.
For the second integral in (
6.34
), using the same notation as above (see (
6.26
)),
we see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}C_{2}(t)\Big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{m}\sup_{k_{1},k_{2}\approx 1}\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[\big\|T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\big\|T_{2}^{S}[m_{0}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|T_{3}^{S}[m_{0}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&+\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[m_{1}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big]\\
&+2^{m}\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\sum_{k_{1}<5,k_{2}\approx 1}\big\|T_{1}^{S,2}[m_{2}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(6.38)

Once again it suffices to look at the
T_{1}^{S,1}
term above, since the others are identical;
the bilinear estimate (
5.40b
) and the boundedness of wave operators give

\displaystyle\begin{split}2^{m}{\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\|}_{L^{2}}&\lesssim 2^{m}{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{2-}}{\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}(\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g})\|}_{L^{\infty}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}\\
&+2^{m}2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big).\end{split}

(6.39)

The first term above can be bounded using (
4.21
) and (
4.27
):

\displaystyle 2^{m}{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{2-}}{\|e^{{\epsilon}_{2}isL}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g})\|}_{L^{\infty}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\cdot{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}\|}_{L^{1}_{\xi}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\big)^{2}2^{(C_{0}+1)\delta_{N}m}\lesssim 2^{(C_{0}+1)\delta_{N}m}\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}Z,

which suffices for our purposes (see (
6.9
) and Remark
6.3
).

Next we estimate the remainder. We can pick
D=5m
as before and see that the last term on
the right-hand side of (
6.39
) is fast decaying:
using (
4.28
) and (
4.21
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}2^{-D}\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)&\lesssim 2^{-5m}\cdot{\|\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\|}_{L^{2}}\cdot{\|\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\rho(2^{m})^{2}2^{-2m}\lesssim 2^{-4m}Z.\end{split}

(6.40)

This completes the estimate (
6.31
).

Proof of (
6.32
)
.
For the second estimate, using the same notation for
b
as in (
6.18
) and (
6.21
), but without the
\xi/\langle\xi\rangle
factor, we can write

\displaystyle\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,g)\Big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}\sup_{k_{1},k_{2}\approx 1}\Big[\sum_{i=0,2,3}{\big\|T_{i}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}

\displaystyle+{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}+{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\Big]

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}\sum_{k_{1}<5,k_{2}\approx 1}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}.

We focus again on the
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]
term since the others can be treated identically.
Using (
5.40b
), and (
6.40
),

\displaystyle\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|e^{itL}\partial_{t}g\big\|}_{L^{24/17-}_{x}}{\big\|e^{itL}g\big\|}_{L^{6}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot\rho(2^{m})m,

having used (
4.21
) and (
4.17
) in the last inequality.
This is sufficient to obtain a bound as in (
6.32
).
∎

6.4.
Fermi-continuous interaction

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following lemma
which deals with the bilinear (and trilinear) interaction of the good (continuous) and bad (Fermi) components:

Lemma 6.9
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
6.2
, for all
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{+,-\}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,h)\big\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(h,g)\big\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim 2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(2^{m}){\varepsilon}^{\beta},

(6.41)

for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
,
and

\displaystyle{\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,h)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}+{\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(h,g)\big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}\lesssim 2^{3m/4}\rho^{2-3\beta}(2^{m}).

(6.42)

Proof.

We adopt similar notation to those in Lemmas
6.6
and
6.8
above.

Proof of (
6.41
)
.
Applying again the same reduction from the beginning of Lemma
6.6
,
and using the notation from (
6.34
), we can
focus on estimating the
L^{2}
-norms of the terms

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}],\qquad C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[h_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}],\\
C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}],\qquad C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[h_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}].\end{split}

(6.43)

Estimates for the
C_{1}
terms
.
We have analogues of the estimate (
6.35
), with the definitions (
5.34
) and (
6.21
), for all the terms in (
6.43
).
In particular, we see that the first term is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}](t)\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}s\tau_{m}(s)e^{-isL}\Big[\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\\
&+T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)+\sum_{i=2,3}T_{i}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\\
&+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big(T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)+T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\Big)\Big]\,ds\Big\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(6.44)

As before it suffices to treat the contribution from
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]
since the others are almost identical.
An analogue of Remark
6.7
holds in this case and
therefore we may reduce to sum over
k_{1},k_{2}
with only
O(m)
terms.
Note also that a direct application of the bilinear bounds of Theorem
5.7
,
as done before in Lemma
6.8
, would not suffice in this case.
We first apply the Strichartz estimate from Lemma
2.6

with the indices
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r},\gamma)=(2+,\infty-,0+),

and then use (
5.40b
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}s\tau_{m}(s)e^{-isL}T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{3m/2+}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{1+}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{3m/2+}\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{6/5-}}{\|e^{isL}P_{k_{2}}h\|}_{L^{6}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\Big].\end{split}

(6.45)

Using (
4.21
) and (
4.19
)
we obtain an estimate for the first term on the last line of (
6.45
) by

\displaystyle C2^{3m/2}\cdot 2^{(C_{0}+1)\delta_{N}m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}{\varepsilon}^{\beta}

which is sufficient for (
6.41
).
To estimate the contribution from
\mathcal{D}
(recall (
5.39
)) we can use once again the admissible choice
D=5m
,
and the a priori bound (
4.13
) on
\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h}\|_{L^{2}}
and (
4.21
).

To treat the term
C_{1,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[h_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}]
we instead
use first the Strichartz estimates with indices
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r},\gamma)=(6,3,2/3)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}s\tau_{m}(s)e^{isL}T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{11m/6+\delta_{N}m}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{3/2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{11m/6+\delta_{N}m}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[{\|e^{isL}P_{k_{2}}\partial_{s}h\|}_{L^{2+}}{\big\|e^{isL}g\big\|}_{L^{6-}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}\\
&\qquad+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\Big].\end{split}

(6.46)

Using (
4.20
), together with Bernstein and the fact that the frequencies of
h
are bounded by
C2^{M_{0}}
,
and (
4.17
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}h\big\|}_{L^{2+}}{\big\|e^{isL}g\big\|}_{L^{6-}}\lesssim\rho(2^{m})\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot 2^{2\delta_{N}m}\lesssim 2^{-2m+2\delta_{N}m}\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}Z,\end{split}

which is sufficient for the desired bound, as per the usual Remark
6.3
.
Using also (
4.26
) we can easily handle the remainder term
\mathcal{D}
in (
6.46
)
by making the usual choice
D=5m
.

Estimates for the
C_{2}
terms
.
Let us now look at the
C_{2}
terms from (
6.43
); recall the definition from (
6.34
).
We can proceed similarly to the proof above.
First, with the usual notation, we write the analogue of (
6.44
)
(see also (
6.38
)) for the first term:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[g_{{\epsilon}_{1}},h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}](t)\Big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)e^{-isL}\Big[\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\\
&+\sum_{i=2,3}T_{i}^{S}[m_{0}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)+T_{1}^{S,2}[m_{2}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\\
&+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big(T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\\
&+T_{1}^{S,1}[m_{1}^{j}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\Big)\Big]\,ds\Big\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(6.47)

Focusing on the usual main contribution,
an application of the Strichartz estimate with
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r},\gamma)=(6,3,2/3)

and (
5.40b
) give us

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{5m/6}\cdot 2^{M_{0}}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|T_{1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\big\|}_{L^{3/2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim 2^{5m/6}\cdot 2^{M_{0}}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[{\big\|e^{isL}\partial_{s}g\big\|}_{L^{6-}}{\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}(\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}})\big\|}_{L^{2+}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\Big].\end{split}

(6.48)

Using (
4.21
) and (
4.13
) we can bound by an absolute constant
the sum over
k_{2}
of first term in the last expression above; using (
4.21
) and the bound

\displaystyle{\|\partial_{\xi}\partial_{s}\widetilde{g}\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})

(6.49)

we can bound the remainder term in (
6.48
) with the usual choice
D=5m
.

For the last term
C_{2,{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}}[h_{{\epsilon}_{1}},g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}]
from (
6.43
)
(recall the notation (
6.34
)) the roles of
h
and
g
are reversed
compared to the term just treated above. Starting with the analogue of (
6.47
) matters are reduced to bounding

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{m}(s)e^{isL}T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}.\end{split}

(6.50)

Using (
5.40b
) we upperbound the above term by

\displaystyle\begin{split}C2^{m}\cdot\sup_{s\approx 2^{m}}\Big[{\big\|e^{isL}P_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}h\big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}{\|\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g}\|}_{L^{1+}_{\xi}}\cdot 2^{C_{0}M_{0}}+2^{-D}\cdot\mathcal{D}\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}is\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{1}}\partial_{s}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}is\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\Big].\end{split}

Then we can use (
4.20
), and interpolate (
4.26
) with (
4.27
) to estimate
the sum over
k_{1}
of the first term above by

\displaystyle C2^{m}\cdot 2^{(C_{0}+)\delta_{N}m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\cdot 2^{(C_{0}+)\delta_{N}m}\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}\cdot Z

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(C_{0}+)\delta_{N}m}\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}\cdot Z,

where we used that
2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\leqslant Z\rho(2^{m})^{\beta/2}
for the first line, and
2^{m}\rho(2^{m})\lesssim 1
for the second. Given our choices of parameters, this bound suffices by Remark
6.3
.

Using (
4.28
) we can take care of the remainder in the usual way.
This concludes the proof of (
6.41
).

Proof of (
6.42
)
.
Moving on to the second estimate, we adopt the same notation above
and proceed similarly to the proof of (
6.32
).
For the first term on the left-hand side of (
6.42
) we can write up to irrelevant constants

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,h)=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big[T_{0}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2,3}T_{i}^{S}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)+T_{1}^{S,2}[b_{0}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{|k_{1}-k_{2}|<5,k_{2}\leqslant M_{0}}\Big[T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}}\big)

\displaystyle+T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}^{\prime}]\big(e^{{\epsilon}_{2}it\langle\xi\rangle}\varphi_{k_{2}}\widetilde{h_{{\epsilon}_{2}}},e^{{\epsilon}_{1}it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}\widetilde{g_{{\epsilon}_{1}}}\big)\Big].

We focus again on the
T_{1}^{S,1}[b_{1}]
term since the others can be treated identically,
using Bernstein’s inequality to ensure convergence of the sums.
Using (
5.40b
) with the choice of
D=5m
, we have, up to a faster decaying term,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(g,h)\Big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}{\big\|e^{itL}\partial_{t}g\big\|}_{L^{24/17}_{x}}{\big\|e^{itL}h\big\|}_{L^{6-}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot\rho(2^{m})^{1-\beta}2^{10\delta_{N}m}\varepsilon^{\beta},

having used (
4.21
) and (
4.19
).
This suffices for (
6.42
).

A similar estimate can be used to get the same bound when the roles of
g
and
h
are exchanged:
using Theorem
5.7
followed by (
4.20
) and (
4.18
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}\sum_{k_{1}}{\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\xi\mapsto x}e^{it\langle\xi\rangle}\partial_{t}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m,k_{1},k_{2}}(h,g)\Big\|}_{L^{8/7}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{C_{0}\delta_{N}m}\sup_{t\approx 2^{m}}\sum_{k_{1}}{\big\|\partial_{t}P_{k_{1}}h\big\|}_{L^{2+}_{x}}{\big\|e^{itL}g\big\|}_{L^{8/3-}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(C_{0}+2)\delta_{N}m}\cdot\rho(2^{m})\cdot\rho(2^{m})2^{m}\cdot 2^{-3m/8+}.

This suffices to bound the second term on the left-hand side
of (
6.42
) as desired, and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.5.
Continuous interactions

We now deal with terms that are quadratic in
h.

Note that these are easier than the ones treated before since
h

essentially satisfies better estimates than
g
does.

Lemma 6.10
.

For all
{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}}\in\{+,-\}
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\partial_{\xi}T^{{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}},(1)}_{m}(h,h)\big\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{\beta}2^{m}\rho^{1-\beta+\delta^{\prime}}(2^{m}),

(6.51)

for some
\delta^{\prime}>0,
and
